), i.e. we are effectively fixing the same
\Phi_{+}
and
\Psi_{-}
as in SDYM. This means that we have Dirichlet for
\Phi_{+}
and Neumann for
\Phi_{-}
, which explains why it has not been considered before. Let us stress that keeping the same degrees of freedom fixed on the boundary in YM and SDYM does not mean that the self-dual boundary condition is imposed (
\Psi_{-}\epsilon=D_{-}\Phi_{-}\neq 0
, i.e.
F_{AA}\neq 0
).

Proceeding as above, from the
A_{i}=a_{i}+zE_{i}+...
vantage point, we fix the positive helicity part of
a_{i}
(supposed to be a source for the CFT current) and the negative helicity part of
E_{i}
(supposed to be a source for the gauge field on the boundary). This might be a good interpretation of what happens in SDYM: the theory keeps
F_{AA}
as
\Psi_{AA}
(it satisfies the same equation as
F_{AA}
would) and preserves half of the YM’s
\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}
. Therefore, one should maintain the same interpretation for
\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}
in (SD)YM and for
\Psi_{AA}
one should use the same interpretation as for
F_{AA}
, the only difference is that one should apply these interpretations to the components with specific helicities.

4.3
Duality

Once the AdS and CFT ingredients have been introduced above, one can put them together. An action does not yet completely specify a theory on a manifold with a boundary. Boundary conditions need to be prescribed, which in its turn may require adding a boundary term to the action. Within AdS/CFT correspondence, given a theory in the bulk, a natural question to ask is what it is dual to for a specific boundary condition. Also, it is interesting to see how the dual CFT depends on the boundary conditions in the bulk. For example, how the CFT correlators computed with Dirichlet boundary conditions are related to those with Neumann ones. The dependence of AdS/CFT duality on boundary conditions is what we review below, which allows us to approach SDYM.

Dirichlet.

For Dirichlet boundary conditions the AdS/CFT dual CFT has a global symmetry current
J_{m}
on the boundary. The Dirichlet boundary data has the interpretation of a source and the duality implies

\displaystyle\left\langle\exp\int d^{3}x\,J^{m}(x)A_{m}(x)\right\rangle_{\text{CFT}}

\displaystyle=\int D\Phi\Big|_{\Phi^{0}_{i}\rightarrow A_{i}}\exp{S[\Phi]}\,.

(4.27)

As before,
\Phi^{0}_{i}
denotes the leading fall-off coefficient. Realistically, the bulk action
S
has to contain gravity and other fields to make the bulk theory quantum consistent once gravity is in, but we are insensitive to this incompleteness at the order we work. The semi-classical approximation is to compute the action on the saddle point, which is a solution of the variational problem with the boundary value
\Phi^{0}
of
\Phi
kept fixed to be the source
A
, hence, Dirichlet.

Neumann.

Given a CFT with a global symmetry current
J_{m}
, as before, one can promote the infinitesimal source
A_{m}
to the full-fledged background field.
21
21

21

This may require additional local terms to be added to the action, e.g.
|D\phi|^{2}
contains
J_{m}A^{m}
as well as
-\phi^{\dagger}A_{m}A^{m}\phi
to make the coupling gauge invariant, which were dubbed Seagulls.
Let us assume that there exists a new gauge-invariant action

\displaystyle S[A]

\displaystyle=S_{\text{CFT}}+\int d^{3}x\,J^{m}(x)A_{m}(x)+\text{Seagulls}\,.

(4.28)

Given a gauge field
A_{m}
one can define a conserved current
\tilde{J}_{m}=\epsilon_{mnk}\partial^{n}A^{k}
.
22
22

22

\tilde{J}=*dA
is for
U(1)
(one has to divide
*dA
by
2\pi
to be precise), otherwise one needs to dualize the Yang–Mills field strength,
\tilde{J}=*(dA+AA)
. We will ignore the nonabelian correction to the current.
It then makes sense to take the path integral over
A_{m}
and study the correlation functions of
\tilde{J}_{m}
. This is called the
S
operation
[
3
]
. In practice, one first gets correlators of
A_{m}
itself:

\displaystyle\int DA\,\left\langle\exp\int d^{3}x\,J^{m}(x)A_{m}(x)+E^{m}A_{m}(x)+...\right\rangle_{\text{CFT}}

\displaystyle=\int D\Phi\Big|_{\Phi^{1}_{i}\rightarrow E_{i}}\exp{S[\Phi]}\,.

(4.29)

Here,
\Phi^{1}_{i}
denotes the second, subleading, asymptotic, i.e. corresponds to the Neumann boundary condition. For
s=1
it is picked by
\partial_{z}\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}
, which is the electric field in the axial gauge. Note that
\partial_{i}E^{i}=0
(Gauss law for
U(1)
) and, hence,
E^{AA}=k{}^{A}{}_{B}\,\tilde{A}^{AB}
(
E=\not{k}\tilde{A}
for short) for some
\tilde{A}^{i}\equiv\tilde{A}^{AB}
. This correctly takes into account that a gauge field
A_{m}
should be contracted with a conserved current. Upon this substitution,
k
can be moved to
A_{m}
to yield the correlators of
\tilde{J}_{m}
:

\displaystyle E^{i_{1}}(k_{1})\ldots\langle A_{i_{1}}(k_{1})\ldots\rangle\hskip 10.00002pt\longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\tilde{A}^{i_{1}}(k_{1})\ldots\langle\tilde{J}_{i_{1}}(k_{1})\ldots\rangle

(4.30)

To the leading order one can expand the exponent to find that the effective (nonlocal) kinetic term for
A_{m}
comes from the two-point function of
J_{m}
:

\displaystyle\langle\exp S[A]\rangle

\displaystyle=1+\tfrac{1}{2}\int d^{3}x\,d^{3}y\,A^{m}(x)\langle J_{m}(x)J_{n}(y)\rangle A^{n}(y)+...\,.

(4.31)

The propagator
\langle A_{m}A_{n}\rangle
for
A_{m}
comes from the inverse of the two-point function, which requires some gauge-fixing, a convenient one being the Lorenz gauge
\partial_{m}A^{m}=0
. Then,
\langle J_{m}J_{n}\rangle=ak\pi_{mn}
in the momentum space and, hence,
\langle A_{m}A_{n}\rangle=a^{-1}k^{-1}\pi_{mn}
in the Lorenz gauge. The two-point function of the new current is

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{J}_{m}\tilde{J}_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=a^{-1}k\pi_{mn}\,.

(4.32)

Perhaps, the simplest of this situation is given
[
3
]
by critical QED. More generally, one can start with a CFT that has a generic two-point function

\displaystyle\langle J_{m}J_{n}\rangle=a\pi_{mn}+b\hat{\pi}_{mn}

(4.33)

(
\pi\pi=\pi
,
\pi\hat{\pi}=\hat{\pi}
,
\hat{\pi}\hat{\pi}=-\pi
). The
S
-operation gives to the leading order
[
3
]

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{J}_{m}\tilde{J}_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{a^{2}+b^{2}}k\pi_{mn}+\frac{-b}{a^{2}+b^{2}}k\hat{\pi}_{mn}\,.

(4.34)

Dirichlet vs. Neumann.

The Neumann problem is closely related to the Dirichlet one
[
2
,
1
]
. Suppose we have already computed the path integral with the fields satisfying the Dirichlet condition, i.e. the on-shell action in the first approximation:

\displaystyle e^{S_{\text{eff}}[q]}

\displaystyle=\int D\Phi\Big|_{\Phi^{0}\rightarrow q}\exp{S[\Phi]}\,.

(4.35)

Its variation gives
\delta S_{\text{eff}}[q]=\int_{\partial M}p\,\delta q
, where
p=p[q]
is a functional of the boundary data
q
(fixed by the equations of motion and regularity). In order to replace Neumann with Dirichlet one can add
-\int_{\partial M}q\pi
to the action and, then, integrate over
q
.
23
23

23

This is sometimes being considered as
\Phi
with free boundary conditions since we integrate over all boundary values in the end.
The saddle point approximation instructs us to solve
p[q]=\pi
, i.e. the saddle point imposes the Neumann condition and is equivalent to performing the Legendre transform. The latter has a clear diagrammatic interpretation in the bulk.

Diagrammatics.

There is a way to diagrammatically understand what changing boundary conditions does on the bulk and on the CFT sides
[
12
,
13
]
. While the relations can be given for any
\Delta
and any
d
, we concentrate on the
\Delta=1,2
scalars in
\text{AdS}_{4}
,
[
13
]
. For Dirichlet vs. Neumann the key relations are: (1) the boundary-to-bulk propagators for
\Delta
and
d-\Delta
conditions are related via two-point functions of
O_{\Delta}
or
O_{d-\Delta}
(the latter being inverse of each other); the difference between bulk-to-bulk propagators with
\Delta
and
d-\Delta
boundary conditions factorizes into the product of the boundary-to-bulk propagators and a two-point function. More specifically, we have (
d=3
,
K_{\Delta=2}\equiv K_{D}
and
K_{\Delta=1}\equiv K_{N}
)

\displaystyle K_{D}

\displaystyle=K_{N}\langle O_{2}O_{2}\rangle\,,

\displaystyle G_{D}-G_{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}=K_{N}K_{N}\langle O_{2}O_{2}\rangle=K_{D}K_{D}\langle O_{1}O_{1}\rangle\,.

(4.36)

These relations imply that once
\Delta=1
duality is established, the
\Delta=2
duality follows automatically or other way around. Indeed, one can represent the AdS/CFT correlators obtained with D/N boundary conditions as a particular manipulation over the AdS/CFT correlators obtained with N/D boundary conditions, which quietly performs a Legendre transform. Therefore, one does not have to recompute AdS diagrams one by one for another choice of boundary conditions.

Theta-term.

There is one more operation natural on the CFT side
[
3
]
, which was called T-operation. Given a CFT with a global symmetry current
J_{m}
one can add to the CFT action a Chern–Simons term at level
\kappa
24
24

24

To be precise,
\kappa
has to be
\kappa/(4\pi)
and what was called
T
-operation in
[
3
]
is adding the Chern–Simons term at level half, which corresponds to
\theta\rightarrow\theta+2\pi
in the bulk. Here, we are more concerned with how to reproduce such contact terms from the bulk in general, rather than with making the theory well-defined on arbitrary manifolds, hence,
\kappa
will be assumed a free parameter. Note also, that according to
[
16
,
17
]
, to get self-dual correlation functions one has to take the level into the complex plane (hence, not dividing it by
4\pi
is the least of our problems).

\displaystyle S[A]

\displaystyle=S_{\text{CFT}}+\int d^{3}x\,J^{m}(x)A_{m}(x)+{\kappa}\,\mathrm{Tr}\int AdA+\tfrac{2}{3}A^{3}\,.

(4.37)

This just shifts the coefficient of the contact term given by the parity-odd structure. From the bulk point of view, one can add the same Chern–Simons term as a boundary term, which can also be represented as the theta-term in the bulk. The action is still stationary under the Dirichlet boundary conditions and
A_{m}
is the boundary value to be fixed.

Mixed boundary conditions.

Adding a boundary term does not necessarily modify the boundary conditions. Indeed, given a bulk action that is already stationary under Dirichlet boundary conditions, e.g.
S_{\text{YM}}=\int_{\partial M}E_{i}\delta A^{i}
, and supplementing it with a boundary action
S_{b}[A]
such that
\delta S_{b}[A]=\int_{\partial M}\pi_{i}\delta A^{i}
, one can continue using the Dirichlet conditions. In this case,
S_{b}[A]
, whether it is local or not, will be just an addon to the generating functional of correlators
W_{D}[A]
obtained in the initial Dirichlet theory. If
S_{b}[A]
is local, it will add certain contact terms to the correlation functions, a good example being how a Chern–Simons term adds parity-odd contact terms to
2
- and
3
-point functions. In this cases, adding
S_{b}[A]
to the CFT action means the same for the bulk theory and can be understood as adding a boundary term.

Let us come back to the Neumann/mixed boundary conditions story where the boundary value
A_{m}
is a dynamical field on the CFT side. Adding a boundary term can modify the boundary conditions. As it was already mentioned above, adding the Chern–Simons term with the sources turned off can be interpreted as a mixed boundary condition (the second option is Dirichlet,
\delta A_{i}=0
):

\displaystyle\delta S=\delta A^{m}(i\kappa B_{m}+E_{m})

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle S[A]=S_{\text{YM}}[A]+i\kappa S_{\text{CS}}[A]\,.

(4.38)

In order to turn on the source, i.e. to fix
Q_{m}=(i\kappa B_{m}+E_{m})
on the boundary, one can add a boundary term
-\int_{\partial M}(i\kappa B_{m}+E_{m})A^{m}
, essentially a Legendre transform. Alternatively, one can just add
-\int_{\partial M}A^{m}Q_{m}^{0}
, where
Q_{m}^{0}
is considered a fixed boundary data. Boundary conditions for vector fields were extensively discussed in
[
78
]
.

For the Dirichlet boundary condition the bulk partition function is a generating functional
W[A_{i}]
of the boundary correlators
\langle J...J\rangle
. A gauge transformation of
A_{i}
will lead to contact terms that are due to Ward identities that the correlators satisfy. Therefore, one expects that the result is invariant up to contact terms. For Neumann/mixed boundary conditions, one gets correlation functions of a gauge field on the boundary, which are not gauge-invariant. For the
U(1)
case upon passing to the magnetic field, i.e. to the dual current,
\tilde{J}=*dA
, one should observe the same gauge independence up to contact terms as for Dirichlet. For a non-abelian gauge group, the dual current is
\tilde{J}=*(dA+A^{2})
and it is only covariantly conserved. Therefore, the correlation functions of
\tilde{J}
do depend on the gauge choice, as we will see.

Flow of mixed boundary conditions.

A complementary question is what happens when the mixed boundary conditions are slightly changed. The piece of the bulk-to-bulk propagator that depends on the boundary conditions is a homogeneous one, see Section
5
. For the jump between the Neumann and a generic,
\gamma\neq 0
, mixed boundary condition we have

\displaystyle G_{\gamma}-G_{\text{N}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\cos(\gamma)[\cos(\gamma)\pi+i\sin(\gamma)\hat{\pi}]\,,

(4.39a)

\displaystyle K_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\sin(\gamma)[\sin(\gamma)\pi+i\cos(\gamma)\hat{\pi}]K_{N}\,.

(4.39b)

The same arguments as in
[
12
,
13
]
can be applied to show that one can express the correlators
\langle A...A\rangle
of the gauge field for any
\gamma
in terms of those for
\gamma=\pi/2
. It looks even better for an infinitesimal change of the mixed-boundary conditions
25
25

25

The formulas in this section are tied to the standard form of YM in terms of
A_{\mu}
. As we noted, the self-dual limit requires the Chalmers–Siegel formulation. For example, the expressions here would simply diverge for
\gamma=-i\infty
. It would be interesting to adapt them in such a way as to get the results for the correlators we obtain later in the text. The easiest way is to rescale the external lines or to compute boundary limit of correlators of
\Psi_{AA}
,
\bar{F}_{A^{\prime}A^{\prime}}
, see Section
6
.

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}G

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}P\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}K

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}e^{-kz}P\,,

\displaystyle P=[-\sin(2\gamma)\pi+i\cos(2\gamma)\hat{\pi}]\,.

(4.40)

Self-dual holography.

Based on the discussion above, let us formulate a natural proposal for self-dual holography. The free boundary data in SDYM are
\Phi_{+}^{0}
and
\Psi_{-}^{0}
, i.e. Dirichlet. Via the Chalmers–Siegel action they can be traced back to YM, where they correspond to Dirichlet for
\Phi_{+}
and Neumann for
\Phi_{-}
. Indeed, we see that
\Psi_{AA}
corresponds to a conserved current on the boundary, which is exactly a Neumann data for
A_{i}
and
\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}
corresponds to a gauge field on the boundary, which is a Dirichlet data for
A_{i}
. Bearing in mind this picture, the dual CFT has a positive helicity component of the current
J_{+}
that couples to
\Phi_{+}
and a negative helicity gauge field that couples to
\Psi_{-}
. By dualizing the gauge field’s field strength, one can build
J_{-}
, which together with
J_{+}
gives a complete current. Therefore, the AdS/CFT dual of a self-dual theory is a CFT of the same type (with a current) as for all the other boundary conditions.

A less exotic model that does not discriminate helicities can be cooked up from two Yang–Mills theories
\text{YM}_{1,2}
coupled together via a bi-fundamental matter. One can impose Dirichlet/Neumann on
\text{YM}_{1}/\text{YM}_{2}
. The CFT dual of the
\text{YM}_{1}
-part would have a current
J_{m}^{1}
and the dual of the
\text{YM}_{2}
-part would have a gauge field
A_{m}
whose magnetic field
B=*(dA+AA)
would play the role of the second current
J_{m}^{2}
.

5
Propagators in various gauges

5.1
General aspects

The kinetic terms of the Yang–Mills and chiral Yang–Mills theories are the same, just written slightly differently (ignoring the boundary term). Therefore, for the purpose of deriving the propagators the theories are identical. While gauge fixing YM theory is textbook, gauge fixing SDYM is discussed below. Besides, all theories are conformally invariant and, hence, there is little need to consider the anti-de Sitter space instead of the flat space with a boundary at
z=0
.
26
26

26

Therefore, we define our theories already in the flat space with a boundary at
z=0
and proceed to impose various gauge conditions. One subtlety is that the gauge fixing conditions we are going to use are not conformally-invariant, e.g.
\partial_{\mu}A^{\mu}=0
is not mapped to
\nabla_{\mu}A^{\mu}
in anti-de Sitter space. It is easier to impose
\partial_{\mu}A^{\mu}=0
, which leads to a modified Lorenz gauge from the AdS vantage point, see
[
79
]
for extension to all spins. There exists one gauge condition that is conformally invariant
[
80
]
, but we do not use it.

Some general aspects that are shared by all theories include the following. The bulk-to-bulk propagator
27
27

27

Earlier references discussing propagators include
[
81
,
82
,
83
,
84
,
65
]
.
consists of two parts — inhomogeneous and homogeneous:

\displaystyle G(-k,z;k,z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=G^{\text{hom}}(-k,z;k,z^{\prime})+G^{\text{inh}}(-k,z;k,z^{\prime})\,.

(5.1)

The inhomogeneous part solves the equation of motion with the delta-function
\delta(z-z^{\prime})
on the right-hand side. It can easily be obtained by the Fourier transform of the usual momentum-space propagators in flat space with respect to
k^{0}

\displaystyle G(k^{i},z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int d\omega\,G(k^{i},k^{0}=\omega)\,e^{i\omega z}\,.

(5.2)

The homogeneous part is needed to impose the required boundary conditions in anti-de Sitter space. The homogeneous one subdivides into two parts, in accordance with the two regions: (a) boundary conditions at the conformal boundary of AdS, i.e.
z=0
; (b) ‘‘boundary conditions deep in the bulk’’, i.e. regularity in the deep interior of AdS (the propagator should go to zero when the points are far away from each other and from the boundary).
28
28

28

At least it should not blow up, see e.g. an example of the (most popular) axial gauge, where it does not decay at large separation.

The Feynman gauge leads to the simplest form of the inhomogeneous part. All other propagators can be related to the Feynman one via a gauge transformation

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} G^{\text{some gauge}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}&(-k,z;k,z^{\prime})=G^{\text{F}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}(-k,z;k,z^{\prime})+\\
&+\nabla_{k,z^{\prime}}^{BB^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{L}}^{AA^{\prime}}(k,z,z^{\prime})+\nabla_{-k,z}^{AA^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{R}}^{BB^{\prime}}(k,z,z^{\prime})+\nabla_{-k,z}^{AA^{\prime}}\nabla_{k,z^{\prime}}^{BB^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{c}}(k,z,z^{\prime})\,,\end{aligned}

(5.3)

which turns out to be very useful since a pure gauge term collapses the bulk integral to a boundary term. There is some redundancy among the three types of pure gauge terms above as the last one can be absorbed by either of the first two. Note that the pure gauge terms can also be split into homogeneous and inhomogeneous. The latter are Fourier transforms of the corresponding pure gauge terms in flat space.

As it was discussed in Section
4
, it is convenient to impose boundary conditions in terms of gauge (co/in)variant quantities such as
F^{AA}
,
\bar{F}^{A^{\prime}A^{\prime}}
. Since
F_{AA}=0
in SDYM, but
\Psi_{AA}
exists, we change our notation to refer to
F_{AA}
, whenever it makes sense, as
\Psi^{AA}
, and to
\bar{F}_{A^{\prime}A^{\prime}}
as
\bar{\Psi}^{A^{\prime}A^{\prime}}
(just to have a symmetric notation). To this end, given a
\langle\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}\Phi^{B,B^{\prime}}\rangle
-propagator for the Yang–Mills theory or
\langle\Psi^{AA}\Phi^{B,B^{\prime}}\rangle
propagator for Chalmers–Siegel theory or SDYM, we compute the gauge-invariant objects of type
\langle\Psi/\bar{\Psi}\,\Psi/\bar{\Psi}\rangle
. The boundary conditions are then imposed as

\displaystyle e^{i\gamma}\langle\Psi^{AA}(-k,z\rightarrow 0)\Psi^{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=-e^{-i\gamma}\langle\bar{\Psi}^{AA}(-k,z\rightarrow 0)\Psi^{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle\,,

(5.4a)

\displaystyle e^{i\gamma}\langle\Psi^{AA}(-k,z\rightarrow 0)\bar{\Psi}^{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=-e^{-i\gamma}\langle\bar{\Psi}^{AA}(-k,z\rightarrow 0)\bar{\Psi}^{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle\,.

(5.4b)

It is also worth checking that the similar boundary conditions are satisfied with respect to
(k,z^{\prime})
-leg since the most general ansatz for the homogeneous terms is not symmetric with respect to the left/right legs exchange.

When imposed this way, i.e. via gauge-invariant
\Psi/\bar{\Psi}
, the boundary conditions are insensitive to pure gauge homogeneous
\nabla\xi
-terms that differentiate between the gauges, but the terms themselves depend on the gauge. In order to fix these terms one could try to impose the boundary condition of the form

\displaystyle e^{i\gamma}\langle\Psi^{AA}(-k,z\rightarrow 0)\Phi^{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=-e^{-i\gamma}\langle\bar{\Psi}^{AA}(-k,z\rightarrow 0)\Phi^{B,B}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle\,,

(5.5)

where only the leg on which the boundary condition is imposed is represented by the gauge-invariant field strengths
F\equiv\Psi
,
\bar{F}\equiv\bar{\Psi}
. However, this condition turns out to be too strong in some gauges. For example, it does not admit solutions that decay in the deep interior of AdS in the axial gauge. In addition, it may feature some discontinuity between Dirichlet and mixed boundary conditions.

Note that the homogeneous terms are not the same in all gauges: they have to satisfy the corresponding gauge conditions and to solve the (effective) equations of motion. Nevertheless, there is a part of the homogeneous terms that is the same in all gauges. It has a
\gamma
-dependence and affects only the physical polarizations. The pure gauge homogeneous terms must not spoil two-point functions on the boundary.

Concretely, except for the axial gauge where we will also need some
z
-dependent terms without the exponent, the homogeneous terms can be parameterized as

\displaystyle G^{\text{hom}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\Big(a_{1}\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+a_{2}\Pi^{\text{o}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+a_{3}\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon^{BB^{\prime}}+a_{4}\epsilon^{AB}\epsilon^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+a_{5}\tfrac{1}{k}(k^{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon^{BB^{\prime}}+k^{BB^{\prime}}\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}})+a_{6}\tfrac{1}{k}(k^{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon^{BB^{\prime}}-k^{BB^{\prime}}\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}})\Big)\,.

This is the most general ansatz in terms of
\epsilon^{AB}
and
k^{AB}
. One can use a slightly more general basis where each two-dimensional subspace is spanned by
k_{A}
,
{{\bar{k}}}_{A}
. This way, the most general ansatz reads (the actual solutions we find can be written in the form above)

\displaystyle G^{\text{hom}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\Big(a_{1}k_{A}k_{A^{\prime}}k_{B}k_{B^{\prime}}+\text{15 more}\Big)

(5.6)

and we will use this ansatz for all gauges. Some of these terms are pure gauge, but the number depends on the additional gauge-fixing conditions that
\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}
has to obey. It is easy to manipulate such pure gauge terms, since

\displaystyle\nabla^{-k,z}_{AA^{\prime}}e^{-kz}

\displaystyle=-e^{-kz}{{\bar{k}}}_{A}k_{A^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}e^{-kz^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=e^{-kz^{\prime}}k_{B}{{\bar{k}}}_{B^{\prime}}

(5.7)

and the result modulo overall
e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}
is

\displaystyle-{{\bar{k}}}_{A}k_{A^{\prime}}T^{\text{L}}_{BB^{\prime}}+T^{\text{R}}_{AA^{\prime}}k_{B}{{\bar{k}}}_{B^{\prime}}-{{\bar{k}}}_{A}k_{A^{\prime}}k_{B}{{\bar{k}}}_{B^{\prime}}T^{\text{c}}\,,

(5.8)

where
T
s parameterize the tensorial structure of
\xi
s. We will use both
\langle...\rangle
and
G
notation for two-point functions. Lastly, in the main text we consider the Feynman and axial gauges, but some comments on the Lorenz and
R_{\xi}
gauges can be found in Appendix
B
, while the complete gauge is discussed in Appendix
I
.

5.2
(Chiral) Yang–Mills theory

The gauge fixing of the Yang–Mills theory is done in the standard way, i.e. we add
\tfrac{1}{2\xi}G^{2}
to the action, where
G
is a gauge-fixing function. We will discuss several gauges: Feynman gauge, i.e.
G=\partial_{\mu}\Phi^{\mu}
,
\xi=1
, (for the simplicity of its propagator); axial gauge, i.e.
G=n_{\mu}\Phi^{\mu}
,
\xi=0
, (for its AdS/CFT helicity-friendly basis); Lorenz/Landau gauge, i.e.
G=\partial_{\mu}\Phi^{\mu}
,
\xi=0
, (for fun); lastly, the
R_{\xi}
-gauge for completeness. Here
n_{\mu}
is taken along the radial
z
-direction, which we associate with
n_{AA^{\prime}}=\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}
.

5.2.1
Feynman gauge

The inhomogeneous part of the second-order propagator in Feynman gauge is

\displaystyle G^{\text{inh}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}(-k,z;k,z^{\prime})=\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}_{\text{F}}=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}}{2k}e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\,,

(5.9)

which comes from the Fourier transform of the flat-space result (Euclidean version)

\displaystyle\langle A_{\mu}A_{\nu}\rangle=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\eta_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}\rangle=-\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\frac{1}{p^{2}}\,,

(5.10)

see Appendix
C
for the Fourier transforms we need.

Naive Dirichlet/Neumann.

The naive Dirichlet (magnetic) and the Neumann (electric) propagators have a very simple form
29
29

29

Note, however, that we imposed the Feynman gauge in the half-space model of
\text{AdS}_{4}
, whereas the previous papers imposed it directly in
\text{AdS}_{4}
, e.g. in Poincare coordinates. Since the most popular gauge-fixing conditions, such the Feynman one, are not conformally invariant, our Feynman gauge differs from the usual one. This greatly simplifies the propagator (cf. the recent
[
65
]
)! On the other hand, the Poincare coordinate version of our Feynman gauge would give
G=\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}-2\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}n^{AA^{\prime}}
for the gauge fixing function. Since
A_{z}
is present, this gauge-fixing breaks
\text{AdS}_{4}
isometries, like an axial gauge.

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D/N}}=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}}{2k}\left(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\mp e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\right)\,,

(5.11)

where the
-
/
+
signs correspond to Dirichlet/Neumann, respectively. They are obtained by the method of images and are just the scalar propagator times
\eta_{\mu\nu}
. Note that the image matrix is taken not to act on the vector index of
A_{\mu}
. In the Feynman gauge the Lagrangian,
\tfrac{1}{2}A_{\mu}\square A^{\mu}
, is that of
4
decoupled scalars and we can choose to treat them in the same way.

Let us have a look at the Dirichlet two-point function as a boundary limit of the bulk-to-bulk propagator:

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime}\rightarrow 0)\rangle_{\text{D}}=-{\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}}z^{\prime}(1-kz+...)\,,

(5.12)

where
z^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
was taken first. The two-point function is given by amputating the
zz^{\prime}
-factor in accordance with the BDHM-prescription
[
85
]
. The leading term is
\delta^{3}(x-y)
, which is the source term. The “response” is proportional to
{\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}}k
. In the Feynman gauge
\Phi_{z}\neq 0
, which is not a problem since it is not dual to anything on the boundary (however,
\Phi_{z}
is a Lagrange multiplier for Gauss’ law, but it stops being so in the Feynman gauge and becomes a dynamical degree of freedom). A worrisome feature is that we do not get the conformally-invariant
\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA,BB}=\delta_{ij}-\tfrac{k_{i}k_{j}}{k^{2}}
-structure, but just
\delta_{ij}
. It gives the correct
\langle++\rangle
and
\langle--\rangle
(note that
\langle+-\rangle=0
always by Lorentz symmetry on the boundary) two-point functions for the physical polarizations, but contains a longitudinal component. This undesirable feature cannot be fixed by an additional homogeneous term. In accordance with AdS/CFT, the boundary current is dual to the canonical momenta on the boundary, i.e. to the electric field
E_{i}=F_{zi}=\partial_{z}A_{i}-\partial_{i}A_{z}
. Therefore, we should extract the boundary value of
\langle F_{zi}\,F_{zi}\rangle
. Alternatively, one can compute
\Psi/\bar{\Psi}
and look at

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}\langle(\Psi^{AA}-\bar{\Psi}^{AA})(\Psi^{BB}-\bar{\Psi}^{BB})\rangle\Big|_{z=z^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=k\epsilon^{AB}\epsilon^{AB}\sim k\delta^{ij}\,,

(5.13)

where we used
\Psi=B+E
,
\bar{\Psi}=B-E
.
30
30

30

Let us decompose
\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}=\phi^{AA^{\prime}}+\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}\phi
, then (anti-)self-dual parts of the field strength read:

\Psi^{AA}=B^{AA}+E^{AA}
,
\bar{\Psi}^{AA}=B^{AA}-E^{AA}
,

B^{AA}=k{}^{A}{}_{B}\,\phi^{AB}
,
E^{AA}=\partial_{z}\phi^{AA}-k^{AA}\phi
.

In Maxwell theory
B^{i}
is always transverse and
E^{i}
is so thanks to Gauss’ law and, hence,
k_{AA}\Psi^{AA}=k_{AA}\bar{\Psi}^{AA}=0
. Via the GKPW prescription
[
86
,
87
]
, the on-shell action is
\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{{\partial M}}A^{\mu}\partial_{z}A_{\mu}
and also does not have the transverse projector built in. Therefore, the naive Feynman gauge is not immediately conformally friendly, but the propagator is simple and fully
4d
Lorentz-covariant.
31
31

31

We recall, however, that, when mapped to AdS, our gauge is not Lorentz covariant.

With the Neumann boundary condition we have a gauge boson propagator on the boundary
\langle A_{i}A_{j}\rangle\sim k^{-1}\delta_{ij}
, which should correspond to the Feynman gauge on the boundary. One can also extract the magnetic field, which is the same as the current dual to
A_{i}
,
J^{i}=\epsilon^{ijk}F_{jk}=B^{i}
.

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}\langle(\Psi^{AA}+\bar{\Psi}^{AA})(\Psi^{BB}+\bar{\Psi}^{BB})\rangle\Big|_{z=z^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=-k\Pi_{\text{e}}^{AA,BB}\sim k\delta^{ij}\,.

(5.14)

Less naive Dirichlet/Neumann.

A less naive Dirichlet/Neumann propagator is

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D/N}}=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}}{2k}e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\pm e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\frac{1}{2k}\left(\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}-\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}\right)\,,

(5.15)

where
+
is for Dirichlet. It is obtained by the method of images, where the reflection also acts on
\mu
of
A_{\mu}
. The additional tensor structure corresponds to a linear combination of the projectors:
n_{\mu}n_{\nu}/n^{2}
onto the direction of
n_{\mu}
and
\Pi^{n}_{\mu\nu}
onto the
3
-dimensional subspace orthogonal to
n_{\mu}
:

\displaystyle\Pi^{n}_{\mu\nu}\equiv\eta_{\mu\nu}-n_{\mu}n_{\nu}/n^{2}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}\equiv\Pi^{n}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}+\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B}\epsilon_{AB^{\prime}})\,.

(5.16)

The propagator contains the operator of the reflection along
n_{\mu}
:

\displaystyle\eta_{\mu\nu}-2n_{\mu}n_{\nu}/n^{2}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}-\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}\,.

(5.17)

The propagator imposes the Dirichlet/Neumann condition for
A_{i}
and the opposite for
A_{z}
. The propagator ensures that Gauss’ law is satisfied. It also implies that

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}\langle(\Psi^{AA}-\bar{\Psi}^{AA})(\Psi^{BB}-\bar{\Psi}^{BB})\rangle\Big|_{z=z^{\prime}=0}=+k\Pi_{\text{e}}^{AA,BB}\,,

(5.18)

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}\langle(\Psi^{AA}+\bar{\Psi}^{AA})(\Psi^{BB}+\bar{\Psi}^{BB})\rangle\Big|_{z=z^{\prime}=0}=-k\Pi_{\text{e}}^{AA,BB}\,,

(5.19)

which is a gauge-invariant way to extract the correlators. For the gauge field itself one finds

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\partial_{z^{\prime}}\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}\Big|_{z=z^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=k\Pi_{n}^{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}\,,

(5.20)

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{N}}\Big|_{z=z^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{k}\Pi_{n}^{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}\,.

(5.21)

The latter is the (boundary) Feynman gauge propagator, i.e.
k^{-1}\delta_{ij}
. The former is
\langle\partial_{z}A_{i}\partial_{z^{\prime}}A_{j}\rangle=k\delta_{ij}
and one should not expect to find a conformally-invariant structure here since the boundary current is dual to
F_{zi}
, which receives a correction due to nonvanishing
A_{z}
. For the near-boundary behavior we have

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}\Big|_{z^{\prime}\rightarrow 0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{2k}-z^{\prime}\Pi_{n}^{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}e^{-kz}+...\,,

(5.22)

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{N}}\Big|_{z^{\prime}\rightarrow 0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{k}\Pi_{n}^{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}e^{-kz}-z^{\prime}\frac{\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{2}e^{-kz}+...\,.

(5.23)

\boldsymbol{\Psi-\Psi}
boundary conditions.

Let us use the most general ansatz with
16
terms (
5.6
) for the homogeneous part of the propagator. They satisfy
\square\bullet=0
due to
e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}
and this does not constrain the tensor structure. Out of these
16
terms we can single out
9
of type
32
32

32

To be precise
\nabla
’s should have subscripts
\pm k
and
z/z^{\prime}
!

\displaystyle T^{AA^{\prime}}_{1}\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}+T_{2}^{BB^{\prime}}\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}+c\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,,

(5.24)

where
T_{1,2}^{AA^{\prime}}
is the most general spin-tensor, i.e. it has
4
parameters. Out of the
9
structures, the last one appears also in the first two. Therefore, we have only
7
pure-gauge structures, which do not affect the
\Psi-\Psi
boundary conditions. Note, however, that the pure gauge terms cannot reproduce neither the
n_{\mu}n_{\nu}
nor
\Pi^{n}_{\mu\nu}
tensor structures. In addition, the pure gauge terms do not affect the two-point functions when
\Phi
is contracted with the physical polarizations.

As we are going to impose the
\Psi-\Psi
boundary conditions, it is important to know what the inhomogeneous part of the propagator gives for this:

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Psi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}

\displaystyle=-\Pi^{n}_{AA,BB}\delta(z-z^{\prime})\,,

(5.25a)

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\bar{\Psi}_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}

\displaystyle=+\Pi^{n}_{AA,BB}\delta(z-z^{\prime})+k\left(-\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA,BB}+\Pi^{\text{o}}_{AA,BB}\right)e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\,,

(5.25b)

\displaystyle\langle\bar{\Psi}_{AA}(-k,z)\Psi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}

\displaystyle=+\Pi^{n}_{AA,BB}\delta(z-z^{\prime})+k\left(-\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA,BB}-\Pi^{\text{o}}_{AA,BB}\right)e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\,,

(5.25c)

\displaystyle\langle\bar{\Psi}_{AA}(-k,z)\bar{\Psi}_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}

\displaystyle=-\Pi^{n}_{AA,BB}\delta(z-z^{\prime})\,,

(5.25d)

where one can use the
\Pi^{n}_{AA,BB}=\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{AB}
form of the projector, which is just
\delta_{ij}
.

The rest of the
16-7=9
independent coefficients are completely fixed by the mixed boundary conditions for
\gamma\neq 0
. The final result, dropping the pure gauge terms, is
33
33

33

For the first time the mixed boundary condition propagator was discussed in
[
88
]
in the non-covariant complete gauge
A_{3,0}=0
.

\displaystyle G^{\text{homo},\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}}{2k}\Pi^{\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}\,,

(5.26)

where the projector
\Pi^{\gamma}
has the structure of the two-point function in a parity-violating theory (to get one, one needs to multiply it by
k
). It admits two equivalent forms
34
34

34

Note that on the CFT side all allowed 2-point structures have the form of
kk{{\bar{k}}}{{\bar{k}}}
or
{{\bar{k}}}{{\bar{k}}}kk
. Having either
kkkk
or
{{\bar{k}}}{{\bar{k}}}{{\bar{k}}}{{\bar{k}}}
would lead to
\langle\pm\mp\rangle\neq 0
, i.e. to a violation of conformal symmetry.

\displaystyle\Pi^{\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{aligned} \cos(2\gamma)\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+i\sin(2\gamma)\Pi^{\text{o}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}\,,\\
\frac{1}{4k^{2}}\left(e^{2i\gamma}k_{A}k_{A^{\prime}}{{\bar{k}}}_{B}{{\bar{k}}}_{B^{\prime}}+e^{-2i\gamma}{{\bar{k}}}_{A}{{\bar{k}}}_{A^{\prime}}k_{B}k_{B^{\prime}}\right)\,.\end{aligned}\right.

(5.27)

For the record we also give

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Psi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{hom}}

\displaystyle=e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\frac{e^{-2i\gamma}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}k^{B}k^{B}}{2k}\,,

(5.28a)

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\bar{\Psi}_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{hom}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(5.28b)

\displaystyle\langle\bar{\Psi}_{AA}(-k,z)\Psi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{hom}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(5.28c)

\displaystyle\langle\bar{\Psi}_{AA}(-k,z)\bar{\Psi}_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{hom}}

\displaystyle=e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\frac{e^{+2i\gamma}k^{A}k^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{B}{{\bar{k}}}^{B}}{2k}\,,

(5.28d)

where it can be useful to note that

\displaystyle\frac{{{\bar{k}}}_{A}{{\bar{k}}}_{A}k_{B}k_{B}}{2k^{2}}

\displaystyle=(\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA,BB}-\Pi^{\text{o}}_{AA,BB})\,,

\displaystyle\frac{k_{A}k_{A}{{\bar{k}}}_{B}{{\bar{k}}}_{B}}{2k^{2}}

\displaystyle=(\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA,BB}+\Pi^{\text{o}}_{AA,BB})\,.

(5.29)

That
\langle\Psi\Psi\rangle\neq 0
tells us that the
\langle\Psi\Phi\rangle
two-point function does not satisfy the SDYM equation on the
\Phi
-leg unless
\gamma=-i\infty
.

It is worth noting that Gauss’ law is satisfied for all values of
\gamma
. For
\gamma=0
, i.e. Dirichlet/Magnetic, there is some discontinuity since the linear systems for
\gamma=0
and
\gamma\neq 0
have different rank (some of the equations are proportional to
\sin(\gamma)
). The linear structure that is released for
\gamma=0
is
k^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{A^{\prime}}{{\bar{k}}}^{B}k^{B^{\prime}}
. For this structure the magnetic field vanishes because it corresponds to
A_{i}\sim k_{i}
(however,
A_{\mu}
is not pure gauge). The structure violates Gauss’ law and we eliminate it. With this in mind the solution at
\gamma=0
coincides with the limit
\gamma\rightarrow 0
of the general solution. The homogeneous part of the
\gamma=0
propagator reads (the inhomogeneous is always the same)

\displaystyle G^{\text{homo},\gamma=0}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}}{2k}\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}\,.

(5.30)

This differs from the less naive Dirichlet propagator by
k_{i}k_{j}/k^{2}
and by
n_{\mu}n_{\nu}
terms

\displaystyle G^{\text{homo},D}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}-G^{\text{homo},\gamma=0}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4k}\left(\frac{k_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}}{k^{2}}+\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}\right)e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,,

(5.31)

which are pure gauge, of course, since the gauge-invariant part has already been fixed:

(
5.31
)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{R}}^{BB^{\prime}}+\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}\xi_{L}^{AA^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \xi_{\text{R}}^{BB^{\prime}}&=+\frac{1}{8k^{3}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}{{\bar{k}}}^{B}k^{B^{\prime}}\,,\\
\xi_{\text{L}}^{AA^{\prime}}&=-\frac{1}{8k^{3}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}k^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{A^{\prime}}\,.\end{aligned}

(5.32)

An alternative form

(
5.31
)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{R}}^{BB^{\prime}}+\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{L}}^{AA^{\prime}}+\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{c}}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \xi_{\text{R}}^{BB^{\prime}}&=-\frac{1}{4k^{2}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\epsilon^{BB^{\prime}}\,,\\
\xi_{\text{L}}^{AA^{\prime}}&=-\frac{1}{4k^{2}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}\,,\\
\xi_{\text{c}}&=+\frac{1}{4k^{3}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,,\end{aligned}

(5.33)

looks more complicated, but will prove to be useful when discussing gauge independence of physical observables.
Since the additional terms are pure gauge the gauge-invariant two-point functions stay the same. They are given by adding (
5.25
) and (
5.28
). The homogeneous term for the Neumann condition is the negative of that for the Dirichlet one:

\displaystyle G^{\text{homo},\text{N}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}-G^{\text{homo},\gamma=\pi/2}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-\eqref{diffdgamma}\,.

(5.34)

Therefore, the pure gauge term is the same up to a sign. The boundary limit for
\gamma=0
is

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}\Big|_{z=z^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=+\tfrac{1}{2k}(\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}-\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}})\,.

(5.35)

This corresponds to
A_{i}
of the form
\tfrac{1}{2k}\frac{k_{i}k_{j}}{k^{2}}
, i.e. with vanishing magnetic field. Therefore, the leading asymptotics is effectively absent, as it should. The boundary-to-bulk propagator is

\displaystyle\partial_{z^{\prime}}\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}\Big|_{z^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{e^{-kz}}{2}(\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}})\,.

(5.36)

The two-point function, which comes from the subleading term, is

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\partial_{z^{\prime}}\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}\Big|_{z=z^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{k}{2}(\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}})\,.

(5.37)

For generic
\gamma
we find for the two-point function of the gauge field and for the boundary-to-bulk propagator

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle\Big|_{z=z^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=+\tfrac{1}{2k}(\Pi^{\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}-\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}})\,,

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle\Big|_{z^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=e^{-kz}\tfrac{1}{2k}(\Pi^{\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}-\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}})\,.

In writing the expressions above we have dropped all pure gauge terms that can be added in the bulk.

As discussed in Appendix
D
, the mixed/Robin boundary condition on the initial variables is equivalent to the Dirichlet boundary condition on certain new canonical variables. It is interesting to compute the two-point function for arbitrary
\gamma
in a gauge-invariant way in terms of the new variables. The canonical variable we fix on the boundary is
Q

\displaystyle Q^{AA}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left[+\Psi^{AA}e^{i\gamma}+\bar{\Psi}^{AA}e^{-i\gamma}\right]\,,

\displaystyle P^{AA}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left[-\Psi^{AA}e^{i\gamma}+\bar{\Psi}^{AA}e^{-i\gamma}\right]\,,

(5.38)

where
P
is the canonically conjugate momentum. The matrix above belongs to
Sp(2)
. For free theory the bulk Lagrangian is
\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_{z}Q)^{2}+\text{spatial derivatives}
, whose on-shell value upon integration by parts is
\tfrac{1}{2}PQ|_{z=0}
. Therefore, the two-point function is

\displaystyle\langle P_{AA}(-k,0)P_{BB}(k,0)\rangle

\displaystyle=k\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA,BB}\,,

(5.39)

which is obvious since a canonical transformation does not change the symplectic potential
p\,dq
.

\boldsymbol{\Psi-\Phi}
boundary conditions.

We can try to impose
\Psi-\Phi
boundary conditions with respect to both legs, which is important since the ansatz is not symmetric with respect to
z-z^{\prime}
. We find exactly the same answer as above for the gauge-invariant part
G^{\text{homo},\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}
, of course. In addition, all pure gauge terms are also fixed (not by hand as above, but by the boundary conditions) except for the doubly pure-gauge term

\displaystyle a\tfrac{1}{2k^{2}}\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}_{-k,z}\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}_{k,z^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,.

(5.40)

Nicely, the pure gauge terms, which we found to account for the difference (
5.34
) between the Neumann propagator obtained via images and the
\gamma=\pi/2
propagator with the pure gauge terms dropped, are now fixed. These terms do not depend on
\gamma
and are the same as for the Neumann gauge. All other pure gauge terms are now fixed to zero.

We again note that the system degenerates slightly for
\gamma=0
and, in addition to the doubly pure-gauge term, the same two pure gauge terms

\displaystyle\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}{{\bar{k}}}^{B}k^{B^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,,

\displaystyle\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}k^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{A^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,,

(5.41)

as appeared in the discussion of the Dirichlet/Neumann conditions above, cannot be fixed. For
\gamma\neq 0
they do not depend on
\gamma
, actually, and give the right pure gauge term for the Neumann boundary condition. For
\gamma=0
they are released and can be set to minus that of the Neumann’s, which is the right structure for the Dirichlet.

To summarize, for the Dirichlet/Neumann boundary conditions our best candidate is (
5.15
), while for the mixed boundary conditions one should use the homogeneous term (
5.30
) completed by the pure gauge terms (
5.31
) and (
5.34
):

Dirichlet

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle G_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}^{\text{D}}=\eqref{lessnaiveF}_{+}=G^{\text{inh}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+G^{\text{homo},\gamma=0}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+\eqref{diffdgamma}\,,

(5.42)

Neumann

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle G_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}^{\text{N}}=\eqref{lessnaiveF}_{-}=G^{\text{inh}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+G^{\text{homo},\gamma=\pi/2}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}-\eqref{diffdgamma}\,,

(5.43)

\displaystyle\text{generic},\gamma\neq 0

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle G_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}^{\gamma}=G^{\text{inh}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+G^{\text{homo},\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}-\eqref{diffdgamma}\,,

(5.44)

where we note that (
5.31
) can be represented in a pure gauge form in more than one way.

There is a strong motivation to consider (
5.44
) for mixed boundary conditions as it is uniquely fixed by the
\Psi-\Phi
boundary conditions, which also fixes pure gauge terms. As is easy to see, (
5.31
) affects boundary conditions for the unphysical components,
\Phi_{z}
and
\Phi_{k}=k_{i}\Phi^{i}
. Indeed, for the Dirichlet conditions,
\Phi_{k}
is fixed and
\partial_{z}\Phi_{z}
is also fixed (as a consequence of the gauge fixing). The latter implies that the Dirichlet propagator obeys the Dirichlet condition for
\Phi_{i}
and Neumann one for
\Phi_{z}
. For the Neumann propagator it is the opposite. Via the BRST-transformations these conditions are directly related to ghosts and, hence, to whether or not the gauge symmetries survive in the boundary theory. Both options are available. Therefore, one can keep them in mind by defining

\displaystyle\text{generic},\gamma\neq 0

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle G_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}^{\gamma}=G^{\text{inh}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+G^{\text{homo},\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+\sigma\eqref{diffdgamma}\,,

(5.45)

where
\sigma=\pm 1
(
\sigma=1
can be dubbed Dirichlet-like and
\sigma=-1
can be dubbed Neumann-like). We will find that for
\sigma=1
the results in different gauges agree, i.e. they are (bulk)gauge independent.

5.2.2
Axial gauge

The inhomogeneous part of the AdS bulk-to-bulk propagator in the axial gauge is obtained by Fourier transforming the well-known flat-space result
[
89
]

\displaystyle G_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\left(\eta_{\mu\nu}-\frac{p_{\mu}n_{\nu}+n_{\mu}p_{\nu}}{(n\cdot p)}+p_{\mu}p_{\nu}\frac{1}{(n\cdot p)^{2}}(\xi p^{2}+n^{2})\right)\,,

(5.46)

where
n
is taken to be the radial direction
n_{AA^{\prime}}=\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}
and we set
\xi=0
to have the strict axial gauge. The Fourier transform gives

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &G^{\text{inh, A}}_{AA^{\prime};BB^{\prime}}(-k,z;k,z^{\prime})=\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}_{\text{A}}=\\
&\qquad=\frac{-1}{2k}\big(\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}+\frac{k_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}}{2k^{2}}\big)e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}-\frac{1}{4k^{2}}|z-z^{\prime}|k_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}\,.\end{aligned}

(5.47)

The first bracket is the same structure as the parity even two-point function

\displaystyle\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}+\frac{k_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}}{2k^{2}}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}^{\text{e}}\,,

(5.48)

which is just not written in the manifestly symmetric form. One might be surprised by the last linear term. It comes from the regularized
\mathrm{PV}\tfrac{1}{\omega^{2}}
,
\omega=(p\cdot n)
. Such additional poles are a general feature of noncovariant gauges and require some care
[
89
]
. The propagator satisfies

\displaystyle\square_{z}G^{\text{inh, A}}_{AA^{\prime};BB^{\prime}}-\tfrac{1}{2}k_{AA^{\prime}}k^{CC^{\prime}}G^{\text{inh, A}}_{CC^{\prime};BB^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\delta(z-z^{\prime})\Pi^{n}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}\,.

(5.49)

Here,
\square_{z}=\partial^{2}_{z}-k^{2}
. The propagator is in fact symmetric in
AA^{\prime}
and
BB^{\prime}
separately since the transversality with
n_{AA^{\prime}}=\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}
just implies the symmetry. Therefore, we can as well write
\Phi^{AA}
to stress that
A_{z}=0
.

One can also represent the difference between the propagators in the axial and Feynman gauge as a pure gauge term:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}_{\text{A}}&=\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}_{\text{F}}+\\
&+\nabla_{k,z^{\prime}}^{BB^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{L}}^{AA^{\prime}}+\nabla_{-k,z}^{AA^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{R}}^{BB^{\prime}}+\nabla_{-k,z}^{AA^{\prime}}\nabla_{k,z^{\prime}}^{BB^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{c}}\,,\end{aligned}

(5.50)

where

\displaystyle\xi_{\text{L}}^{AA^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{L,R}}\,,

\displaystyle\xi_{\text{R}}^{BB^{\prime}}=+\epsilon^{BB^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{L,R}}\,,

(5.51a)

\displaystyle\xi_{\text{L,R}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4k^{2}}\operatorname{sign}(z-z^{\prime})(-1+e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|})\,,

(5.51b)

\displaystyle\xi_{\text{c}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4k^{3}}(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}+k|z-z^{\prime}|)\,.

(5.51c)

Note that none of the terms on the r.h.s. is orthogonal to
n_{\mu}
by itself, but all together they are. This is stressed by writing
\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}
instead of
\Phi_{AA}
.

Homogeneous terms.

For the homogeneous terms we take the ansatz that contains the most general structure for
\exp{[-k(z+z^{\prime})]}
. It also has to contain the linear term
(z+z^{\prime})
in order to ensure the decay of the propagator in the deep bulk of AdS, which is now spoiled by the
|z-z^{\prime}|
-term in the inhomogeneous part. The ansatz reads

\displaystyle G^{\text{hom}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\Big(a_{1}k_{A}k_{A^{\prime}}k_{B}k_{B^{\prime}}+\text{15 more}\Big)+(z+z^{\prime})\times\Big(b_{1}k_{A}k_{A^{\prime}}k_{B}k_{B^{\prime}}+\text{15 more}\Big)+

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\Big(c_{1}k_{A}k_{A^{\prime}}k_{B}k_{B^{\prime}}+\text{15 more}\Big)\,.

Firstly, we constrain the ansatz by imposing the axial gauge and the free equations of motion

\displaystyle A_{z}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\square A_{i}-\partial_{i}\partial^{k}A_{k}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(5.52)

Very few survive as a result:

\displaystyle(c+b(z+z^{\prime}))k^{AA}k^{BB}+\sum_{\alpha,\beta=\pm}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\epsilon_{\alpha}^{AA}\epsilon_{\beta}^{BB}\,,

(5.53)

where
\epsilon^{AA}_{\alpha}=\{k^{A}k^{A},{{\bar{k}}}^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}\}
are the polarization vectors of the free physical states. Indeed, the product of the boundary-to-bulk propagators is always an admissible homogeneous solution, which is not pure gauge. The other terms are pure gauge. Secondly, we impose the boundary conditions on the complete propagator.

\boldsymbol{\Psi-\Psi}
boundary conditions plus regularity.

The linear piece of (
5.53
) disappears from the boundary conditions. Having the linear term free is crucial for being able to impose the regularity in the deep bulk. The final answer is

\displaystyle G^{\text{hom, A}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=G^{\text{hom},\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{4k^{2}}(z+z^{\prime})k_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}\,.

(5.54)

All coefficients of the exponential terms turn out to be fixed by the boundary conditions. The linear term is designed to cancel the leading behavior of
|z-z^{\prime}|
-term in the inhomogeneous part. Note, however, that at
z\rightarrow\infty
the leftover,
|z-z^{\prime}|-(z+z^{\prime})=-2z^{\prime}
is finite. In other words, the propagator does not decay to zero at large separation.

The first term,
G^{\text{hom},\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}
, is the same as in the Feynman gauge and will be the same in all gauges. In the axial gauge it can be recognized as the products of the boundary-to-bulk propagators:

\displaystyle e^{-2i\gamma}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{A^{\prime}}k^{B}k^{B^{\prime}}+e^{+2i\gamma}k^{A}k^{A^{\prime}}{{\bar{k}}}^{B}{{\bar{k}}}^{B^{\prime}}\sim e^{-2i\gamma}\Phi^{AA^{\prime}}_{-}(-k,z)\Phi_{-}^{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})+e^{+2i\gamma}\Phi^{AA^{\prime}}_{+}(-k,z)\Phi_{+}^{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\,.

We also note that there is no discontinuity for
\gamma=0
. Finally, the complete propagator reads
35
35

35

For
\gamma=0
this agrees with
[
26
]
, where the propagator was represented as

\displaystyle\langle A_{i}(z)A_{j}(z^{\prime})\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dpp\sqrt{zz^{\prime}}\frac{J_{\frac{1}{2}}(pz)J_{\frac{1}{2}}(pz^{\prime})}{k^{2}+p^{2}}\mathcal{T}_{ij}\,,

(5.55)

where
\mathcal{T}_{ij}=\eta_{ij}+\frac{k_{i}k_{j}}{p^{2}}
, i.e. it becomes the projector onto the direction transverse to
k_{i}
when
p^{2}=-k^{2}
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{A}}&=-\frac{1}{2k}\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}+G^{\text{hom},\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+\\
&-\frac{1}{4k^{2}}|z-z^{\prime}|k_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{4k^{2}}(z+z^{\prime})k_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}\,.\end{aligned}

(5.56)

Since the difference between any two gauges is pure gauge, it is convenient to represent the last piece in a pure gauge form as

\displaystyle\frac{k_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}}{4k^{2}}(z+z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4k^{2}}\Big[\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}[(z+z^{\prime})]+\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon^{BB^{\prime}}+\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}\Big]\,,

(5.57)

which is somewhat reminiscent of the pure gauge terms for the inhomogeneous part. For practical applications it is useful to set the inhomogeneous part of the Feynman propagator as the reference point

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{A}}=\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}_{\text{F}}+G^{\text{hom},\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+\\
&\quad+\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\xi^{\text{L}}_{AA^{\prime}}+\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}^{-k,z}\xi^{\text{R}}_{BB^{\prime}}+\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}^{-k,z}\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{c}}\,,\end{aligned}

(5.58)

where

\displaystyle\xi_{\text{L}}^{AA^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}\frac{\operatorname{sign}\left(z-z^{\prime}\right)\left(1-e^{-k\left|z-z^{\prime}\right|}\right)-1}{4k^{2}}\,,

(5.59a)

\displaystyle\xi_{\text{R}}^{AA^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}\frac{\operatorname{sign}\left(z-z^{\prime}\right)\left(e^{-k\left|z-z^{\prime}\right|}-1\right)-1}{4k^{2}}\,,

(5.59b)

\displaystyle\xi_{\text{c}}

\displaystyle=\frac{k\left|z-z^{\prime}\right|+e^{-k\left|z-z^{\prime}\right|}-k\left(z+z^{\prime}\right)}{4k^{3}}\,.

(5.59c)

In the diagrams the pure gauge terms are immediately reduced to the boundary terms. Therefore, we will need only

\displaystyle\xi_{\text{L}}^{AA^{\prime}}\big|_{z^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=-\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}\frac{e^{-kz}}{4k^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\xi_{\text{R}}^{BB^{\prime}}\big|_{z=0}

\displaystyle=-\epsilon^{BB^{\prime}}\frac{e^{-kz^{\prime}}}{4k^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\xi_{\text{c}}\big|_{z=z^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4k^{3}}\,.

(5.60)

Note that, for instance,
\xi_{\text{R}}
enters as
-\partial_{z}\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}
into the integral. Since
z=0
is the lower limit, we have
\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{R}}^{BB^{\prime}}|_{z=0}
, etc. In fact, all of the boundary terms above contribute with
\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon^{BB^{\prime}}
tensor structure, which is
n_{\mu}n_{\nu}
. This means that they can be dropped, but this is not always useful. Note that none of the terms on the r.h.s. of (
5.58
) is orthogonal to
n_{\mu}
by itself. Nevertheless, such representation of the propagator is useful in practice because it isolates energy poles of different type. We will see on the example of an exchange diagram that the Feynman piece has the leading pole and is easy to match with the flat-space amplitude. If instead we choose to drop the pure gauge terms at the price of symmetrizing the Feynman propagator in
AA^{\prime}
and
BB^{\prime}
(this is equivalent to making it orthogonal to
n_{AA^{\prime}}
), it will be hard to isolate the leading pole and make contact with the flat limit (the symmerization contains 4 terms, one of which produces the poles and others conspire not to have it when summed, which is difficult to see).

\boldsymbol{\Psi-\Phi}
boundary conditions.

Here the exponential homogeneous terms have the same solution, of course. However, some of the linear terms turn out to be also fixed. In particular, we have

\displaystyle G^{\text{hom}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=G^{\text{homo},\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{4k^{2}}(z+z^{\prime})k_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}\,,

(5.61)

with
-
instead of
+
. This violates the regularity in the deep bulk. On top of that there is a discontinuity between mixed boundary conditions and the Dirichlet one. Therefore, the
\Psi-\Phi
boundary conditions seem to be too strong.

Boundary-to-bulk propagator, two-point function.

Let us first take
z^{\prime}=0
to get the boundary-to-bulk propagator. For generic
\gamma\neq 0
the leading coefficient does not vanish

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,0)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2k}\Big[\Pi^{\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}-\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}\Big]e^{-kz}\,,

(5.62)

which is the boundary-to-bulk propagator for the mixed boundary conditions. For the Dirichlet condition,
\gamma=0
, we have to look at the subleading term

\displaystyle\partial_{z^{\prime}}\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,0)\rangle

\displaystyle=-\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}e^{-kz}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{k_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}}{k^{2}}\,.

(5.63)

The last term vanishes when its boundary indices are contracted with the transverse polarization vector. From the expressions above we can get the two-point functions. For generic
\gamma\neq 0
we have

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,0)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,0)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2k}\Big[\Pi^{\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}-\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}\Big]\,,

(5.64)

which is a two-point function of a gauge field on the boundary. For the Dirichlet condition we expand

\displaystyle\partial_{z^{\prime}}\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,0)\rangle

\displaystyle=-\Pi^{n}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+\mathcal{O}(z)

(5.65)

to find the
\delta^{3}(x)
-term on the boundary.
36
36

36

The scalar analog is
\exp[-kz]=1-kz+...
, where the first term is the usual
\delta^{3}(x)
on the boundary and the next term gives the two-point function.
The subleading term is the two-point function:

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\partial_{z^{\prime}}\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,0)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,0)\rangle

\displaystyle=k\,\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}\,,

(5.66)

which is the right conformally-invariant expression. We can also have a look at the gauge-invariant observables on the boundary

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}\langle(\Psi^{AA}-\bar{\Psi}^{AA})(\Psi^{BB}-\bar{\Psi}^{BB})\rangle\Big|_{z=z^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{2}\Big[\Pi^{\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}\Big]\,,

(5.67)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}\langle(\Psi^{AA}+\bar{\Psi}^{AA})(\Psi^{BB}+\bar{\Psi}^{BB})\rangle\Big|_{z=z^{\prime}=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{2}\Big[\Pi^{\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}-\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}\Big]\,.

(5.68)

5.3
Self-dual Yang–Mills theory (SDYM)

Since SDYM is not usually found in textbooks, it is instructive to discuss how to gauge fix this theory, see e.g.
[
50
]
for more details and for examples with loop diagrams.

Gauge fixing.

Let us begin with the component action (we keep the full Chalmers–Siegel action for the time being)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\int\Psi^{AA}(\partial_{AC^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{A,}{}^{C^{\prime}}\,+\Phi_{A,C^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{A,}{}^{C^{\prime}}\,-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\Psi_{AA})\,.

(5.69)

The BRST transformations are defined as usual for the Yang–Mills theory (below we use the gauge-covariant derivative
D_{AA^{\prime}}=\partial_{AA^{\prime}}\bullet+[\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}},\bullet]
)

\displaystyle\delta\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=D^{AA^{\prime}}c\,,

\displaystyle\delta\Psi^{AA}

\displaystyle=[c,\Psi^{AA}]\,,

\displaystyle\delta c

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}[c,c]\,,

\displaystyle\delta\bar{c}

\displaystyle=B\,,

\displaystyle\delta B

\displaystyle=0\,,

(5.70)

and
\Psi
is treated as a matter field in the adjoint.
Here,
c
,
\bar{c}
is the (anti)ghost and
B
is the Nakanishi-Lautrup field, all borrowed from the standard Yang–Mills theory. We can try the usual choice for the gauge fixing fermion

\displaystyle O

\displaystyle=\bar{c}\left(\tfrac{\xi}{2}B-G(\Phi)\right)\,,

\displaystyle sO

\displaystyle=B\left(\tfrac{\xi}{2}B-G(\Phi)\right)+\text{ghosts}\,,

(5.71)

where
G=\partial_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}
for Lorenz gauge and
G=n_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}
for the axial one. We uniformize them as
G=q_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi^{AA^{\prime}}
, where
q_{AA^{\prime}}
is either
\partial_{AA^{\prime}}
or
n_{AA^{\prime}}
. Now, several options are available. One can integrate out
B
, as usual, getting
\tfrac{1}{2\xi}G^{2}
, where it is tempting to take the
\xi\rightarrow 0
limit. In the limit we find
q_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}=0
and, hence, the kinetic term is invertible since we have
3(\Psi^{AB})+3(\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}})
off-shell degrees of freedom (one degree of freedom removed from
\Phi
now). Alternatively, one can set
\xi=0
from the start and notice that
B
can be combined with
\Psi^{AA}
into a “traceful”
\Psi^{A,B}

\displaystyle\Psi^{AB}\partial_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{B}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,-\epsilon^{AB}Bq_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{B}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,\,.

(5.72)

In the case
q_{AA^{\prime}}=\partial_{AA^{\prime}}
, the free SDYM action acquires the most suggestive form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\Psi^{A,B}\partial_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{B,}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,\,,

\displaystyle\Psi^{A,B}=\Psi^{AB}-\epsilon^{AB}B\,.

(5.73)

This can be called the Feynman gauge in the sense that no actual gauge condition is imposed on
\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}
. This is also justified by the fact that the gauge-fixed Chalmers–Siegel action, upon integrating out
\Psi^{A,B}
, gives the expected
\tfrac{1}{2}\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}\square\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}
kinetic term. More generally, one finds

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\Psi^{A,B}\Pi_{A,B|CC^{\prime}}\Phi^{C,C^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle\Pi_{A,B|CC^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_{AC^{\prime}}\epsilon_{CB}+\partial_{BC^{\prime}}\epsilon_{CA}+\epsilon_{AB}q_{CC^{\prime}})\,.

(5.74)

With
B
joining
\Psi^{AA}
the set of fields is balanced again,
4(\Psi^{A,B})+4(\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}})
, and the kinetic term is invertible. Since the vertices are still made of
\Psi^{AA}
, which is symmetric, the anti-symmetric part of
\Psi^{A,B}
will not contribute.
37
37

37

As a side remark, we can start with the cYM action in the first order form,
\mathcal{L}\sim F_{AB}\Psi^{AB}-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\Psi_{AB}\Psi^{AB}
, relate
\epsilon
to
\xi
to get
\mathcal{L}\sim\Psi^{A,B}D_{AC^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{B,}{}^{C^{\prime}}\,-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\Psi_{A,B}\Psi^{A,B}+\text{ghosts}
, which also requires
G=D_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}
gauge fixing term.

5.3.1
Lorenz/Feynman gauge

In the Feynman gauge we find the following propagator in flat space

\displaystyle-p_{AB^{\prime}}\langle\phi{}_{B}{}^{B^{\prime}}\,(-p)\phi_{C,C^{\prime}}(p)\rangle_{\text{2nd order}}=\langle\psi_{A,B}(-p)\phi_{C,C^{\prime}}(p)\rangle_{\text{1st order}}=-\frac{\epsilon_{BC}p_{AC^{\prime}}}{p^{2}}\,,

(5.75)

which is related in the expected way to the second order propagator of the Yang–Mills theory. Note that with indices symmetrized, i.e. with
B
integrated out and the Lorenz gauge imposed, we simply find

\displaystyle-p_{AB^{\prime}}\langle\phi{}_{A}{}^{,B^{\prime}}\,(-p)\phi_{C,C^{\prime}}(p)\rangle_{\text{2nd order}}=\langle\psi_{AA}(-p)\phi_{C,C^{\prime}}(p)\rangle_{\text{1st order}}=-\frac{\epsilon_{AC}p_{AC^{\prime}}}{p^{2}}\,.

(5.76)

Note, however, that with
K_{AA;BB^{\prime}}=p_{AB^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BA}
, the symmetrized propagator satisfies

\displaystyle G^{AA;BB^{\prime}}K_{AA;CC^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\epsilon{}_{C}{}^{B}\,\epsilon{}_{C^{\prime}}{}^{B^{\prime}}\,-p{}_{C}{}^{B^{\prime}}\,p{}^{B}{}_{C^{\prime}}\,/p^{2}\,,

\displaystyle G^{AA;CC^{\prime}}K_{BB;CC^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-\epsilon{}_{B}{}^{A}\,\epsilon{}_{B}{}^{A}\,\,,

(5.77)

i.e. with the
p
-transverse projector on the
\Phi
-leg. The effective equations of motion in the Feynman gauge are

\displaystyle\nabla{}^{A}{}_{C^{\prime}}\,\Phi^{B,C^{\prime}}-\epsilon\Psi^{A,B}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\nabla{}_{A}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,\Psi^{A,B}=0\,.

(5.78)

In the Lorenz gauge we have instead (valid for all values of
\epsilon
)

\displaystyle\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\nabla{}^{A}{}_{C^{\prime}}\,\Phi^{A,C^{\prime}}-\epsilon\Psi^{AA}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\nabla{}_{C}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,\Psi^{AC}=\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}\alpha\,,

(5.79)

for some
\alpha
(this corresponds to the fact that only the
p
-transverse part of
p{}_{C}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,\Psi^{AC}
needs to vanish, i.e. the expression has to be proportional to
p^{AA^{\prime}}
). In anti-de Sitter space, either directly or via the Fourier transform, we have (we use
\Psi^{A,B}=\nabla{}^{A}{}_{C^{\prime}}\,\Phi^{B,C^{\prime}}
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\Psi_{A,B}(-k,z)\Phi_{C,C^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}_{\text{F}}&=(\nabla_{-k,z})_{AA^{\prime}}\langle\Phi{}_{B,}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,(-k,z)\Phi_{C,C^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}_{\text{F}}=\\
&=-\frac{\epsilon_{BC}}{2k}(k_{AC^{\prime}}-\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})k\epsilon_{AC^{\prime}})e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\,.\end{aligned}

(5.80)

This is just the spin-half field propagator (
D.34
) times
\epsilon_{AB}
. The propagator in the Lorenz gauge is the symmetrized part of the above:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}_{\text{L}}=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}}{2k}(k_{AB^{\prime}}-\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})k\epsilon_{AB^{\prime}})e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\,.\end{aligned}

(5.81)

Here, the
\Phi-\Phi
propagator can be taken either in the Feynman,
R_{\xi}
or Lorenz gauge with the same result at the end (the
p_{\mu}p_{\nu}
-term will disappear). The
\Psi
-indices are always effectively symmetrized since the vertices contain only the symmetric part of
\Psi^{A,B}
anyway. As a consequence of the equations of motion for
\Phi
, the propagator satisfies

\displaystyle(\nabla_{k,z^{\prime}}){}^{B}{}_{B^{\prime}}\,\langle\Psi^{AA}(-k,z)\Phi^{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=-\delta(z-z^{\prime})\epsilon^{AB}\epsilon^{AB}\,.

(5.82)

Homogeneous terms.

Naively, taking the derivative of the Yang–Mills propagator in the Feynman gauge, as was done for the inhomogeneous terms, we find the following homogeneous term (for the
\Psi-\Psi
boundary conditions and we keep the pure gauge terms)

\displaystyle G^{AA;BB^{\prime}}_{\text{hom}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4k^{2}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\Big[-e^{-2i\gamma}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}k^{B}k^{B^{\prime}}+c{{\bar{k}}}^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}k^{B}{{\bar{k}}}^{B^{\prime}}+c^{\prime}k^{AA}k^{B}{{\bar{k}}}^{B^{\prime}}\Big]\,.

(5.83)

Note that the free terms are proportional to
k^{B}{{\bar{k}}}^{B^{\prime}}
, which is just
\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}
, i.e. they are pure gauge. The
c^{\prime}
-term breaks Gauss’ law on the
\Psi=B+E
leg (but the other leg is still
\Phi
, i.e. this is not a gauge-invariant statement). The SDYM equation on the right leg is satisfied only if
\gamma=-i\infty
, i.e. the self-dual (SD) boundary condition
\Psi^{AA}\equiv\nabla{}^{A}{}_{A^{\prime}}\,\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}=0
at
z=0
, cf. also (
5.28a
),

\displaystyle-\nabla_{k,z^{\prime}}^{BB^{\prime}}G{}^{AA^{\prime};B}{}_{B^{\prime}}\,{}_{\text{hom}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2k}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}e^{-2i\gamma}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{A^{\prime}}k^{B}k^{B}\,.

(5.84)

The SDYM equation on the left leg implies

\displaystyle-\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}G_{\text{hom}}{}^{A}{}_{A^{\prime},}{}^{BB^{\prime}}\,

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{\prime}}{4k}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}k^{A^{\prime}}k^{B}{{\bar{k}}}^{B^{\prime}}\,,

(5.85)

which is of the form
\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}\alpha^{BB^{\prime}}
(in fact, it is doubly pure-gauge). Therefore, the equations of motion for
\Psi
are satisfied in the Lorenz gauge. To summarize, the leftover pure gauge terms tolerate self-duality, but
\gamma
needs to be set to
-i\infty
, i.e. the SDYM propagator wants the SD boundary conditions, which is not surprising.

In the Feynman gauge our best candidate for the propagator is the one fixed by the
\Psi-\Phi
boundary conditions. In this case we find

\displaystyle G^{AA;BB^{\prime}}_{\text{hom}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4k^{2}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\Big[e^{-2i\gamma}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}k^{B}k^{B^{\prime}}+k^{AA}k^{B}{{\bar{k}}}^{B^{\prime}}\Big]=

(5.86)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4k^{2}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}e^{-2i\gamma}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}k^{B}k^{B^{\prime}}-\frac{k^{AA}}{4k^{2}}\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,,

(5.87)

where the coefficient of the second, pure gauge-term, is fixed (by the Neumann conditions, in fact). The SDYM equations of motion are satisfied for
\gamma=-i\infty
, as above. Since the
\Psi-\Phi
boundary conditions extrapolate nicely from the Neumann point, the final result for the SDYM propagator is its inhomogeneous part plus the Neumann correction

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{L}}=\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}_{\text{L}}-\frac{k_{AA}}{4k^{2}}\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}=\\
&=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}}{2k}(k_{AB^{\prime}}-\operatorname{sign}(z-z^{\prime})k\epsilon_{AB^{\prime}})e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}+\sigma\frac{k_{AA}}{4k^{2}}\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,.\end{aligned}

(5.88)

Here, we reinstated
\sigma=\pm 1
and the Neumann option is
\sigma=-1
.
Since the last term is pure gauge, only its boundary value

\displaystyle\sigma\frac{k^{AA}}{4k^{2}}\epsilon^{BB^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}

(5.89)

will be needed. The boundary-to-bulk propagators follow:

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,0)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{L}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{k^{A}k^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{B}{{\bar{k}}}^{B^{\prime}}}{4k^{2}}e^{-kz^{\prime}}\,,

(5.90)

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,0)\rangle_{\text{L}}

\displaystyle=+\frac{{{\bar{k}}}^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}k^{B}k^{B^{\prime}}}{4k^{2}}e^{-kz}-\frac{k^{AA}k^{BB^{\prime}}}{2k^{2}}e^{-kz}\,.

(5.91)

The very last term will cancel against the physical polarization vector we choose. When the legs on the boundary are contracted with the physical polarizations we get the same results as for the axial gauge, cf. Section
5.3.2
.

5.3.2
Axial gauge

In the strict axial gauge,
n_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}=0
, the kinetic operator
D
needs to be projected onto the space orthogonal to
n
:

\displaystyle D^{\perp}_{AA^{\prime}|BB}

\displaystyle=D_{AA^{\prime}|BB}-n^{-2}n_{AA^{\prime}}n^{CC^{\prime}}D_{CC^{\prime}|BB}=\epsilon_{BA}p_{BA^{\prime}}+n^{-2}p_{BC^{\prime}}n{}_{B}{}^{C^{\prime}}\,n_{AA^{\prime}}\,,

(5.92)

where
n^{2}=n\cdot n
and
(n\cdot p)=n_{CC^{\prime}}p^{CC^{\prime}}
and
D_{AA^{\prime}|BB}=\epsilon_{BA}p_{BA^{\prime}}
.
The propagator is the inverse of
D^{\perp}
above and reads

\displaystyle G^{BB|CC^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{p\cdot p}\left(\epsilon^{BC}p^{BC^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{(p\cdot n)}p{}^{B}{}_{M^{\prime}}\,n^{BM^{\prime}}p^{CC^{\prime}}\right)\,,

(5.93)

which satisfies

\displaystyle G^{CC|AA^{\prime}}D_{AA^{\prime}|BB}^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\epsilon{}_{B}{}^{C}\,\epsilon{}_{B}{}^{C}\,\,,

\displaystyle D_{AA^{\prime}|BB}^{\perp}G^{BB|CC^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\epsilon{}_{A}{}^{C}\,\epsilon{}_{A^{\prime}}{}^{C^{\prime}}\,-n^{-2}n_{AA^{\prime}}n^{CC^{\prime}}\,.

(5.94)

We also have the familiar relation

\displaystyle-p_{AB^{\prime}}\langle\phi{}_{A}{}^{B^{\prime}}\,\phi_{CC^{\prime}}\rangle_{\text{2nd order}}^{\text{A}}=\langle\psi_{AA}\phi_{CC^{\prime}}\rangle_{\text{1st order}}^{\text{A}}\,,

(5.95)

now for the axial gauge. The Fourier transform gives the following inhomogeneous bulk-to-bulk propagator, where it is convenient to use the Lorenz gauge as a reference point,

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{A}}^{\text{inh}}=\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{F}}^{\text{inh}}+\nabla^{k,z^{\prime}}_{BB^{\prime}}\xi_{AA}^{\text{L}}\,.

(5.96)

Here, the pure gauge term comes from (
5.51
)

\displaystyle\xi_{\text{L}}^{AA}

\displaystyle=k^{AA}\xi_{\text{L,R}}\,,

\displaystyle\xi_{\text{L,R}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4k^{2}}\operatorname{sign}(z-z^{\prime})(-1+e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|})\,.

(5.97)

There is another form, where the indices are explicitly symmetrized on the Feynman propagator

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}_{\text{A}}&=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}}{2\tilde{a}k}\big(k_{AB}-\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})k\epsilon_{AB}\big)e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}+\xi_{\text{L,R}}k_{AA}k_{BB}=\\
&=\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}_{\text{F}}+\xi_{\text{L,R}}k_{AA}k_{BB}\,.\end{aligned}

The propagator satisfies

\displaystyle-\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}_{k,z^{\prime}}\langle\Psi^{AA}(-k,z)\Phi{}^{B}{}_{B^{\prime}}\,(k,z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=-\delta(z-z^{\prime})\epsilon^{AB}\epsilon^{AB}\,,

(5.98)

\displaystyle-\nabla^{MA}_{-k,z}\langle\Psi{}^{A}{}_{M}\,(-k,z)\Phi^{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=-\delta(z-z^{\prime})\epsilon^{AB}\epsilon^{AB}\,.

(5.99)

In the second line the equations of motion for
\Psi
are projected onto the
n
-transverse direction via the symmetrization,
-\nabla^{MA}\Psi{}^{A}{}_{M}\,
. Note that the effective equations of motion read

\displaystyle\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi^{AA^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\nabla{}^{A}{}_{C^{\prime}}\,\Phi^{AC^{\prime}}-\epsilon\Psi^{AA}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\nabla{}_{C}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,\Psi^{AC}=\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}\alpha\,,

(5.100)

for some
\alpha
. The latter means that
\nabla{}_{C}{}^{A}\,\Psi^{AC}=0
.

Homogeneous terms.

As before, we try to obtain the homogeneous terms from those of Yang–Mills theory, to find

\displaystyle G_{AA;BB}^{\text{hom}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4k^{2}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}e^{-2i\gamma}{{\bar{k}}}_{A}{{\bar{k}}}_{A}k_{B}k_{B}+\frac{1}{4k^{2}}k_{AA}k_{BB}\,.

(5.101)

The linear term
(z+z^{\prime})
turned into the last constant term. The inhomogeneous part of the propagator features a similar term

\displaystyle G_{AA;BB}^{\text{inh}}

\displaystyle\ni-\frac{1}{4k^{2}}k_{AA}k_{BB}\operatorname{sign}(z-z^{\prime})\,.

(5.102)

Similarly to the Yang–Mills case it all came from, we see that there is a cancellation for
z\rightarrow\infty
between the two terms and they double for
z^{\prime}\rightarrow\infty
, leaving some constant background value:

\displaystyle G_{AA;BB}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\theta(z^{\prime}-z)\frac{1}{2k^{2}}k_{AA}k_{BB}\,,

\displaystyle z\gg z^{\prime}\text{ or }z^{\prime}\gg z\,.

(5.103)

The equation of motion for the
\Phi
-leg,

\displaystyle\nabla_{k,z^{\prime}}{}^{B}{}_{B^{\prime}}\,G^{AA;BB^{\prime}}_{\text{hom}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2k}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}e^{-2i\gamma}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}k^{B}k^{B}\,,

(5.104)

is satisfied in the self-dual limit,
\gamma\rightarrow-i\infty
. The (symmetrized) equation of motion for the
\Psi
-leg is also satisfied

\displaystyle-\nabla_{-k,z}^{MA}G_{\text{hom}}{}^{A}{}_{M;}{}^{BB}\,

\displaystyle=0\,.

(5.105)

Therefore, in the axial gauge the SDYM propagator is a smooth limit
\gamma\rightarrow-i\infty
of the YM propagator, as expected. In this limit, the complete SDYM propagator reads

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{A}}^{\text{inh}}=\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{F}}^{\text{inh}}+\nabla^{k,z^{\prime}}_{BB^{\prime}}\hat{\xi}_{AA}\,,

(5.106)

where

\displaystyle\hat{\xi}_{AA}

\displaystyle=-\frac{k_{AA}}{4k^{2}}\left(\operatorname{sign}\left(z-z^{\prime}\right)\left(1-e^{-k\left|z-z^{\prime}\right|}\right)-1\right)\,.

(5.107)

The last
(-1)
in the bracket is the contribution of the constant homogeneous term. The boundary limit of this gives

\displaystyle\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}k_{AA}e^{-kz}\frac{1}{4k^{2}}\,.

(5.108)

Note that this is not symmetric since
\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}
is not. The Feynman part gives the opposite contribution to the propagator and the complete expression is symmetric. One can make a similar comment to the one below (
5.60
), i.e. this split may be helpful to reveal the pole structure and flat limit.

5.4
Chalmers–Siegel’s amendments

For the purpose of gauge fixing and deriving the propagators the Chalmers–Siegel action can be taken in the form (
2.12
) (dropping the topological and other boundary terms, the latter, of course, must support the boundary conditions)

\displaystyle S_{\text{Ch.Si.}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\int\Psi^{AA}(\nabla_{AC^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{A,}{}^{C^{\prime}}\,+g\,\Phi_{A,C^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{A,}{}^{C^{\prime}}\,)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\Psi^{AB}\Psi_{AB}\,.

(5.109)

Since the
\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}
-field is the same as in the Yang–Mills and SDYM theories and
\Psi
is auxiliary in the former, most of the results can be borrowed. One can use the same gauge-fixing options as in YM and SDYM. The
