We are indebted to Simone Giombi for a lot of very useful discussions regarding this project.
E.S. is grateful to Dhruva K.S., Sachin Jain, Euihun Joung, Arthur Lipstein and Alexey Sharapov for useful discussions on the topics related to this paper. The work of E. S. and R. van D. was partially supported by the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme (grant agreement No 101002551).

Appendix A
Conventions

It can be useful to collect all notation at one place, which is where we are now. We use the Penrose-Rindler rules to raise and lower two-component indices, e.g.
\xi^{A}=\epsilon^{AB}\xi_{B}
and
\xi^{B}\epsilon_{BA}=\xi_{A}
. These rules are applied to all objects, e.g. to derivatives,
\partial^{A}=\epsilon^{AB}\partial_{B}
, which makes
\partial^{A}\neq\partial/\partial y^{A}
. It is also true that
\epsilon^{12}=\epsilon_{12}=1
,
\epsilon_{AC}\epsilon^{AB}\equiv\epsilon{}_{C}{}^{B}\,\equiv\delta{}_{C}{}^{B}\,=-\epsilon{}^{B}{}_{C}\,
. Two indices in which a tensor is symmetric or to be symmetrized can be denoted by the same letter, e.g.
\Psi^{AA}\equiv\Psi^{A_{1}A_{2}}
and means that
\Psi
is symmetric. The symmetrization is defined to be a projector, i.e. one has to divide by the number of permutations, e.g.
\xi^{A}\eta^{A}\equiv\tfrac{1}{2}(\xi^{A_{1}}\eta^{A_{2}}+\xi^{A_{2}}\eta^{A_{1}})
.

4d
vectors, in particular coordinates, are bi-spinors,
\mathrm{x}^{AA^{\prime}}
. We define
\mathrm{x}^{2}\equiv-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{x}_{AA^{\prime}}\mathrm{x}^{AA^{\prime}}=-\det\mathrm{x}^{AA^{\prime}}
,
\mathrm{x}_{AA^{\prime}}\mathrm{x}^{AA^{\prime}}=2\det\mathrm{x}^{AA^{\prime}}
. In Poincare coordinates or the flat half-space model of
\text{AdS}_{4}
we split
\mathrm{x}^{AA^{\prime}}=x^{AA^{\prime}}+i\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}z
, where
x^{AA^{\prime}}=x^{A^{\prime}A}
are along the boundary
z=0
. Note that
\partial_{AA^{\prime}}\mathrm{x}^{BB^{\prime}}=\epsilon{}_{A}{}^{B}\,\epsilon{}_{A^{\prime}}{}^{B^{\prime}}\,
, giving
\partial_{AA^{\prime}}\mathrm{x}^{AA^{\prime}}=4
. To get the Euclidean signature we can define

\displaystyle x^{AA^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}w&x_{3}\\
x_{3}&-\bar{w}\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{x}^{AA^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}w&x_{3}+iz\\
x_{3}-iz&-\bar{w}\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{x}^{2}

\displaystyle=w\bar{w}+x_{3}^{2}+z^{2}\,,

(A.1)

where
w=x_{1}+ix_{2}
. The Hermiticity conditions for the Euclidean signature are
(x^{AA^{\prime}})^{\dagger}|_{x_{3}\rightarrow-x_{3}}=x_{AA^{\prime}}
. In flat space the vierbein can be taken
e^{AA^{\prime}}=d\mathrm{x}^{AA^{\prime}}=dx^{AA^{\prime}}+i\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}dz
. Regardless, the choice of coordinates we define the basis of (anti)self-dual two-forms

\displaystyle H^{AB}

\displaystyle=e{}^{A}{}_{C^{\prime}}\,\wedge e^{BC^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{H}^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=e{}_{C}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,\wedge e^{CB^{\prime}}\,,

(A.2)

that satisfy

\displaystyle e^{AA^{\prime}}\wedge e^{BB^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon^{AB}\bar{H}^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}{H}^{AB}\,.

(A.3)

We also need
H^{AB}\wedge\bar{H}^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\equiv 0
and
H^{AA}\wedge H^{BB}=\tfrac{1}{3}\epsilon^{AB}\epsilon^{AB}\mathrm{vol}
, where
\mathrm{vol}\equiv H^{AB}\wedge H_{AB}=-\bar{H}_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\wedge\bar{H}^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}
is a unique (up to a multiple) four-form. In practice,
H_{AB}\wedge H^{AB}=v\,d^{4}x
. In the Cartesian coordinates we have

\displaystyle H^{AA}

\displaystyle=d^{2}x^{AA}+2idx^{AA}\wedge dz\,,

\displaystyle H_{AB}\wedge H^{AB}

\displaystyle=4i\,d^{2}x_{AA}\wedge dx^{AA}\wedge dz

(A.4)

and
d^{2}x_{AA}\wedge dx^{AA}=6dw\wedge d\bar{w}\wedge db=-12id^{3}x
. Therefore,
v=48
. To properly normalize the kinetic term in the chiral form, let us massage its free part as

\displaystyle F_{AB}F^{AB}

\displaystyle=\partial_{AC^{\prime}}A{}_{A}{}^{C^{\prime}}\,\partial{}^{A}{}_{M^{\prime}}\,A^{AM^{\prime}}=A{}_{A}{}^{C^{\prime}}\,p_{AC^{\prime}}p{}^{A}{}_{M^{\prime}}\,A^{AM^{\prime}}=-p^{2}A_{MM^{\prime}}A^{MM^{\prime}}=2p^{2}A_{\mu}A^{\mu}\,,

where we temporarily imposed the Lorenz gauge condition
p\cdot A=0
. This implies
a=1/8
for the action of the form
S_{\text{cYM}}=\frac{2a}{g^{2}}\,\mathrm{Tr}\int F_{AB}F^{AB}
.

Appendix B
Lorenz gauge

For completeness, let us also consider Lorenz gauge. The flat-space propagator is

\displaystyle G_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\left(\eta_{\mu\nu}-\frac{p_{\mu}p_{\nu}}{p^{2}}\right)\,.

(B.1)

Its direct Fourier transform leads to

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &G^{\text{inh}}_{AA^{\prime};BB^{\prime}}(-k,z;k,z^{\prime})=\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}=\\
&=\frac{e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}}{k}\Big[\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}-\tfrac{1}{4}\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}(1-k|z-z^{\prime}|)-\tfrac{z}{4}(k_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}+k_{BB^{\prime}}\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}})+\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{4k^{2}}(1+k|z-z^{\prime}|)k_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}\Big]\,.\end{aligned}

(B.2)

As before, this can be linked to the Feynman gauge via a pure gauge term

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}_{\text{L}}

\displaystyle=\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}_{\text{F}}+\nabla_{-k,z}^{AA^{\prime}}\nabla_{k,z^{\prime}}^{BB^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{c}}\,,

(B.3)

where

\displaystyle\xi_{\text{c}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4k^{3}}e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}(1+k|z-z^{\prime}|)\,.

(B.4)

Since the propagator differs by a double-gauge term, it produces the same contributions to
\Psi-\Psi
and
\Psi-\Phi
boundary behavior as the Feynman gauge. The inhomogeneous part satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \square\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,0)\rangle&=\delta(z)(\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon^{BB^{\prime}}-2\epsilon^{AB}\epsilon^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}})+\\
&-\frac{e^{-k|z|}}{2k}(k^{AA^{\prime}}-\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}k\operatorname{sign}(z))(k^{BB^{\prime}}-\epsilon^{BB^{\prime}}k\operatorname{sign}(z))\,,\end{aligned}

(B.5)

where the expression on the r.h.s. is divergence-free on both legs, i.e. it is a Fourier transform of
(\eta_{\mu\nu}-p_{\mu}p_{\nu}/p^{2})
. The
\xi=0
equations of motion (EOM) give

\displaystyle\text{EOM}_{\xi=0}\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,0)\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta(z)(\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon^{BB^{\prime}}-2\epsilon^{AB}\epsilon^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}})\,,

(B.6)

which is equivalent of
\text{EOM}_{\xi=0}G_{ij}=\delta(z-z^{\prime})\delta_{ij}
in the usual vector language.

Homogeneous terms.

We can start with the most general ansatz of type
e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}T_{AA^{\prime};BB^{\prime}}
, featuring the most general tensor structure that can be at most linear in
z
and
z^{\prime}
, bearing in mind that the inhomogeneous part also has at most linear terms in front of the exponent. However, such linear terms are inconsistent with the free equations of motion,
\square\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}=0
, and transversality
\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}=0
. Therefore,
T_{AA^{\prime};BB^{\prime}}
does not depend on
z
,
z^{\prime}
. There are
9
linearly independent structures that survive in
T_{AA^{\prime};BB^{\prime}}
. However,
5
of them can be represented in a pure gauge form with the help of

\displaystyle T^{AA^{\prime}}_{1}\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}+T_{2}^{BB^{\prime}}\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}+c\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,.

(B.7)

The ansatz above is also assumed to be constrained by the transversality, which leaves exactly the
5
free parameters. Since the inhomogeneous part decays exponentially as the points run away from each other, the homogeneous component only serves to impose the boundary conditions and there is no issue with regularity (as different from the axial gauge).

\boldsymbol{\Psi-\Psi}
boundary conditions.

As the counting here-above shows, we are left with a
4
-parameter family of homogeneous terms that survive up to the
\Psi-\Psi
boundary conditions. There is no degeneracy for
\gamma=0
(Dirichlet) and the mixed boundary conditions fix the homogeneous term to be

\displaystyle G^{\text{hom}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=G^{\text{homo},\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}+\text{pure gauge}\,.

(B.8)

\boldsymbol{\Psi-\Phi}
boundary conditions.

In the Lorenz gauge this simply fails. The linear pre-exponent terms present in the inhomogeneous solution produce contributions that cannot be canceled by the homogeneous ones except for Dirichlet boundary conditions. This clearly shows that the
\Psi-\Phi
boundary conditions can be too strong. Lastly, the doubly pure gauge term (
B.4
) should be compensated by a homogeneous one as for
R_{\xi}
-gauge.

R_{\xi}
-gauge.

R_{\xi}
can also be useful to check that the physical results do not depend on
\xi
. In the flat space the difference between the Feynman and the
R_{\xi}
propagators is a doubly pure-gauge term of the form

\displaystyle(\xi-1)\frac{p_{\mu}p_{\nu}}{p^{2}p^{2}}\,.

(B.9)

Therefore, in order to get the
R_{\xi}
-propagator we just need to add

\displaystyle\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}_{-k,z}\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}_{k,z^{\prime}}\xi_{\text{c}}\,,

\displaystyle\xi_{\text{c}}=(\xi-1)\frac{e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}}{4k^{3}}(1+k|z-z^{\prime}|)\,.

(B.10)

Since it is a doubly pure-gauge term, it leads to the same results for the homogeneous terms that are fixed by the boundary conditions. Let us also note that in the double boundary limit
\xi_{\text{c}}\neq 0
and one can expect a contribution to the four-point function coming from the integration by parts and it is a nontrivial consistency check that the final result is
\xi
-independent. The integration by parts gives
\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon^{BB^{\prime}}(4k^{3})^{-1}
. Therefore, it is clear that one needs to add the following homogeneous pure gauge term

\displaystyle\nabla^{AA^{\prime}}_{-k,z}\nabla^{BB^{\prime}}_{k,z^{\prime}}\tilde{\xi}_{\text{c}}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}_{\text{c}}=-(\xi-1)\frac{e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}}{4k^{3}}(1+k(z+z^{\prime}))\,.

(B.11)

Appendix C
Fourier transforms, propagators and integrals

Fourier transforms.

All Fourier transforms can be done with the help of the following

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pv}\frac{1}{\omega}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega}{(\omega^{2}+\mu^{2})}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pv}\frac{1}{\omega^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega^{2}-\mu^{2}}{(\omega^{2}+\mu^{2})^{2}}\,.

(C.1)

Therefore, we get the following integrals

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{\pi}\int e^{i\omega z}\frac{1}{(\omega^{2}+k^{2})}&=e^{-k|z|}\frac{1}{k}\,,\\
\frac{1}{\pi}\int e^{i\omega z}\frac{1}{(\omega^{2}+k^{2})^{2}}&=e^{-k|z|}\frac{(1+k|z|)}{2k^{3}}\,,\\
\frac{1}{\pi}\int e^{i\omega z}\frac{i\omega}{(\omega^{2}+k^{2})(\omega^{2}+\mu^{2})}&=(e^{-k|z|}-1)\frac{\operatorname{sign}(z)}{k^{2}}\,,\\
\frac{1}{\pi}\int e^{i\omega z}\frac{1}{(\omega^{2}+k^{2})}\frac{\omega^{2}-\mu^{2}}{(\omega^{2}+\mu^{2})^{2}}&=-\frac{1}{k^{3}}(e^{-k|z|}+k|z|)\,,\\
\frac{1}{\pi}\int e^{i\omega z}\frac{\omega}{(\omega^{2}+\mu^{2})}&=-\operatorname{sign}z\,,\\
\frac{1}{\pi}\int e^{i\omega z}\frac{\omega^{2}-\mu^{2}}{(\omega^{2}+\mu^{2})^{2}}&=-|z|\,.\end{aligned}

(C.2)

Feynman gauge propagators.

The Dirichlet and Neumann propagators in Feynman gauge are

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}&=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}}{2k}\Big(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}-e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\Big)-\frac{\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{2k}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,,\\
\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{N}}&=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}}{2k}\Big(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}+e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\Big)+\frac{\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{2k}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,,\end{aligned}

(C.3)

respectively. For mixed boundary conditions we use the decomposition

\displaystyle G=G_{\text{inh}}+G_{\text{pure gauge}}+G_{\gamma}

(C.4)

and the various components read

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{inh}}&=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}}{2k}e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\,,\\
\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{pure gauge}}&=-\frac{\sigma}{4k}\Big(\frac{k_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}}{k^{2}}+\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}\Big)e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,,\\
\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\gamma}&=\frac{e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}}{2k}\Pi^{\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}\,,\end{aligned}

(C.5)

see (
5.27
) for the definition of
\Pi^{\gamma}
.
The derivatives of the Dirichlet and Neumann propagators read

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}&=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}}{2k}\big(k_{AB^{\prime}}-\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})k\epsilon_{AB^{\prime}}\big)e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}+\frac{\epsilon_{AB^{\prime}}}{2k}\bar{k}_{A}k_{B}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,,\\
\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{N}}&=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}}{2k}\big(k_{AB^{\prime}}-\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})k\epsilon_{AB^{\prime}}\big)e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}-\frac{\epsilon_{AB^{\prime}}}{2k}\bar{k}_{A}k_{B}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\end{aligned}

(C.6)

and the propagators with derivatives on both legs are given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle F_{AA}(-k,z)F_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}&=-\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{AB}\delta(z-z^{\prime})+\frac{\bar{k}_{A}\bar{k}_{A}k_{B}k_{B}}{2k}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,,\\
\langle F_{AA}(-k,z)F_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{N}}&=-\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{AB}\delta(z-z^{\prime})-\frac{\bar{k}_{A}\bar{k}_{A}k_{B}k_{B}}{2k}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,.\end{aligned}

(C.7)

The single derivatives of the components of the propagator with mixed boundary conditions are

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}&=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}}{2k}\big(k_{AB^{\prime}}-\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})k\epsilon_{AB^{\prime}}\big)e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\,,\\
\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{pure gauge}}&=\sigma\frac{k_{AA}}{4k^{2}}\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,,\\
\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{homo,}\gamma}&=-\frac{e^{-2i\gamma}}{4k^{2}}\bar{k}_{A}\bar{k}_{A}k_{B}k_{B^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,.\end{aligned}

(C.8)

and their double derivatives are given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle F_{AA}(-k,z)F_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}&=-\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{AB}\delta(z-z^{\prime})\,,\\
\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Psi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{pure gauge}}&=0\,,\\
\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Psi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{homo},\gamma}&=\frac{e^{-2i\gamma}}{2k}\bar{k}_{A}\bar{k}_{A}k_{B}k_{B}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,.\end{aligned}

(C.9)

Axial gauge propagators.

In Appendix
G.1
, we define the gauge variation between axial gauge and Feynman gauge, of the difference between the Neumann and Dirichlet propagator,
\Delta\Delta\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle
, see (
G.15
) and (
G.16
). Alternatively, it can be expressed as

\displaystyle\Delta\Delta\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4k^{3}}(k_{A}\bar{k}_{A^{\prime}}k_{B}\bar{k}_{B^{\prime}}+\bar{k}_{A}k_{A^{\prime}}\bar{k}_{B}k_{B^{\prime}})e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,.

(C.10)

Its single derivatives read

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta\Delta\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle&=\frac{1}{2k^{2}}k_{AA}k_{B}\bar{k}_{B^{\prime}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,,\\
\Delta\Delta\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Psi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle&=\frac{1}{2k^{2}}\bar{k}_{A}k_{A^{\prime}}k_{BB}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\end{aligned}

(C.11)

and its double derivative is given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta\Delta\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Psi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle&=0\,.\end{aligned}

(C.12)

Integrals.

It is useful to collect the following integrals

\displaystyle\int dz\,e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3})z}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}}\,,

(C.13)

\displaystyle\int dz\,dz^{\prime}\,e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{E_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\,,

(C.14)

\displaystyle\int dz\,dz^{\prime}\,e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{E}\left(\frac{1}{E_{\text{L}}}+\frac{1}{E_{\text{R}}}\right)\,,

(C.15)

\displaystyle\int dz\,dz^{\prime}\,e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\operatorname{sign}(z-z^{\prime})e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{E}\left(\frac{1}{E_{\text{L}}}-\frac{1}{E_{\text{R}}}\right)\,,

(C.16)

\displaystyle\int dz\,dz^{\prime}\,e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\operatorname{sign}(z-z^{\prime})e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{E}\left(\frac{1}{k_{1}+k_{2}}-\frac{1}{k_{3}+k_{4}}\right)\,,

(C.17)

\displaystyle\int dzdz^{\prime}e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(k_{1}+k_{2})(k_{3}+k_{4})}\,,

(C.18)

\displaystyle\int dz\,dz^{\prime}\,e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}|z-z^{\prime}|e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{E}\left(\frac{1}{(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}}+\frac{1}{(k_{3}+k_{4})^{2}}\right)\,,

(C.19)

\displaystyle\int dz\,dz^{\prime}\,e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}(z+z^{\prime})e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{E}{(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}(k_{3}+k_{4})^{2}}\,,

(C.20)

where
E=k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}+k_{4}
,
E_{\text{L}}=k_{1}+k_{2}+k
,
E_{\text{R}}=k_{3}+k_{4}+k
,
k=|k_{1}+k_{2}|=|k_{3}+k_{4}|
.

Propagator integrals.

With the above integrals, we will evaluate the bulk integrals that appear in the four-point diagrams in Lorenz/Feynman gauge, i.e. we only consider terms in the propagator that contain an exponential. These integrals consist of bulk-to-bulk propagators and the exponentials stemming from the boundary-to-bulk propagators. Since we keep the notation minimal, it is worth mentioning once that we integrate
z
and
z^{\prime}
from
0
to
\infty
.

The integral for the
\langle\Phi\Phi\rangle
propagators for Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions read

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}&\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}\,e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=-\frac{1}{EE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\big(\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}+\frac{E}{2k}\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}\big)\,,\\
\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}&\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{N}}\,e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=\\
&=-\frac{1}{EE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\big(\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}-\frac{E}{2k}\big(\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}-2\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\big)\big)\,.\end{aligned}

(C.21)

For mixed boundary conditions we provide

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}&=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}}{EE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\big(1+\frac{E}{2k}\big)\,,\\
\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}&=\frac{1}{E_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\,.\end{aligned}

(C.22)

The latter integral is relevant for all homogeneous terms, i.e.
\langle\Phi\Phi\rangle^{\text{pure gauge}}
,
\langle\Phi\Phi\rangle^{\gamma}
, and their descendants.
We observe that the residue of the energy pole is independent of the chosen boundary conditions. Moreover, it turns out to be proportional to the flat space
\langle\Phi\Phi\rangle
propagator.

The flat space propagator for
\langle\Psi\Phi\rangle
can also be obtained in the flat limit after performing the bulk integral of the single derivative propagator. Only the inhomogeneous component of the propagator yields the flat space propagator, while the homogeneous terms provide higher energy corrections. Let us therefore demonstrate the integral of the former:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=\\
=&-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}}{2kEE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\Big(\big(E_{\text{L}}+E_{\text{R}}\big)k_{AB^{\prime}}-k\big(E_{\text{R}}-E_{\text{L}}\big)\epsilon_{AB^{\prime}}\Big)=\\
=&-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}}{2kEE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\Big((E+2k)k_{AB^{\prime}}-\big(2(k_{3}+k_{4})-E\big)k\epsilon_{AB^{\prime}}\Big)=\\
=&\frac{\epsilon_{AB}}{EE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\big(3_{A}\bar{3}_{B^{\prime}}+4_{A}\bar{4}_{B^{\prime}}-\frac{E}{2k}k_{A}\bar{k}_{B^{\prime}}\big)\,.\end{aligned}

(C.23)

The integrals of the single derivatives of the Dirichlet and Neumann propagators are
55
55

55

One can replace
3\bar{3}+4\bar{4}
with
-(1\bar{1}+2\bar{2})
, but one needs to remember that the indices are not symmetrized (there is no full momentum conservation), hence, there will be a term of order
E
as well.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}&\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}\,e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=\\
&=\frac{\epsilon_{AB}}{EE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\big(3_{A}\bar{3}_{B^{\prime}}+4_{A}\bar{4}_{B^{\prime}}\big)-\frac{1}{2kE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\Big(\epsilon_{AB}k_{A}\bar{k}_{B^{\prime}}-\epsilon_{AB^{\prime}}\bar{k}_{A}k_{B}\Big)\,,\\
\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}&\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{N}}\,e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=\\
&=\frac{\epsilon_{AB}}{EE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\big(3_{A}\bar{3}_{B^{\prime}}+4_{A}\bar{4}_{B^{\prime}}\big)-\frac{1}{2kE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\Big(\epsilon_{AB}k_{A}\bar{k}_{B^{\prime}}+\epsilon_{AB^{\prime}}\bar{k}_{A}k_{B}\Big)\,,\\
\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}&\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}\,e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=\\
&=\frac{\epsilon_{AB}}{EE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\big(3_{A}\bar{3}_{B^{\prime}}+4_{A}\bar{4}_{B^{\prime}}\big)-\frac{1}{2kE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\Big(\epsilon_{AB}k_{A}\bar{k}_{B^{\prime}}+\epsilon_{AB^{\prime}}\bar{k}_{A}k_{B}\Big)\,.\end{aligned}

(C.24)

The integrals of the double derivative of the propagators become

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}&\langle F_{AA}(-k,z)F_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{AB}}{E}\,,\\
\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}&\langle F_{AA}(-k,z)F_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}\,e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{AB}}{E}+\frac{\bar{k}_{A}\bar{k}_{A}k_{B}k_{B}}{2kE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\,,\\
\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}&\langle F_{AA}(-k,z)F_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{N}}\,e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{AB}}{E}-\frac{\bar{k}_{A}\bar{k}_{A}k_{B}k_{B}}{2kE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\,.\end{aligned}

(C.25)

The residue of the energy pole again coincides with the flat space two-point function. Lastly, for propagators with one leg on the boundary one finds

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}G(k;z,z^{\prime}=0)&=\frac{1}{E_{\text{L}}}G(k;0,0)\,,\\
\int G(k;z=0,z^{\prime})e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}&=\frac{1}{E_{\text{R}}}G(k;0,0)\,.\end{aligned}

(C.26)

Here,
G
contains
e^{-kz}
or
e^{-kz^{\prime}}
, which leads to
E_{\text{L,R}}
. On the r.h.s. this exponent is eliminated by having
z=z^{\prime}=0
.

Appendix D
Boundary terms, mixed boundary conditions

A toy model is the scalar field
\mathcal{L}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_{z}\phi)^{2}+...
that generates the following variation

\displaystyle\delta S

\displaystyle=\text{bulk eom}+\int_{\partial M}\delta\phi\,\partial_{z}\phi\,.

(D.1)

This variation is suitable for Dirichlet boundary conditions and no additional terms are needed. As it was pointed out in e.g.
[
63
,
64
,
94
]
, it is instructive to use the Hamiltonian language within the AdS/CFT correspondence, i.e. to write the bulk action in the form
S=\int p\dot{q}-H(q,p)
, where
\dot{q}=\dot{\phi}=\partial_{z}\phi
. Such an action is again designed for Dirichlet boundary conditions as it produces
p\delta q
on the boundary. Within the AdS/CFT dictionary, the coordinate
q
is fixed on the boundary to be the source
q|_{z=0}=q_{0}
and the momentum, when the action is evaluated on-shell, will be equal to the one-point function
p=\langle O\rangle_{q_{0}}
in the presence of
q_{0}
according to
\delta S=\int_{\partial M}p\,\delta q
. This also gives two closely related recipes to compute tree-level correlators: either as the on-shell action as a functional of
q_{0}
or as
p
at the boundary as a functional of
q_{0}
. Now, suppose that we would like to impose different/mixed (Robin) boundary conditions, e.g. to keep
Q=aq+bp
fixed at the boundary instead of
q
. Its variation implies
a\delta q+b\delta p=0
, with
a\neq 0
,
b=0
corresponding to the Dirichlet problem and
a=0
,
b\neq 0
to the Neumann one.

One can always find a canonically conjugate variable,
P=cq+dp
, so that changing
(q,p)
to
(Q,P)
is a canonical transformation

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}Q\\
P\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}q\\
p\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in Sp(2)\,.

(D.2)

Therefore, any other (mixed) boundary condition is a Dirichlet one for another pair of canonical variables. In the new variables, the variation is (assuming
\delta Q=0
)

\displaystyle\delta S

\displaystyle=\text{bulk eom}+\int_{\partial M}(cQ-aP)b\delta P\,.

(D.3)

It can be compensated by adding a boundary term

\displaystyle S_{\text{bndry}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial M}\tfrac{\mu}{2}Q^{2}-bcQP+\tfrac{1}{2}abP^{2}\,.

(D.4)

The coefficient of
Q^{2}
is not fixed, but it is a local counter-term on the boundary. The on-shell variation of the action is
S=\int_{\partial M}P\delta Q
, as desired. The on-shell value of the action is
S=\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\partial M}PQ
(always the same in the canonical variables and dropping the contact term).

In the main text we choose to solve the equations of motion in terms of the initial variables
p
and
q
while imposing the mixed boundary conditions. It is also instructive to see what happens in terms of the new variables. The free equations have the form
\partial_{z}\xi=M\xi
, where
\xi=(q,p)
. The new equations are
\partial_{z}\eta=M^{\prime}\eta
,
\eta=(Q,P)
with
M^{\prime}=A^{-1}MA
. For a generic
M^{\prime}
we find the following second order equation

\displaystyle\partial_{z}^{2}Q

\displaystyle=\partial_{z}Q\,\mathrm{Tr}M^{\prime}-Q\det M^{\prime}\,,

\displaystyle M^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle M=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
k^{2}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(D.5)

In our case,
M
is as above and the equations turn out to have the same form
(\partial_{z}^{2}-k^{2})Q=0
(note that
\mathrm{Tr}M^{\prime}=\mathrm{Tr}M
,
\det M^{\prime}=\det M
). The Dirichlet propagator is
e^{-kz}
and the on-shell action leads to the standard kernel
k/2
. Therefore, there is absolutely no noticeable effect in the new canonical variables if the initial equations are
(\partial_{z}^{2}-k^{2})q=0
. Interactions will make a difference, unless the theory is
Sp(2)
-invariant.

With mixed boundary conditions for the scalar field it may still be convenient to stick to the initial field variables. Therefore, we can use
a\delta q+b\delta p=0
to find

\displaystyle S_{\text{bndry}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial M}\frac{b}{2a}p^{2}+\frac{\mu^{\prime}}{2}Q^{2}\,,

(D.6)

where
\mu^{\prime}
is again a free parameter and
Q=aq+bp
(not meant as a new canonical variable). The on-shell action in the old variables is

\displaystyle S_{\text{on-shell}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial M}\frac{1}{2}pq+S_{\text{bndry}}=\int_{\partial M}\frac{1}{2}pq+\frac{b}{2a}p^{2}+\frac{\mu^{\prime}}{2}Q^{2}=\int_{\partial M}\frac{1}{2a}Qp+\frac{\mu^{\prime}}{2}Q^{2}\,,

(D.7)

which can be massaged into

\displaystyle S_{\text{on-shell}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial M}\frac{1}{2}PQ+\frac{1}{2}(\mu^{\prime}-\tfrac{c}{a})Q^{2}\,.

(D.8)

Therefore,
\mu=\mu^{\prime}-\tfrac{c}{a}
and the two on-shell actions agree, of course. An important point here is that the non-contact part of the on-shell action, which is due to
Qp/(2a)
, does not even depend on what
P
is. Indeed, generically,
P\sim p+\bullet Q
, i.e. the difference between old
p
and new
P
is a contact term. Therefore, the only thing to decide is what the boundary condition is (equivalently, what the source to be kept fixed is or what the ratio
a/b
is). There is no physical information in the choice of
P
(ratio
c/d
), unless the coefficient of the contact term has a meaning. The overall scale factors of
P
and
Q
also do not matter. The simplest choice for (
D.7
) would be to have
P=p/a
.

In practice, we also face a situation where a contact term is already present (e.g. a Chern–Simons term)

\displaystyle\delta S=\text{bulk eom}+\int_{\partial M}(p+\xi q)\delta q\,.

(D.9)

In this case we just need to subtract the last piece to reduce the problem to the one we have already solved

\displaystyle S_{\text{bndry}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial M}-\frac{\xi}{2}q^{2}+\frac{b}{2a}p^{2}+\frac{\mu^{\prime}}{2}Q^{2}\,.

(D.10)

The on-shell action does not change. In the Yang–Mills theory,
q\sim A_{i}
,
p\sim\partial_{z}A_{i}
and the contact term is the Chern–Simons term,
AdA
, on the boundary (an additional power of the momentum as compared to just
Q^{2}
does not change anything). Another special case is when we would like to approach the Neumann boundary condition as
a=0
. As it is seen from (
D.6
) this limit appears subtle. With a small rearrangement between the main and the contact term we can find another form, which is smooth for
a\rightarrow 0
,

\displaystyle S_{\text{bndry}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial M}-pq-\frac{a}{2b}q^{2}+\frac{\mu^{\prime\prime}}{2}Q^{2}\,.

(D.11)

With this choice the on-shell action is

\displaystyle S_{\text{on-shell}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial M}\frac{1}{2}pq+S_{\text{bndry}}=\int_{\partial M}-\frac{1}{2}pq-\frac{a}{2b}q^{2}+\frac{\mu^{\prime\prime}}{2}Q^{2}=\int_{\partial M}-\frac{1}{2b}qQ+\frac{\mu^{\prime\prime}}{2}Q^{2}\,.

(D.12)

Lastly, let us note that there is an ambiguity in what
P
is. The simplest option is to keep
P=p
, i.e.
c=0
,
ad=1
, which works for all cases, but the Neumann, where
Q=p
. In fact, from the AdS/CFT perspective it is the Dirichlet condition that is special (the leading falloff vanishes), while all mixed ones, Neumann’s including, can be considered together since the leading falloff never vanishes. Therefore, for non-Dirichlet boundary conditions (
\gamma\neq 0
in the main text), we can adopt
P=cq
,
d=0
,
bc=-1
. This means that we can read off the correlation function by extracting the leading fall-off of the bulk field
\phi
(modulo the
-1/b
-factor per leg).

Perturbation theory with mixed boundary conditions.

Suppose we have a mixed (Robin) boundary problem:

\displaystyle\Delta q

\displaystyle=\rho\,,

\displaystyle aq+bq^{\prime}\Big|_{\partial M}

\displaystyle=f\,,

\displaystyle aG+bG^{\prime}\Big|_{\partial M}=0\,.

(D.13)

Green’s theorem implies (
s
is a point on the boundary and
′
is the normal derivative)

\displaystyle q(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}G(x,y)\,\rho(y)+\int_{\partial M}q(s)G^{\prime}(s,x)-q^{\prime}(s)G(s,x)\,.

(D.14)

With the help of the boundary conditions one can rewrite the latter in two different ways
56
56

56

The Green’s identity is by no means a statement that one can specify two boundary data independently.

\displaystyle q(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}G(x,y)\,\rho(y)-\int_{\partial M}\frac{f(s)}{b}G(s,x)=\int_{M}G(x,y)\,\rho(y)+\int_{\partial M}\frac{f(s)}{a}G^{\prime}(s,x)\,.

(D.15)

From the AdS/CFT viewpoint,
\rho
encodes nonlinear corrections, which will be taken into account order by order, while
f
is the source and the combination of
q
and
p=q^{\prime}
to be kept fixed at the boundary. The Dirichlet condition can be approached either as
b=0
or
a=\infty
(and the Neumann as
a=0
or
b=\infty
). In the former case,
b=0
, we need to use the second expression above. In the latter case,
a=\infty
, we have to use the second and to rescale the source by
a
, as otherwise it will be suppressed. The first expression is suitable for the Neumann or generic mixed boundary conditions. The set of boundary conditions is optimistically
\mathbb{C(R)P}^{1}
, i.e. it is about the ratio
a/b
or
b/a
. At generic values of
a
,
b
one can think of
a
,
b
as ’renormalizing’ the source. When some of them becomes infinite one also has to rescale the source to approach the Dirichlet (Neumann) limits as
a=\infty
(
b=\infty
). Since we are computing correlation functions, a finite rescaling of the source does not modify the properly normalized OPE coefficients, etc. Therefore, we will drop
a
or
b
from the denominator of the formula.

Scalar field example.

As an example, let us impose mixed boundary condition on the scalar field, i.e. to keep

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=aq+bp=a\phi+b\partial_{z}\phi

(D.16)

fixed at the boundary. The Green’s function should satisfy
aG(0,z^{\prime})+b\partial_{z}G(0,z^{\prime})=0
. The solution is

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2k}\left(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}-\frac{a+bk}{a-bk}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\right)\,.

(D.17)

b=0
or
a\rightarrow\infty
(Dirichlet) and
a=0
or
b\rightarrow\infty
(Neumann) are correctly recovered

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2k}\left(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\mp e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\right)\,.

(D.18)

In particular, the boundary-to-bulk propagator is
\tfrac{b}{a-bk}e^{-kz^{\prime}}
for
b\neq 0
. It is not conformally invariant since the mixed boundary conditions for the scalar field are not, except for the pure Neumann or Dirichlet. Dirichlet is a somewhat a special case since the leading falloff changes and one has
G\sim zK
instead of
G\sim z^{0}K
for all the other boundary conditions. The boundary-to-bulk propagator is
e^{-kz^{\prime}}
. Its first term in the small
z^{\prime}
expansion is a contact term,
\delta^{3}(x-y)
, and it is the second term that determines the two-point function
\langle O(-k)O(k)\rangle\sim k
, which is of a dimension-
2
operator. In the Neumann case the boundary-to-bulk propagator is
-\frac{1}{k}e^{-kz}
and it is the
\mathcal{O}(z)
-coefficient that is a contact term. The two-point function is
\langle O(-k)O(k)\rangle\sim k^{-1}
, which is of a dimension-
1
operator. As discussed in the main text, the difference between the Dirichlet and Neumann propagators

\displaystyle G_{\text{D}}-G_{\text{N}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}=\frac{1}{k}e^{-kz}\langle O_{2}O_{2}\rangle\frac{1}{k}e^{-kz^{\prime}}

(D.19)

is the product of two
\Delta=1
boundary-to-bulk propagators times the two-point function of the Hubbard-Stratonovich field.

As one can see, the mixed boundary conditions are not conformally invariant for the scalar field. Nevertheless, one can partially replicate the Yang–Mills case by imposing different mixed boundary conditions, keeping
Q=akq+bp
fixed. Here, multiplying by
k
mimics the magnetic field,
B^{i}\sim\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{j}A_{k}
, while
p\sim\partial_{z}\phi
, corresponding to the subleading falloff, mimics the electric field
E_{i}\sim\partial_{z}A_{i}
. While this is fine for the Maxwell theory, taking
k\phi
is a nonlocal operation in position space for the scalar field, but it makes sense and it is just the shadow transform that maps an operator of dimension
\Delta
to the one of dimension
d-\Delta
. Indeed,
\phi
at the boundary has conformal dimension
1
, its shadow has dimension
2
(note that
\tilde{O}=(k^{2})^{d/2-\Delta}O
up to a numerical factor for a dimension-
\Delta
scalar operator
O
). The new (conformally-invariant) Green’s function is

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2k}\left(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}-\frac{a+b}{a-b}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\right)\,.

(D.20)

The scalar field example is, perhaps, the simplest one where a one-parameter family of boundary conditions can be imposed and it is a toy model for the case of a gauge field.
57
57

57

In principle, one can consider one-parameter family of boundary conditions for a massive scalar field with an arbitrary mass since the dimensions of the dual operators are
\Delta
and
d-\Delta
, i.e. the shadow transform still works!
We will see that this is also the propagator for the helicity states in Yang–Mills theory with mixed boundary conditions.

The Hamiltonian form of the action has certain advantages for AdS/CFT applications. Some theories are naturally in the first order form (e.g. SDYM or fermions), the rest can be put into such a form. For instance, the scalar field toy model can be recast in the first order form as
p\partial_{z}\phi-\epsilon p^{2}/2+...
, where
\epsilon p=\partial_{z}\phi
is the equation of motion for
p
(here,
\epsilon
is reminiscent of the one in the Chalmers–Siegel action). When written in the first order form, there is also a
\langle p\phi\rangle
two-point function, which has the inhomogeneous term
\operatorname{sign}(z-z^{\prime})\exp[-k|z-z^{\prime}|]
, similar to the SDYM propagator. Introducing
p
can even be helpful for the Dirichlet boundary conditions as
\partial_{z}\phi
picks the subleading falloff’s coefficient.

Toy model of SDYM and Chalmers–Siegel action.

Let us consider a toy model with the action

\displaystyle S_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\int\psi D_{+}\phi-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\psi^{2}+\text{interactions}\,,

(D.21)

where
D_{\pm}=\partial_{z}\pm|k|
. For the interactions one can take
\psi\phi^{2}/2
to mimic the main story. This model may not look Lorentz invariant (from the bulk point of view), but it is. Integrating out
\psi
we get
\psi=\epsilon^{-1}D_{+}\phi
and

\displaystyle S_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{-1}\tfrac{1}{2}\int D_{+}\phi D_{+}\phi+...=-\epsilon^{-1}\tfrac{1}{2}\int\phi D_{-}D_{+}\phi+...\,.

(D.22)

Note that
D_{-}D_{+}=\partial^{2}_{z}-k^{2}
, which is the usual kinetic term in the flat space.
58
58

58

The trick here is that
\vec{k}^{2}
come originally as
\vec{k}\cdot\vec{k}
, but one can reinterpret it as
|k|^{2}
, which leads to
D_{\pm}
.
As is shown in the main text, the theories of
s=1/2
and
s=1
can be represented via a pair of actions
S_{\epsilon}
. For
\epsilon\neq 0
we can safely get rid of
\psi
and the discussion above about the Dirichlet/Neumann/mixed boundary conditions applies.

Let us consider the
\epsilon=0
limit as a toy-model of SDYM vs. Chalmers–Siegel relation. Firstly, the equations loose one derivative and become first order, which drastically changes the options for boundary conditions: Dirichlet only. The amount of boundary data is preserved: one can fix
\phi
,
\psi
in “SDYM” vs.
\phi
,
\partial_{z}\phi=D_{+}\phi-k\phi=\epsilon\psi-k\phi
in “Chalmers–Siegel”. The variation and the free equations of motion are (the minus comes from the fact that
z=0
is the lower limit)

\displaystyle\delta S

\displaystyle=-\int_{\partial M}\psi\delta\phi\,,

\displaystyle D_{+}\phi=0\,,

\displaystyle D_{-}\psi=0\,.

(D.23)

The boundary data are
\psi_{0}
and
\phi_{0}
. However, only solutions of
D_{+}\phi=0
are admissible since we have
\phi=e^{-kz}\phi_{0}
and
\psi=e^{+kz}\psi_{0}
, the solutions for
\psi
being irregular in the bulk. Therefore, only
\phi_{0}
is free whereas
\psi=0
. This is consistent with the variation
\delta S
and no boundary terms are needed. The on-shell action vanishes, but a two-point function can be generated via a boundary term
k\phi^{2}
.
59
59

59

In this simple model,
\phi
,
\partial_{z}\phi
have dimensions
\Delta=1,2
in the original second order theory, hence,
\phi^{2}
is not conformally invariant.
The bulk-to-bulk propagator is

\displaystyle\langle\psi(z)\phi(z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}[\operatorname{sign}(z-z^{\prime})-1]=e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\theta(z^{\prime}-z)\,.

(D.24)

It obeys
D_{+}^{z^{\prime}}\langle\psi(z)\phi(z^{\prime})\rangle=\delta(z-z^{\prime})
,
D_{-}^{z}\langle\psi(z)\phi(z^{\prime})\rangle=-\delta(z-z^{\prime})
. Note that there is no regular homogeneous solution that can be added since
D_{-}\psi=0
implies
e^{+kz}
, which is consistent with us not having much choice in the boundary conditions. The boundary limits of the two-point function are

\displaystyle\langle\psi(0)\phi(z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{-kz^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle\langle\psi(z)\phi(0)\rangle

\displaystyle=0\,,

(D.25)

which is consistent with the boundary problem and “Fefferman-Graham” expansion
\phi=e^{-kz}\phi_{0}
.

Let us try to relate “SDYM” to “Chalmers–Siegel” and to the initial
\phi
-theory. One can introduce
F_{\pm}=\pm D_{\pm}\phi
. The mixed boundary condition is of type
akq+bp=Q
,
\delta Q=0
, where
a=\cos(\gamma)
,
b=i\sin(\gamma)
. The boundary condition can be rewritten as
2Q=F_{+}e^{+i\gamma}+F_{-}e^{-i\gamma}
. Here, we impose the conformally-invariant boundary condition by replacing
q
with
kq
(see the comment above about the shadow transform), which also mimics
B=\not{k}\Phi
for YM. In the initial
\phi
-theory we are free to impose mixed boundary conditions. This requires a boundary term, which we adjust to the neighborhood of the Neumann boundary condition:

\displaystyle\epsilon\delta S

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial M}\delta\phi\partial_{z}\phi\equiv\int_{\partial M}p\,\delta q

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\epsilon S_{\text{bndry}}=\int_{\partial M}-pq-\frac{a}{2b}q^{2}\,.

(D.26)

In order to make the “Chalmers–Siegel” action stationary, one has to add a different boundary term (various forms of this term are possible and we have chosen the simplest one)

\displaystyle\delta S_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial M}\psi\delta\phi\equiv\epsilon^{-1}\int_{\partial M}F_{+}\delta\phi

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle S_{\text{bndry}}=-\tfrac{1}{4}\epsilon(1-e^{2i\gamma})\int_{\partial M}\psi\tfrac{1}{k}\psi\,.

(D.27)

In “SDYM” we cannot impose mixed boundary conditions. Therefore, it is interesting to see how we can reproduce “Chalmers–Siegel” as a perturbation of “SDYM”. Let us try to interpret
\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\psi^{2}
as a deformation of “SDYM”. In the “Chalmers–Siegel” theory the
\psi-\phi
propagator remains the same, but there is also
\phi-\phi
:

\displaystyle\langle\phi(z)\phi(z^{\prime})\rangle=-\frac{\epsilon}{2k}[e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}-e^{2i\gamma}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}]\,.

(D.28)

From the “SDYM” vantage point the
\phi-\phi
propagator corresponds to the diagram with
\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\psi^{2}
-insertion. If we integrate two
\psi-\phi
propagators we get

\displaystyle\epsilon\int dz^{\prime\prime}\,\langle\psi(z^{\prime\prime})\phi(z)\rangle\,\langle\psi(z^{\prime\prime})\phi(z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\phi(z)\phi(z^{\prime})\rangle\big|_{\gamma=0}\,,

(D.29)

where we recall that
\gamma=0
is the Dirichlet boundary condition. Indeed, the Dirichlet boundary condition is the only possibility for
\phi
in “SDYM”. The additional term comes from the boundary vertex
S_{\text{bndry}}
. Therefore, if we treat all
\epsilon
-terms as a perturbation, we get the correct
\phi-\phi
propagator with mixed boundary conditions out of the Dirichlet “SDYM” propagator.

One more comment is that
S_{\text{bndry}}
(
D.27
) looks nonlocal as it contains
\frac{1}{k}
. This would definitely be true in the “SDYM” picture. However, in the “Chalmers–Siegel” picture
\psi=\epsilon^{-1}F_{+}
and the coefficient of
1/k
is a total derivative
\partial_{z}\phi\partial_{z}\phi
on the boundary. One can adjust this boundary term to make it explicitly local. The most general ansatz (of the right weight and including the ’non-local’ piece from “SDYM”) reads

\displaystyle S_{\text{bndry}}=\int_{\partial M}\tfrac{\alpha}{2}qkq+\beta qF_{+}+\tfrac{\kappa}{2}F_{+}\tfrac{1}{k}F_{+}\,.

(D.30)

We can fine tune
\kappa=0
and still satisfy the mixed boundary conditions with

\displaystyle S_{\text{bndry}}=\epsilon^{-1}\int_{\partial M}-\tfrac{(a-b)}{2b}qkq-{F_{+}q}=\epsilon^{-1}\int_{\partial M}-F_{+}q+\tfrac{1}{1-e^{2i\gamma}}qkq\,.

(D.31)

The variation of the complete action gives on-shell

\displaystyle\delta S

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{-1}\int_{\partial M}-\frac{\delta Qq}{b}=\epsilon^{-1}\int_{\partial M}\frac{i\delta Qq}{\sin(\gamma)}\,.

(D.32)

Note that the boundary terms are chosen to be well-behaved in the Neumann limit, as usual.

One can try to add the
\psi\phi^{2}/2
interaction and see what “YM”, “SDYM” and “Chalmers–Siegel” give. Since the
\langle\psi(z)\phi(0)\rangle
-propagator vanishes in SDYM the
3
-,
4
- and all tree-level correlators vanish as well (one-loop diagrams do not have to vanish). In the genuine SDYM there are two
\phi
and two
\psi
that have opposite
D_{\pm}
operators acting on them, which allows one to have one dynamical
\phi
and one dynamical
\psi
, making the model much more nontrivial, see the main text. Therefore, in “SDYM” tree-level
=0
. By contrast, the “Chalmers–Siegel” and “YM” theories do seem to have tree-level amplitudes, but very simple. Let us define
\mathcal{F}_{+}=D_{+}\phi+\phi^{2}/2
.
60
60

60

One can go further and define
\mathcal{F}_{-}=-D_{-}\phi+\phi^{2}/2
and covariant derivatives
\mathcal{D}_{+}=D_{+}+\phi
and
\mathcal{D}_{-}=-D_{-}+\phi
. Given this, we also have
\mathcal{D}_{+}\mathcal{F}_{-}-\mathcal{D}_{-}\mathcal{F}_{+}\equiv 0
.
With the Lagrangian
(\mathcal{F}_{+})^{2}/2
the cubic vertex is
\phi^{2}D_{+}\phi
, which vanishes on-shell since
D_{+}
annihilates the boundary-to-bulk propagator. One contribution to the quartic amplitude is through
\phi^{4}
, which gives the
1/E
energy pole. Another contribution comes from two
D_{+}
hitting the
\phi-\phi
bulk-to-bulk propagator. This yields the
\psi-\psi
propagator, which is
-\delta(z-z^{\prime})
, the net result being the same
1/E
(there is a cancellation between this pseudo-exchange and the quartic as in YM). Had we changed the relative coefficient between the cubic and the quartic, so that the Lagrangian is not a square, the amplitude would have been nonzero. In “Chalmers–Siegel” theory,
\psi(z)=0
as in “SDYM”. At this point, the toy model misses the key aspects of SDYM and of the Chalmers–Siegel theories where the correlators do not vanish.

Chiral symmetry breaking, a toy-model of self-duality.

As we have already seen, the spin-half theory’s action, when expressed in terms of helicity components is very close to the toy-model above. Let us work out the propagators as well. Let us consider a massless bulk Dirac fermion
\chi^{\alpha}=(\Psi^{A},\Phi^{A^{\prime}})
and its conjugate
\bar{\chi}_{\alpha}=(\bar{\Psi}_{A^{\prime}},\bar{\Phi}_{A})
, which are related by complex conjugation. Simultaneously, we consider the case of Weyl fermions where
\Psi^{A}
and
\Phi^{A^{\prime}}
are related via a reality condition. There are
4
blocks in the two-point function
\langle\chi\bar{\chi}\rangle
:

\displaystyle\langle\chi^{\alpha}(k,z)\bar{\chi}^{\beta}(-k,z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\langle\Psi^{A}\bar{\Phi}^{B}\rangle&\langle\Psi^{A}\bar{\Psi}^{B^{\prime}}\rangle\\
\langle\Phi^{A^{\prime}}\bar{\Phi}^{B}\rangle&\langle\Phi^{A^{\prime}}\bar{\Psi}^{B^{\prime}}\rangle\end{pmatrix}\,.

(D.33)

The diagonal blocks satisfy the homogeneous Weyl equation and, in fact, have to vanish unless there is a chiral symmetry breaking. The off-diagonal blocks are easy to find by Fourier transforming
\not{p}/p^{2}
, see Appendix
C
for more detail:

\displaystyle\langle\Psi^{A}\bar{\Psi}^{B^{\prime}}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2k}e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}[k^{AB^{\prime}}+k\epsilon^{AB^{\prime}}\operatorname{sign}(z-z^{\prime})]\,,

(D.34a)

\displaystyle\langle\Phi^{A^{\prime}}\bar{\Phi}^{B}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2k}e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}[k^{A^{\prime}B}-k\epsilon^{A^{\prime}B}\operatorname{sign}(z-z^{\prime})]\,.

(D.34b)

As reviewed above, the Dirichlet/Neumann boundary conditions pick (anti)-chiral components of
\chi
(or
\bar{\chi}
). Note that at
m=0
the two falloffs have the same
\Delta=3/2
. In our convention
\Gamma_{Z}
is off-diagonal and the chirality projection
1\pm i\Gamma_{z}
can be imposed as
\Psi^{A}=\pm i\Phi^{A}
. A more general one-parameter family of conformally-invariant boundary conditions is possible
[
95
]

\displaystyle e^{i\alpha}\Psi^{A}=-e^{-i\alpha}\Phi^{A}\,.

(D.35)

In order to impose this boundary condition we have to activate the diagonal blocks:

\displaystyle\langle\Psi^{A}\bar{\Phi}^{B}\rangle

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2k}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}k^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{B}e^{-2i\alpha}\,,

\displaystyle\langle\Phi^{A^{\prime}}\bar{\Psi}^{B^{\prime}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2k}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}{{\bar{k}}}^{A^{\prime}}k^{B^{\prime}}e^{+2i\alpha}\,,

(D.36)

where we have already imposed the boundary condition, which is manifested by the
\alpha
-dependence. One sees that the
AB
and
A^{\prime}B^{\prime}
two-point functions never vanish for the usual boundary conditions, i.e. Dirichlet, Neumann and even mixed ones. In principle, one can consider the “(anti) self-dual limit” where
\alpha\rightarrow\pm i\infty
.

Massless fields of spin-half admit a one-parameter family of conformally-invariant boundary conditions, which illustrates some of the features we later find for gauge fields. Generic
\alpha
can be associated with mixed boundary conditions. In
x
-space the inhomogeneous solution reads

\displaystyle\langle\Psi^{A}(x_{1},z_{1})\bar{\Psi}^{B^{\prime}}(x_{2},z_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{x_{12}^{AB^{\prime}}+i\epsilon^{AB^{\prime}}z_{12}}{[x^{2}_{12}+z^{2}_{12}+i0]^{2}}\,.

(D.37)

The homogeneous solutions are just the images of the above under
z^{\prime}\rightarrow-z^{\prime}
. The above seems to have an unambiguous limit for the two-point function. However, this is not true. The
\epsilon^{AB}
term is a delta-sequence
\delta_{z}(x_{12})
and cannot be just dropped at
z=0
. Indeed, in the Fourier space we see that the double-boundary limit

\displaystyle\langle\Psi^{A}\bar{\Psi}^{B^{\prime}}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2k}e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}[k^{AB^{\prime}}+k\epsilon^{AB^{\prime}}\operatorname{sign}(z-z^{\prime})]\rightarrow-\tfrac{1}{2k}[k^{AB^{\prime}}\pm k\epsilon^{AB^{\prime}}]

(D.38)

depends on whether we approach it from
z<z^{\prime}
or from
z>z^{\prime}
. The difference is a contact term. Another way so see there has to be a jump across
z=0
is to recall the equation
(k_{AC^{\prime}}-\partial_{z}\epsilon_{AC^{\prime}})S^{AB^{\prime}}=\delta(z)\epsilon{}_{C^{\prime}}{}^{B^{\prime}}\,
for the propagator. Integrating over a small interval around
z=0
one confirms
S
has to jump. The two-point function of a
\Delta=3/2
operator should be regularized, e.g. one can use the differential regularization to find

\displaystyle\frac{x_{AA}}{(x^{2})^{2}}\sim\partial_{AA}\square\log(x^{2}M^{2})\,.

(D.39)

The same
\operatorname{sign}(z-z^{\prime})
will be present in the gauge boson propagator in Chalmers–Siegel formulation and SDYM. Since
\Delta=3/2
the spin-half case is also extremal in the sense that the shadow dimension
d-\Delta
coincides with
\Delta
. Therefore, one can also consider
\delta^{3}(x-y)
as a legit contribution to the two-point function.

Appendix E
Color ordering

In Appendix
F.2
and Section
6.2
, we derived the color-stripped four-point flat-space amplitudes and AdS correlation functions, respectively. In this section we discuss the appropriate color ordering for the exchange diagrams and the four-point contact diagram. In principle, color ordering is a standard material, but we make sure everything works the same for SDYM since the action/vertices are different from YM.

The exchange diagrams contain two vertices, each of which is accompanied by

\displaystyle f^{abc}=\mathrm{Tr}\Big([T^{a},T^{b}]T^{c}\Big)\,,

(E.1)

where the generators
T^{a}
are in the fundamental representation. Consequently, every exchange diagram comes with a double color trace

\displaystyle f^{abe}f^{cde}=\mathrm{Tr}\Big([T^{a},T^{b}]T^{e}\Big)\mathrm{Tr}\Big([T^{c},T^{d}]T^{e}\Big)\,.

(E.2)

At tree level,
SU(N)
and
U(N)
gauge theories are equivalent due to a well-known phenomenon called photon-decoupling. For
U(N)
we have the identity

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(T^{a}A)\mathrm{Tr}(T^{a}B)=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}(AB)\,.

(E.3)

Hence, the double trace in (
E.2
) can be expressed in terms of a single trace,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\Big([T^{a},T^{b}]T^{e}\Big)\mathrm{Tr}\Big([T^{c},T^{d}]T^{e}\Big)=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}\Big([T^{a},T^{b}][T^{c},T^{d}]\Big)\,.

(E.4)

We simplify notation by replacing the Lie algebra generator
T^{a}
by
i=1,2,3,4
, i.e. the same labels with which the momenta of the corresponding fields are dressed. Expanding out the commutators, we now find

\displaystyle f^{12e}f^{34e}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\mathrm{Tr}[1234]-\mathrm{Tr}[2134]-\mathrm{Tr}[1243]+\mathrm{Tr}[2143]\Big)\,.

(E.5)

The full
s
-channel contribution to the amplitude is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{s}(1234)=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}_{s}(1234)\Big(\mathrm{Tr}[1234]-\mathrm{Tr}[2134]-\mathrm{Tr}[1243]+\mathrm{Tr}[2143]\Big)\,.

(E.6)

Here,
\mathcal{A}_{s}(1234)
is the color-stripped amplitude/correlation function, c.f. (
F.36
); we added the ordering
1234
for convenience. The
t
-channel is obtained by swapping
1\leftrightarrow 3
in the
s
-channel, while for the
u
-channel one should swap
2\leftrightarrow 3
. In other words, the channels are related by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{M}_{t}(1234)&=\mathcal{M}_{s}(3214)\,,&\mathcal{M}_{u}(1234)=\mathcal{M}_{s}(1324)\,.\end{aligned}

(E.7)

Thus, we find

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{M}_{t}(1234)&=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}_{t}(1234)\Big(\mathrm{Tr}[3214]-\mathrm{Tr}[2314]-\mathrm{Tr}[3241]+\mathrm{Tr}[2341]\Big)\,,\\
\mathcal{M}_{u}(1234)&=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}_{u}(1234)\Big(\mathrm{Tr}[1324]-\mathrm{Tr}[3124]-\mathrm{Tr}[1342]+\mathrm{Tr}[3142]\Big)\,.\end{aligned}

(E.8)

In total, this amounts to 12 different terms. Due to the cylicity condition of the trace, the full amplitude,
\mathcal{M}(1234)\equiv\mathcal{M}_{s}(1234)+\mathcal{M}_{t}(1234)+\mathcal{M}_{u}(1234)
, can be written as 6 independent terms of acyclic permutations of
Tr[1234]
. Collecting similar terms, one obtains

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{M}(1234)&=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\mathrm{Tr}[1234]\big(\mathcal{A}_{s}(1234)+\mathcal{A}_{t}(1234)\big)-\mathrm{Tr}[1342]\big(\mathcal{A}_{s}(1234)+\mathcal{A}_{u}(1234)\big)+\\
&-\mathrm{Tr}[1243]\big(\mathcal{A}_{s}(1234)+\mathcal{A}_{u}(1234)\big)+\mathrm{Tr}[1432]\big(\mathcal{A}_{s}(1234)+\mathcal{A}_{t}(1234)\big)+\\
&+\mathrm{Tr}[1423]\big(-\mathcal{A}_{t}(1234)+\mathcal{A}_{u}(1234)\big)+\mathrm{Tr}[1324]\big(-\mathcal{A}_{t}(1234)+\mathcal{A}_{u}(1234)\big)\Big)\,.\end{aligned}

(E.9)

Note that each term is obtained from the first by a non-cyclic permutation of the legs. As a result, to know the full amplitude/correlator it suffices to compute only the color-ordered partial amplitude/correlator

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\text{partial}}(1234)=\frac{1}{2}\big(\mathcal{A}_{s}(1234)+\mathcal{A}_{t}(1234)\big)\,.

(E.10)

As is discussed in Section
6.3
and Appendix
F
, the YM exchange diagram (partially) cancels against the four-point contact diagram. This necessarily means that the four-point function contains the color-structure of the
s
-,
t
- and
u
-channel. Indeed, the four-point vertex reads

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} V^{\text{quartic}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime},DD^{\prime}}&=2g^{2}\Big[f^{abe}f^{cde}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\epsilon_{C^{\prime}D^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{AD}\epsilon_{BC}+\epsilon_{AC}\epsilon_{BD})+\\
&-f^{ade}f^{bce}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}D^{\prime}}\epsilon_{B^{\prime}C^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{CD}+\epsilon_{AC}\epsilon_{BD})+f^{ace}f^{bde}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}C^{\prime}}\epsilon_{B^{\prime}D^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{CD}-\epsilon_{AD}\epsilon_{BC})\Big]\,.\end{aligned}

(E.11)

For simplicity, the four-point vertex used in Section
6.3
and Appendix
F
contains only the
s
-channel component.

Appendix F
Flat Space

All amplitudes in flat space are textbook. Nevertheless, there is a reason to recompute them first: the leading term in the anti-de Sitter amplitude in the limit
E\rightarrow 0
is exactly the flat-space amplitude. Therefore, it is useful to understand how to compute it in the optimal way and how to relate results in different gauges to each other. Of course, the amplitude is gauge invariant, but different gauges can often-times be related to each other channel by channel, which simplifies the analysis.

F.1
Three point

F.1.1
SDYM

Let us recall that the SDYM action reads

\displaystyle S=\mathrm{Tr}\int\Psi^{AA}F_{AA}\,.

(F.1)

The self-dual field strength is defined by
F_{AA}=\partial_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{A,}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,+\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{A,}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,
. The gauge field
\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}
carries positive helicity, while the field
\Psi^{AA}
carries negative helicity. Expanding the field strength in the action gives the cubic interaction

\displaystyle S_{3}=\mathrm{Tr}\int\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{A,}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,\Psi^{AA}=\epsilon^{AC}\epsilon^{BC}\epsilon^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\mathrm{Tr}\int\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}\Psi_{CC}\,.

(F.2)

From this we can read off the Feynman rule for the cubic scattering amplitude, which is displayed in Figure (
17
). We will omit color factors from here on. For more details, see Appendix
E
. The polarization vectors in Lorenz gauge are given by

\displaystyle(\epsilon^{+}_{i})^{AA^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{i}^{A}\bar{i}^{A^{\prime}}}{\langle q_{i}i\rangle}\,,

\displaystyle(\epsilon^{-}_{i})^{AA}

\displaystyle=i^{A}i^{A}\,.

(F.3)

Here,
q_{i}^{A}
are reference spinors and
i^{A}
,
\bar{i}^{A^{\prime}}
are the spinors that factorize the
i
-th (null) momentum,
p_{i}^{AA^{\prime}}=i^{A}\bar{i}^{A^{\prime}}
. We also introduce the notation
61
61

61

While in most (flat-space) literature angle brackets and square brackets are used for spinors in different representations of the Lorentz group, i.e. the barred and unbarred spinors, we adopt the convention to use only angle brackets. This allows for a smooth transition to AdS, where both barred and unbarred spinors are in the same representation, even allowing contractions of the type
\langle i\bar{j}\rangle
.

\displaystyle\langle ij\rangle\equiv-\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}i^{A}j^{A^{\prime}}=i^{A}j_{A}\,.

(F.4)

p_{1}
p_{2}
p_{3}
(+,AA^{\prime})
(+,BB^{\prime})
(-,CC)

:

V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC}=2g\epsilon_{AC}\epsilon_{BC}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}

Figure 17:
SDYM Lorenz gauge Feynman rules in flat space.

The SDYM color-stripped amplitude is depicted in Figure
18
and reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{3}=V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC}(\epsilon_{1}^{+})^{AA^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{2}^{+})^{BB^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{3}^{-})^{CC}=-2g\frac{\langle q_{1}3\rangle\langle q_{2}3\rangle}{\langle q_{1}1\rangle\langle q_{2}2\rangle}\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle\,.

(F.5)

p_{1}
p_{2}
p_{3}
(+)
(+)
(-)

:

\mathcal{A}_{3}=V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC}(\epsilon_{1}^{+})^{AA^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{2}^{+})^{BB^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{3}^{-})^{CC}

Figure 18:
SDYM three-point function.

The amplitude is gauge-invariant and should not depend on the reference spinors. To show that their dependence disappears, we use momentum conservation

\displaystyle 1^{A}\bar{1}^{A^{\prime}}+2^{A}\bar{2}^{A^{\prime}}+3^{A}\bar{3}^{A^{\prime}}=0

(F.6)

and contract this with
q_{1A}\bar{2}_{A^{\prime}}
and
q_{2A}\bar{1}_{A^{\prime}}
to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle q_{1}3\rangle&=\frac{\langle q_{1}1\rangle\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle}{\langle\bar{2}\bar{3}\rangle}\,,&\langle q_{2}3\rangle&=-\frac{\langle q_{2}2\rangle\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{3}\rangle}\,,\end{aligned}

(F.7)

respectively. The amplitude can now be written in the well-known form

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{3}=2g\frac{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle^{3}}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{3}\rangle\langle\bar{2}\bar{3}\rangle}\,.

(F.8)

F.1.2
YM

SDYM is a closed subsector of the full YM theory. Effectively, this means that SDYM has only access to scattering amplitudes with a specific helicity configuration, i.e. all but one
+
for tree-level diagrams. While the amplitudes for these diagrams coincide for SDYM and YM, the formulations of both theories are vastly different. For example, the YM action contains a second-order differential operator, as opposed to the first-order differential operator in SDYM. Moreover, the Yang–Mills three-point vertex contains a derivative, while SDYM’s does not. Here we will provide techniques that show how the amplitudes in SDYM and YM are related and we prove their equivalence in flat space.

Flat space.

To facilitate the comparison between YM and SDYM, we derive the Feynman rules for YM in a way that resembles the SDYM Feynman rules. To this end, we recall

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} F_{AA}&=\partial_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{A,}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,+\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{A,}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,\,,&\bar{F}_{A^{\prime}A^{\prime}}&=\partial_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi{}^{A}{}_{,A^{\prime}}\,+\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}\Phi{}^{A}{}_{,A^{\prime}}\,\end{aligned}

(F.9)

and the YM action

\displaystyle S_{\text{YM}}=\frac{1}{4g^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}\int\big(F^{AA}F_{AA}+\bar{F}^{A^{\prime}A^{\prime}}\bar{F}_{A^{\prime}A^{\prime}}\big)\,,

(F.10)

i.e. we set
a=\frac{1}{4}
in (
2.1
). The topological term

\displaystyle S_{\text{top}}=\frac{1}{4g^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}\int\big(F^{AA}F_{AA}-\bar{F}^{A^{\prime}A^{\prime}}\bar{F}_{A^{\prime}A^{\prime}}\big)

(F.11)

can be added to the action to obtain

\displaystyle S_{\text{cYM}}=\frac{1}{2g^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}\int F^{AA}F_{AA}\,.

(F.12)

For completeness, let us decompose the cubic YM action into the self-dual and anti-self-dual parts,
S_{3}=S_{3}^{\text{SD}}+S_{3}^{\text{ASD}}
, coming from the
F^{AA}F_{AA}
– and
\bar{F}^{A^{\prime}A^{\prime}}\bar{F}_{A^{\prime}A^{\prime}}
– term, respectively. Using (
F.9
), one finds these actions to be

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} S_{3}^{\text{SD}}&=\frac{1}{2g^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}\int\partial_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{A,}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,\Phi^{A,}{}_{B^{\prime}}\,\Phi^{A,B^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{4g^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}\int\epsilon^{B^{\prime}C^{\prime}}(\epsilon^{AC}\partial^{BA^{\prime}}+\epsilon^{AB}\partial^{CA^{\prime}})\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}\Phi_{C,C^{\prime}}\,,\\
S_{3}^{\text{ASD}}&=\frac{1}{2g^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}\int\partial_{AA^{\prime}}\Phi{}^{A,}{}_{A^{\prime}}\,\Phi{}_{B,}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,\Phi^{B,A^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{4g^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}\int\epsilon^{BC}(\epsilon^{A^{\prime}C^{\prime}}\partial^{AB^{\prime}}+\epsilon^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\partial^{AC^{\prime}})\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}\Phi_{C,C^{\prime}}\,,\end{aligned}

(F.13)

where the derivative is acting only on the first field.

An immediate difference from SDYM is that the YM propagator and vertex do not discriminate between different helicity structures. Especially, the SD and ASD vertex take on a much more complicated form,
62
62

62

In order to evaluate the field theory using perturbative methods, we rescale
\Phi\rightarrow g\Phi
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} V^{\text{SD}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime}}&=\frac{g}{2}\Big[\epsilon_{B^{\prime}C^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{AC}p^{1}_{BA^{\prime}}+\epsilon_{AB}p^{1}_{CA^{\prime}})+\epsilon_{C^{\prime}A^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{BA}p^{2}_{CB^{\prime}}+\epsilon_{BC}p^{2}_{AB^{\prime}})+\\
&+\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{CB}p^{3}_{AC^{\prime}}+\epsilon_{CA}p^{3}_{BC^{\prime}})\Big]\,,\\
V^{\text{ASD}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime}}&=\frac{g}{2}\Big[\epsilon_{BC}(\epsilon_{A^{\prime}C^{\prime}}p^{1}_{AB^{\prime}}+\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}p^{1}_{AC^{\prime}})+\epsilon_{CA}(\epsilon_{B^{\prime}A^{\prime}}p^{2}_{BC^{\prime}}+\epsilon_{B^{\prime}C^{\prime}}p^{2}_{BA^{\prime}})+\\
&+\epsilon_{AB}(\epsilon_{C^{\prime}B^{\prime}}p^{3}_{CA^{\prime}}+\epsilon_{C^{\prime}A^{\prime}}p^{3}_{CB^{\prime}})\Big]\,,\end{aligned}

(F.14)

respectively. The YM vertex and the topological vertex read

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime}}&=V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime}}^{\text{SD}}+V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime}}^{\text{ASD}}\,,\\
V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime}}^{\text{top}}&=V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime}}^{\text{SD}}-V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime}}^{\text{ASD}}\,,\end{aligned}

(F.15)

respectively. This is also depicted in Figure
19
and
20
, where the SD and ASD vertices are represented by a black and white dot, respectively.

p_{1}
p_{2}
p_{3}
(+,AA^{\prime})
(+,BB^{\prime})
(-,CC^{\prime})
=
+
p_{1}
p_{2}
p_{3}
YM vertex:

Any topological contributions are expected to vanish, as flat space has no boundary. This can also be seen from the vertices directly, as we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} V^{\text{top}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime}}&\equiv V^{\text{SD}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime}}-V^{\text{ASD}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime}}=\\
&=g\Big[\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}C^{\prime}}(p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3})_{CB^{\prime}}-\epsilon_{AC}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}(p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3})_{BC^{\prime}}\Big]=0\,,\end{aligned}

(F.16)

which vanishes because of the four-momentum conservation. To obtain this expression, we used the identity

\displaystyle X^{A}Y^{B}=X^{B}Y^{A}+\epsilon^{AB}X_{C}Y^{C}\,.

(F.17)

(F.18)

In Figure

Although this leads to a wealth of diagrams for higher order correlators, the vertex simplifies greatly if positive helicity polarization vectors are attached. The polarization vectors for YM read
64
64

64

Note that the sign in
(\epsilon^{-})^{AA^{\prime}}
may differ from some sources in the literature. Our sign convention is chosen such that the YM and SDYM negative helicity polarization vectors are related through (
F.20
).

\displaystyle(\epsilon^{+}_{i})^{AA^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{i}^{A}\bar{i}^{A^{\prime}}}{\langle q_{i}i\rangle}\,,

\displaystyle(\epsilon^{-}_{i})^{AA^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{i^{A}\bar{q}_{i}^{A^{\prime}}}{\langle\bar{q}_{i}\bar{i}\rangle}\,.

(F.19)

Namely, the SD vertex simplifies using

\displaystyle p{}^{A}{}_{A^{\prime}}\,(\epsilon^{+}_{i})^{AA^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle p{}^{A}{}_{A^{\prime}}\,(\epsilon_{i}^{-})^{AA^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=i^{A}i^{A}\,.

(F.20)

We observe that when momenta act on positive helicity states it vanishes and it gives back the polarization vector for the
\Psi
field when acting on negative helicity states. For the ASD vertex we have

\displaystyle p^{AA^{\prime}}(\epsilon^{-}_{i}){}_{A}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle p^{AA^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{i}^{+}){}_{A}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,

\displaystyle=\bar{i}^{A^{\prime}}\bar{i}^{A^{\prime}}\,,

(F.21)

i.e. when the momentum acts on negative helicity states it gives zero and when it acts on the positive helicity states it gives the “anti-SDYM” polarization vectors. The three-point scattering amplitude obtained from the SD vertex is

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\text{SD}}_{3}=V^{\text{SD}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{1}^{+})^{AA^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{2}^{+})^{BB^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{3}^{-})^{CC^{\prime}}=-g\frac{\langle q_{1}3\rangle\langle q_{2}3\rangle}{\langle q_{1}1\rangle\langle q_{2}2\rangle}\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle=g\frac{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle^{3}}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{3}\rangle\langle\bar{2}\bar{3}\rangle}\,,

(F.22)

where (
F.7
) was used, and the ASD vertex yields
65
65

65

Although it also seems possible to eliminate
\bar{q}_{3}
using similar relations as (
F.7
), these relation would contain contractions between unbarred momentum spinors, e.g.
\langle 12\rangle
. For three-particle kinematics one must choose either
1^{A}\sim 2^{A}\sim 3^{A}
or
\bar{1}^{A}\sim\bar{2}^{A}\sim\bar{3}^{A}
. The contraction
\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle
already appears in the amplitude before applying any identities, so it is natural to choose the former. This makes the identities associated to
\bar{q}_{3}
ill-defined.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{A}^{\text{ASD}}_{3}=V^{\text{ASD}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime}}&(\epsilon_{1}^{+})^{AA^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{2}^{+})^{BB^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{3}^{-})^{CC^{\prime}}=\\
&=g\frac{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle}{\langle q_{1}1\rangle\langle q_{2}2\rangle\langle\bar{q}_{3}\bar{3}\rangle}\Big(\langle q_{1}1\rangle\langle q_{2}3\rangle\langle\bar{q}_{3}\bar{1}\rangle+\langle q_{1}3\rangle\langle q_{2}2\rangle\langle\bar{q}_{3}\bar{2}\rangle\Big)=\\
&=g\frac{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle^{2}}{\langle\bar{q}_{3}\bar{3}\rangle}\frac{\langle\bar{q}_{3}\bar{2}\rangle\langle\bar{1}\bar{3}\rangle-\langle\bar{q}_{3}\bar{1}\rangle\langle\bar{2}\bar{3}\rangle}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{3}\rangle\langle\bar{2}\bar{3}\rangle}=g\frac{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle^{3}}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{3}\rangle\langle\bar{2}\bar{3}\rangle}\,,\end{aligned}

(F.23)

where (
F.7
) was used, together with the Fierz identity

\displaystyle\langle ij\rangle\langle km\rangle+\langle ki\rangle\langle jm\rangle+\langle jk\rangle\langle im\rangle=0\,.

(F.24)

It is then straightforward to see that the YM amplitude is

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{3}=\mathcal{A}_{3}^{\text{SD}}+\mathcal{A}_{3}^{\text{ASD}}=2g\frac{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle^{3}}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{3}\rangle\langle\bar{2}\bar{3}\rangle}\,,

(F.25)

which matches the SDYM result (
F.8
). Furthermore, the topological term vanishes,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{3}^{\text{top}}=\mathcal{A}_{3}^{\text{SD}}-\mathcal{A}_{3}^{\text{ASD}}=0\,.

(F.26)

The latter implies that
\mathcal{A}_{3}=2\mathcal{A}_{3}^{\text{SD}}
, which is visualized in Figure
24
.

F.2
Four point

F.2.1
SDYM: Feynman/Lorenz gauge

We start by computing the flat-space scattering amplitude for SDYM as a warm-up. This will also facilitate the comparison with the
\text{AdS}_{4}
correlator in the flat limit. To this end we first compute the propagator. The final answer was already discussed in Section
5
; here we provide more detail. The kinetic term in the action can be written as

\displaystyle S_{\text{kin}}=-\mathrm{Tr}\int\Psi^{A,B}\epsilon_{BC}p_{AC^{\prime}}\Phi^{C,C^{\prime}}\,.

(F.27)

Here, the Feynman gauge is used where the Nakanishi-Lautrup field is absorbed into
\Psi^{A,B}
. The kinetic operator reads

\displaystyle K_{A,B;C,C^{\prime}}(p)=-\epsilon_{BC}p_{AC^{\prime}}

(F.28)

and the propagator
G_{A,B^{\prime};C,C^{\prime}}(p)
should satisfy

\displaystyle K(p){}_{A,B;}{}^{E,E^{\prime}}\,G_{C,D;E,E^{\prime}}(p)=-\epsilon_{AC}\epsilon_{BD}\,.

(F.29)

It is useful to write the Feynman gauge propagator as

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{A,B}(-p)\Phi_{C,C^{\prime}}(p)\rangle_{\text{F}}\equiv G_{A,B;C,C^{\prime}}(p)\,,

(F.30)

which allows one to obtain
\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(p)\Psi_{B,C}(p)\rangle_{\text{F}}
by replacing
p\rightarrow-p
and renaming the indices accordingly. It is then clear that on the other leg one imposes

\displaystyle K(-p){}^{M,N}{}_{;A,A^{\prime}}\,G_{M,N;BB^{\prime}}=-\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\,.

(F.31)

Now, (
F.29
) and (
F.31
) become

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} -p{}_{D}{}^{M^{\prime}}\,\langle\Psi_{A,B}(-p)\Phi_{C,M^{\prime}}(p)\rangle_{\text{F}}&=-\epsilon_{AD}\epsilon_{BC}\,,&p{}^{M}{}_{A^{\prime}}\,\langle\Psi_{M,A}(-p)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(p)\rangle_{\text{F}}&=-\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\,.\end{aligned}

(F.32)

The left-hand sides of these relations come with a different sign, because the kinetic operator acts on the leg with momentum
-p
.
As a check, the propagator should also satisfy the equations of motion for the
\langle\Phi\Psi\rangle
propagator,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} p{}_{B}{}^{M^{\prime}}\,\langle\Phi_{A,M^{\prime}}(-p)\Psi_{C,D}(p)\rangle_{\text{F}}&=-\epsilon_{AD}\epsilon_{BC}\,,&-p{}^{M}{}_{B^{\prime}}\,\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-p)\Psi_{M,B}(p)\rangle_{\text{F}}&=-\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\,.\end{aligned}

(F.33)

Inverting the kinetic term now gives

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\Psi_{A,B}(-p)\Phi_{C,C^{\prime}}(p)\rangle_{\text{F}}&=-\frac{1}{p^{2}}\epsilon_{BC}p_{AC^{\prime}}\,,&\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-p)\Psi_{B,C}(p)\rangle_{\text{F}}&=-\frac{1}{p^{2}}\epsilon_{AC}p_{BA^{\prime}}\,.\end{aligned}

(F.34)

Note that they are indeed related by swapping the indices and reversing the sign of the momentum,
p\leftrightarrow-p
. The interaction term is given by

\displaystyle S_{\text{int}}=\mathrm{Tr}\int\Psi^{AA}\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{A,}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}\int(\epsilon^{AC}\epsilon^{BD}+\epsilon^{AD}\epsilon^{BC})\epsilon^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}\Psi_{C,D}\,.

(F.35)

Please note that the indices on
\Psi
are symmetrized in the interaction term. This allowed us to add the Nakanishi-Lautrup field introduced in Section
5.3
, i.e. replace
\Psi^{AA}\rightarrow\Psi^{A,B}
; due to the explicit symmetrization, the Nakanishi-Lautrup field actually does not enter the interaction. This has the advantage that the Feynman rules can now be written completely in terms of
\Psi^{A,B}
. However, for simplicity we stick to the symmetrized
\Psi^{AA}
, which effectively leads to choosing the Lorenz gauge. The propagator and vertex in this gauge is given in Figure
25
.

p
(-,AA)
(+,BB^{\prime})

:

\langle\Psi_{AA}(-p)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(p)\rangle_{\text{F}}=-\frac{1}{p^{2}}\epsilon_{AB}p_{AB^{\prime}}

p_{1}
p_{2}
p_{3}

:

SDYM Lorenz gauge Feynman rules in flat space.

The

. Explicitly, using

, the

Figure 26:
SDYM Lorenz gauge
s

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{s}=4g^{2}\frac{\langle q_{1}4\rangle\langle q_{2}4\rangle\langle q_{3}4\rangle}{\langle q_{1}1\rangle\langle q_{2}2\rangle\langle q_{3}3\rangle}\frac{\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle}{\langle 12\rangle}\,.

(F.36)

The
t
-channel amplitude is simply obtained by swapping
1\leftrightarrow 3
,
\bar{1}\leftrightarrow\bar{3}
and
q_{1}\leftrightarrow q_{3}
and reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{t}=-4g^{2}\frac{\langle q_{1}4\rangle\langle q_{2}4\rangle\langle q_{3}4\rangle}{\langle q_{1}1\rangle\langle q_{2}2\rangle\langle q_{3}3\rangle}\frac{\langle\bar{1}\bar{4}\rangle}{\langle 23\rangle}\,.

(F.37)

The color-ordered partial amplitude is obtained by summing the
s
-channel and
t
-channel amplitude,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{s}+\mathcal{A}_{t}=4g^{2}\frac{\langle q_{1}4\rangle\langle q_{2}4\rangle\langle q_{3}4\rangle}{\langle q_{1}1\rangle\langle q_{2}2\rangle\langle q_{3}3\rangle}\left(\frac{\langle 23\rangle\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle-\langle 12\rangle\langle\bar{1}\bar{4}\rangle}{\langle 12\rangle\langle 23\rangle}\right)=0\,.

(F.38)

In the last equality we used momentum conservation,

\displaystyle\frac{\langle 23\rangle\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle-\langle 12\rangle\langle\bar{1}\bar{4}\rangle}{\langle 12\rangle\langle 23\rangle}=-\frac{(1^{A}\bar{1}^{A^{\prime}}+2^{A}\bar{2}^{A^{\prime}}+3^{A}\bar{3}^{A^{\prime}}+4^{A}\bar{4}^{A^{\prime}})2_{A}\bar{4}_{A^{\prime}}}{\langle 12\rangle\langle 23\rangle}=0\,.

(F.39)

F.2.2
SDYM: Axial gauge

Scattering amplitudes are gauge-invariant, but the Feynman rules are not. It is therefore instructive to see why a gauge transformation does not affect the amplitudes. Here we will investigate axial gauge, but the reasoning extends to other gauges. The boundary of anti-de Sitter space will lead to important corrections to the arguments below.

In axial gauge, the gauge field
\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}
is orthogonal to a spacelike vector
n^{AA^{\prime}}
. The Feynman rules are summarized in Figure
27
. Note that the vertex used is just the same as in Lorenz gauge, while it should actually be projected onto its
n
-orthogonal component in its first two pairs of indices,

\displaystyle V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC}=\epsilon_{AC}\epsilon_{BC}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2n^{2}}\big(\epsilon_{AC}n_{CA^{\prime}}n_{BB^{\prime}}-\epsilon_{BC}n_{CB^{\prime}}n_{AA^{\prime}}\big)\,.

(F.40)

However, since the legs on the vertices will be contracted with
n
-orthogonal states, this is realized automatically.

p
(-,AA)
(+,BB^{\prime})

:

\begin{aligned} &\langle\Psi^{AA}(-p)\Phi^{BB^{\prime}}(p)\rangle_{\text{A}}=\\
&=-\frac{1}{p^{2}}\left(\epsilon^{AB}p^{AB^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2(p\cdot n)}p{}^{A}{}_{M^{\prime}}\,n^{AM^{\prime}}p^{BB^{\prime}}\right)\end{aligned}

p_{1}
p_{2}
p_{3}
(+,AA^{\prime})
(+,BB^{\prime})
(-,CC)

:

V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC}=2g\,\epsilon_{AC}\epsilon_{BC}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}

Figure 27:
SDYM axial gauge Feynman rules in flat space.

The polarization vectors are again given by (
F.3
). In axial gauge, the reference spinors are restricted by the condition that the polarization vectors need to be
n
-transverse, i.e.
n^{AA^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{i}^{+})_{AA^{\prime}}=0
.
66
66

66

In AdS we will choose
n_{AA^{\prime}}=\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}
and the polarization vectors become symmetric in axial gauge,
(\epsilon_{i}^{+})^{AA^{\prime}}=\frac{\bar{i}^{A}\bar{i}^{A^{\prime}}}{2k_{i}}
and
(\epsilon_{i}^{-})^{AA}=i^{A}i^{A}
.
For our purposes it is not necessary to solve this explicitly; we only need to keep in mind that these polarization vectors are also admissible in the Lorenz gauge. We see that the first term in the propagator is just the Lorenz gauge propagator. In fact, the second term can be written as a gauge transformation of the gauge field,

\displaystyle\langle\Psi^{AA}(-p)\Phi^{BB^{\prime}}(p)\rangle_{\text{A}}=\langle\Psi^{AA}(-p)\Phi^{BB^{\prime}}(p)\rangle_{\text{F}}+p^{BB^{\prime}}\xi^{AA}\,,

(F.41)

with
\xi_{AA}=-\frac{1}{p^{2}}\frac{p{}^{A}{}_{M^{\prime}}\,n^{AM^{\prime}}}{2(p\cdot n)}
. It is then not hard to see that

\displaystyle p^{AA^{\prime}}V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC}\Big|_{\text{two legs on-shell}}=0

(F.42)

and the gauge variation disappears from the amplitude. We thus conclude that the scattering amplitude in axial gauge agrees with the amplitude in the Lorenz gauge, but with specialized reference spinors. This result holds channel by channel.

F.2.3
YM: Feynman gauge

In Section
F.1.2
, we encountered a significant simplification of the three-point amplitude for YM: instead of two different diagrams involving the SD and ASD vertex, the three-point amplitude was expressed using only the SD vertex with the appropriate coefficient. Moreover, this diagram turned out to be easier to evaluate than the diagram containing the ASD vertex, due to the helicities configuration
(++-)
, where most external states have positive helicity.

In this section, we aim to extend this simplification to the four-point exchange diagram with helicity configuration
(+++-)
and compute the four-point amplitude. The simplification of the three-point amplitude arose from an analogous simplifcation at the level of the vertex: the YM vertex can be written entirely in terms of the SD vertex,

\displaystyle V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime}}=2V^{\text{SD}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime}}\,,

(F.43)

as a consequence of the vanishing boundary term. As a result, the
s
-channel diagram can also be expressed using only the SD vertex, see Figure
28
. This diagram contains two vertices, which leads to
9
distinct diagrams corresponding to different derivative placements, according to the decomposition shown in Figure
22
. Fortunately, (
F.20
) and (
F.21
) imply that any diagram vanishes when a derivative acts on a positive helicity external state. For the helicity configuration
(+++-)
, only

non-vanishing diagrams remain, as is shown in Figure
28
.

(F.45)

The contact diagram gives

More precisely, the contact diagram gives three different contributions, proportional to
