\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\text{contact}}=2g^{2}(\epsilon_{1}^{+})^{AA^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{2}^{+})^{BB^{\prime}}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\epsilon_{C^{\prime}D^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{AC}\epsilon_{BD}+\epsilon_{AD}\epsilon_{BC})(\epsilon_{3}^{+})^{CC^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{4}^{-})^{DD^{\prime}}\,.

Thus, the bottom-right diagram in Figure

cancels the contact diagram. In other words,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{s}^{\text{cYM}}+\mathcal{A}_{\text{contact}}=\mathcal{A}_{s}^{\text{SDYM}}\,.

(F.48)

Gauge independence.

It is now easy to see that the scattering amplitude in cYM in Feynman gauge and axial gauge are identical. The same arguments as for SDYM in Section
F.2.2
are used to show that the diagram with one derivative on the internal line is gauge-invariant. As we have seen in this section, only the propagator (with a gauge field on at least one of the legs) changes in axial gauge, while other quantities remain the same. However, the new exchange diagram that appears in cYM contains the
\langle\Psi_{AA}(-p)\Psi_{BB}(p)\rangle
propagator, which is constructed out of gauge-invariant quantities and the contact diagram contains no propagators. In fact, these two diagrams cancel against each other in any gauge.

Appendix G
Gauge (in)dependence day

Let us consider the difference between two four-point functions computed in different gauges. Two gauges differ by pure gauge terms for the bulk-to-bulk propagator (we omit the indices).

\displaystyle G^{\prime}

\displaystyle=G+\Delta G\,,

\displaystyle\Delta G(k,z;k^{\prime},z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\text{L}}\xi_{\text{L}}+\nabla_{\text{R}}\xi_{\text{R}}+\nabla_{\text{L}}\nabla_{\text{R}}\xi_{\text{c}}\,.

(G.1)

Let us consider the simplest example of an exchange between two conserved currents built from scalar fields, which can occur in scalar electrodynamics. The difference between two gauges is

\displaystyle\Delta\langle OOOO\rangle

\displaystyle=\int J^{\mu}(k_{1},k_{2},z)\Delta G(k,z;k^{\prime},z^{\prime})_{\mu\nu}J^{\nu}(k_{3},k_{4},z^{\prime})\,.

(G.2)

The bulk terms disappear due to
\nabla_{\mu}J^{\mu}=0
when the two legs of the current are on-shell, i.e. on the boundary. The remnants are the boundary terms (note that
\vec{k}_{12}=-\vec{k}_{34}
):
68
68

68

Note that for the Lorenz/axial propagators the
\xi_{\mu}
are such that
\xi_{\mu}\sim n_{\mu}
, i.e. only the bulk integral against
J^{z}
can contribute, see below.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta\langle OOOO\rangle&=\int dz^{\prime}\,J^{z}(k_{1},k_{2},0)\xi_{\nu}^{\text{L}}(\vec{k}_{12};z=0;z^{\prime})J^{\nu}(k_{3},k_{4},z^{\prime})+\\
&+\int dz\,J^{\mu}(k_{1},k_{2},z)\xi_{\mu}^{\text{R}}(\vec{k}_{12};z;z^{\prime}=0)J^{z}(k_{3},k_{4},0)+\\
&\qquad+J^{z}(k_{1},k_{2},0)\xi_{\text{c}}(\vec{k}_{12};z=0;z^{\prime}=0)J^{z}(k_{3},k_{4},0)\,.\end{aligned}

(G.3)

Let us restrict to the toy model with four scalar fields interacting with
A_{\mu}
pairwise (so that there are no additional symmetrizations). The current, its
z
-component we need, is

\displaystyle J_{z}

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}\overleftrightarrow{\partial_{z}}\phi_{2}=f_{1}f_{2}\exp[-(k_{1}+k_{2})z](k_{1}-k_{2})\,,

(G.4)

where
f_{1,2}
are
k_{i}^{-1}
for Neumann and
f_{1,2}=1
for Dirichlet. At the boundary the current is just
J_{z}=f_{1}f_{2}(k_{1}-k_{2})
. For the Dirichlet boundary condition
f_{i}=1
and

\displaystyle\int dz^{\prime}\,

\displaystyle J^{z}(k_{1},k_{2},0)\xi_{\nu}^{\text{L}}(\vec{k}_{12};z=0;z^{\prime})J^{\nu}(k_{3},k_{4},z^{\prime})=(k_{1}-k_{2})\int dz^{\prime}\,\xi_{\nu}(\vec{k}_{12},z=0;z^{\prime})J^{\nu}(k_{3},k_{4},z^{\prime})\,,

which, when split into two terms, better be analytic in either
k_{1}
or
k_{2}
. A typical pure gauge term would contain
\exp[-k_{12}z^{\prime}]
at
z=0
and it is impossible to get rid of
k_{1,2}
dependence! Therefore, the only possibility seems to be that
\xi
vanishes at the boundary.

To understand the origin of contact terms, let us consider an example of a four-point correlator

\displaystyle\int\prod_{i=1}^{i=4}d^{3}k_{i}\,\delta^{3}(\sum k_{i})e^{i\sum k_{j}x_{j}}F(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle=\int\prod_{i=1}^{i=3}d^{3}k_{i}\,e^{i\sum_{i}k_{i}(x_{i}-x_{4})}F(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\,,

(G.5)

where
F
is assumed not to depend on, say,
k_{4}
thanks to the momentum conservation.
Provided,
F
depends on, say,
k_{2}
analytically, e.g. it does not depend on it at all,
\exp ik_{2}(x_{2}-x_{4})
factors out and produces
\delta^{3}(x_{2}-x_{4})
. This is how one can get a sum of contact terms

\displaystyle\Delta\langle OOOO\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\delta(x_{i}-x_{j})V(...)\,.

(G.6)

Whenever, the external operators are currents themselves, such contact terms mimic the Ward identities

\displaystyle\partial_{1}\cdot\langle JJJJ\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\delta(x_{1}-x_{i})\langle JJJ\rangle\,.

(G.7)

G.1
Dirichlet Yang–Mills theory

It is important to make sure that the results for gauge-invariant observables do not depend on the gauge. For the Dirichlet boundary conditions the dual operator is a current
J_{i}
and via Witten diagrams we are computing the generating functional
W[A^{i}]
of its correlators. The difference between the Dirichlet propagator in Feynman gauge and axial gauge reads

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)&\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}=\frac{\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{4k}\Big(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}+e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\Big)+\\
&-\frac{k_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}}{4k^{3}}\Big(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}-e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}+k\big(|z-z^{\prime}|-(z+z^{\prime})\big)\Big)\,,\end{aligned}

(G.8)

which can be written as a total derivative (pure gauge),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}&=\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}^{-k,z}\xi_{BB^{\prime}}^{\text{R}}+\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\xi^{\text{L}}_{AA^{\prime}}+\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}^{-k,z}\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\xi^{\text{c}}\,,\\
\xi^{\text{L}}_{AA^{\prime}}&=\frac{\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}}{4k^{2}}\Big(\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})\big(1-e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\big)-\big(1-e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\big)\Big)\,,\\
\xi^{\text{R}}_{BB^{\prime}}&=-\frac{\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{4k^{2}}\Big(\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})\big(1-e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\big)+\big(1-e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\big)\Big)\,,\\
\xi^{\text{c}}&=\frac{1}{4k^{3}}\Big(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}-e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}+k\big(|z-z^{\prime}|-(z+z^{\prime})\big)\Big)\,.\end{aligned}

(G.9)

Now, the crucial observation is that the “gauge parameters” vanish at the boundary:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \xi^{\text{L}}_{AA^{\prime}}(z^{\prime}=0)&=0\,,&\xi^{\text{R}}_{BB^{\prime}}(z=0)&=0\,,&\xi^{\text{c}}(z=0)=\xi^{\text{c}}(z^{\prime}=0)=0\,.\end{aligned}

(G.10)

In other words, the difference between the Feynman and axial gauge computations can be reduced via integration by parts to a boundary term. The latter vanishes thanks to the above (otherwise, it would be impossible to produce even a contact term). The integration by parts lands
\nabla
onto the two on-shell fields of a vertex, which gives zero by the equations of motion. This proves the gauge independence in Yang–Mills theory for the Dirichlet boundary condition.

In the Chalmers–Siegel formulation or, whenever the derivative from the Yang–Mills vertex hits the
\langle\Phi\Phi\rangle
bulk-to-bulk propagator, the
\langle\Psi\Phi\rangle
two-point function emerges. The difference between the Dirichlet propagator in Feynman gauge and axial gauge reads

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)&\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}=-\frac{k_{AA}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{4k}\Big(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}+e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\Big)+\\
&+\frac{k_{AA}k_{BB^{\prime}}}{4k^{2}}\Big(\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})\big(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}-1\big)-\big(e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}-1\big)\Big)\,.\end{aligned}

(G.11)

This difference can be written as pure gauge:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta&\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}=\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\eta_{AA}\,,\\
&\eta_{AA}=-\frac{k_{AA}}{4k^{2}}\Big(\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})\big(1-e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\big)-\big(1-e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\big)\Big)\,,\end{aligned}

(G.12)

where the gauge parameter
\eta_{AA}
vanishes at
z^{\prime}=0
. Therefore, the gauge independence is also manifest for
\langle\Psi\Phi\rangle
-propagator.

Neumann comment.

The difference between the Neumann propagator in Feynman gauge and axial gauge reads

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{N}}&=\frac{1}{4k}(\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}-\frac{k_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}}{k^{2}})(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}+e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})})+\\
&-\frac{k_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}}{4k^{2}}(|z-z^{\prime}|-(z+z^{\prime}))-\frac{\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{2k}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,.\end{aligned}

(G.13)

The inhomogeneous part is the same as for the Dirichlet case. However, there is a discrete difference in the homogeneous terms. This gauge variation can be expressed as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{N}}&=\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}^{-k,z}\xi_{BB^{\prime}}^{\text{R}}+\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\xi^{\text{L}}_{AA^{\prime}}+\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}^{-k,z}\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\xi^{\text{c}}\,,\\
\xi^{\text{L}}_{AA^{\prime}}&=\frac{\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}}{4k^{2}}\Big(\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})\big(1-e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\big)-\big(1+e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\big)\Big)\,,\\
\xi^{\text{R}}_{BB^{\prime}}&=-\frac{\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{4k^{2}}\Big(\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})\big(1-e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\big)+\big(1+e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\big)\Big)\,,\\
\xi^{\text{c}}&=\frac{1}{4k^{3}}\Big(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}+e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}+k\big(|z-z^{\prime}|-(z+z^{\prime})\big)\Big)\,.\end{aligned}

(G.14)

What has changed as compared to the Dirichlet case is the sign of the
\exp[-k(z+z^{\prime})]
-terms. The latter forces the gauge parameters not to vanish at the boundary. However, for Neumann boundary conditions the situation is more complicated once the dual field is a gauge field
A_{i}
on the boundary. Via AdS/CFT we are computing the correlation functions of this gauge field, which, of course, do depend on the gauge choice (the bulk gauge choice also implies a certain gauge choice on the boundary as is visible from the boundary limit of
\langle\Phi\Phi\rangle
). For
U(1)
the gauge field
A_{i}
can be mapped to the dual current
J=*dA
, which is manifestly conserved, and for this case we would expect the difference between two gauges to suddenly become a contact term. However, we have a nonabelian gauge field in the bulk and, hence, on the boundary. The dual current is
J=*(dA+gAA)
and is only covariantly conserved
D_{A}J=0
. There are two complications: (i) to build a current we need higher order corrections
gAA
; (ii)
D_{A}
depends on the gauge field. Below, in Section
G.2
, we consider the simplest example where the gauge field is still abelian.

To streamline the calculation of the gauge variation between Feynman gauge and axial gauge, it is convenient to consider the difference between the gauge variation for the Neumann and Dirichlet case, which we call
\Delta\Delta\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle
. This difference between the gauge variations is

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Delta\Delta\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle\equiv\Delta\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{N}}-\Delta\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}=\\
&=\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}^{-k,z}\Delta\xi^{\text{R}}_{BB^{\prime}}+\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\Delta\xi^{\text{L}}_{AA^{\prime}}+\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}^{-k,z}\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\Delta\xi^{\text{c}}\,,\end{aligned}

(G.15)

with

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta\xi_{AA^{\prime}}^{\text{L}}&=-\frac{\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}}{2k^{2}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,,&\Delta\xi_{BB^{\prime}}^{\text{R}}&=-\frac{\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{2k^{2}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,,&\Delta\xi^{\text{c}}&=\frac{1}{2k^{3}}e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\,.\end{aligned}

(G.16)

After performing the bulk integral, the gauge variation for the Dirichlet case vanishes and we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{A}}\,e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=\\
&=\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\Big(\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{L}}+\Delta\Delta\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle\Big)e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}\,.\end{aligned}

(G.17)

Thus, in the exchange diagram
\Delta\Delta\langle\Phi\Phi\rangle
contains all the relevant information. Taking one derivative, one finds the propagator

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Delta\Delta\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle\equiv\Delta\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{N}}-\Delta\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}=\\
&=\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\Delta\eta^{\text{L}}_{AA}\,,\end{aligned}

(G.18)

where

\displaystyle\Delta\eta^{\text{L}}_{AA}=k{}_{A}{}^{A^{\prime}}\,\Delta\xi^{\text{L}}_{AA^{\prime}}\,.

(G.19)

The double derivative gives

\displaystyle\Delta\Delta\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Psi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle=0\,.

(G.20)

For the single and double derivative cases a similar relation to (
G.17
) exists and
\Delta\Delta\langle\Psi\Phi\rangle
and
\Delta\Delta\langle\Psi\Psi\rangle
are sufficient to compute the gauge variation of exchange diagrams.

G.2
Neumann scalar QED

The simplest toy model is scalar electrodynamics in the bulk. It features the cubic vertex
\bar{\phi}\overleftrightarrow{\partial_{\mu}}\phi
and the quartic vertex
A_{\mu}A^{\mu}\bar{\phi}\phi
. Importantly, the current,
J=-\bar{\phi}\partial\phi+\partial\bar{\phi}\phi-2\bar{\phi}A\phi
is abelian, i.e.
\partial\cdot J=0
on-shell. To check the Neumann case we need a diagram with both internal and external
A
-lines, see Fig.
31
. We evaluate the difference between two gauges in the Neumann case in two steps. Firstly, we already know that the difference between the Feynman and axial gauge vanishes for Dirichlet boundary conditions. Secondly, there is an additional difference to account for the jump to the Neumann case (the boundary-to-bulk propagator for the external
A
also needs to be changed). The latter difference has a very special structure: all
\xi_{\text{L,R}}
are proportional to
n_{\mu}
and the
\partial_{z}
-part of
\nabla
that gives a contribution upon integration by parts is also along
n_{\mu}
. Now,
\xi_{\text{R}}
upon integration by parts leads to
\partial^{\mu}J_{\mu}=0
for free fields represented by the propagators. The boundary term gives

\displaystyle J^{z}(k_{1},k_{2},0)\int\xi_{\text{R}}(k_{1}+k_{2},0,z^{\prime})A_{z}(k_{4},z^{\prime})\phi(k_{3},z^{\prime})\bar{\phi}(k_{5},z^{\prime})

(G.21)

where we note that all
\xi
are proportional to
n_{\mu}
. As a result we pick the
A_{z}
-component on the boundary, while in the Neumann case we need
A_{i}
on the boundary. In other words, the contribution to
A_{i}
from the boundary terms vanishes. There is also another diagram, with three cubic vertices to which this one ’talks’ via gauge invariance when the
\nabla
that belongs to the quartic vertex is integrated by parts. The bulk contribution vanishes as in flat space, but we do not go as far as to analyze the additional terms for this diagram.

k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{5}
k_{3}
k_{4}

Figure 31:
The simplest correlator that has both a gauge field on internal line and one external leg with a gauge field.

G.3
Relation to flat space

The difference between the Dirichlet propagator in Lorenz gauge and axial gauge was given in (
G.9
). It is instructive to perform the bulk integrals one encounters in the exchange diagram for this gauge variation. This reads

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}^{-k,z}\xi^{\text{R}}_{BB^{\prime}}e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}&=\frac{1}{4k}\frac{E_{\text{L}}+E_{\text{R}}+E}{EE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}(E_{\text{R}}-k)}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}\,(1_{A}\bar{1}_{A^{\prime}}+2_{A}\bar{2}_{A^{\prime}})\,,\\
\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\xi^{\text{L}}_{AA^{\prime}}e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}&=\frac{1}{4k}\frac{E_{\text{L}}+E_{\text{R}}+E}{EE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}(E_{\text{L}}-k)}\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}(3_{B}\bar{3}_{B^{\prime}}+4_{B}\bar{4}_{B^{\prime}})\,,\\
\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}^{-k,z}\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\xi^{\text{c}}e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}&=-\frac{1}{4k}\frac{E_{\text{L}}+E_{\text{R}}+E}{EE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}(E_{\text{L}}-k)(E_{\text{R}}-k)}\times\\
&\times(1_{A}\bar{1}_{A^{\prime}}+2_{A}\bar{2}_{A^{\prime}})(3_{B}\bar{3}_{B^{\prime}}+4_{B}\bar{4}_{B^{\prime}})\,.\end{aligned}

(G.22)

Together, the integral of the total gauge variation is

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\Delta\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{4k}\frac{E_{\text{L}}+E_{\text{R}}+E}{EE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\times\\
&\times\Big(\frac{(1_{A}\bar{1}_{A^{\prime}}+2_{A}\bar{2}_{A})\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{k_{1}+k_{2}}+\frac{\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}(3_{B}\bar{3}_{B^{\prime}}+4_{B}\bar{4}_{B^{\prime}})}{k_{3}+k_{4}}-\frac{(1_{A}\bar{1}_{A^{\prime}}+2_{A}\bar{2}_{A^{\prime}})(3_{B}\bar{3}_{B^{\prime}}+4_{B}\bar{4}_{B^{\prime}})}{(k_{1}+k_{2})(k_{3}+k_{4})}\Big)\,,\end{aligned}

(G.23)

which is reminiscent of the difference between the flat-space axial gauge propagator and the Feynman one, c.f. (
5.46
). This allows us to recycle the flat space argument found in
F.2.2
that explains why the flat-space amplitude is gauge invariant, to argue that the AdS correlator for Dirichlet boundary conditions is gauge invariant too. Note that this is an alternative proof of gauge independence to the one given around (
G.10
). While the latter is a shorter proof, the one laid out here bears resemblance with flat-space results.

It now becomes obvious from the decomposition of the gauge parameters into their inhomogeneous and homogeneous components that the Dirichlet boundary condition is a very special case. Precisely the right homogeneous terms are present to recover the flat-space four-momentum (in the flat limit). As will be proved soon, for other boundary conditions an additional structure, that is unfamiliar to flat space, must be added.

Another important observation is that the exchange diagram integral of the gauge variation recovers the flat-space gauge variation exactly, while the same integral of the gauge-independent part of the propagator only gives the flat-space propagator as the residue of the leading energy pole, while also producing higher orders in
E
. Of course, it is not very surprising that these higher orders do not show up here, as gauge invariance is an exact requirement. Meanwhile, the flat-space amplitude is only expected to be found in the flat-space limit of the AdS correlation function, leaving the possibility for higher order corrections in
E
.

It is again instructive to consider the integral for the difference between the gauge variation for the Neumann and Dirichlet case. We find

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}^{-k,z}\Delta\xi_{BB^{\prime}}^{\text{R}}e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}&=\frac{\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{2k^{2}E_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}(k_{AA^{\prime}}-k\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}})\,,\\
\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z}\Delta\xi_{AA^{\prime}}^{\text{L}}e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}&=-\frac{\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}}{2k^{2}E_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}(k_{BB^{\prime}}+k\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}})\,,\\
\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}^{-k,z}\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\Delta\xi^{\text{c}}e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}&=-\frac{1}{2k^{3}E_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}(k_{AA^{\prime}}-k\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}})(k_{BB^{\prime}}+k\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}})\end{aligned}

(G.24)

and

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\big(\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}^{-k,z}\Delta\xi_{BB^{\prime}}^{\text{R}}+\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\Delta\xi_{AA^{\prime}}^{\text{L}}+\nabla_{AA^{\prime}}^{-k,z}\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\Delta\xi^{\text{c}}\big)e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=\\
&=\frac{1}{2kE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\Big(-\frac{(-k_{AA^{\prime}}+k\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}})\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{k}-\frac{\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}(k_{BB^{\prime}}+k\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}})}{k}+\frac{(-k_{AA^{\prime}}+k\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}})(k_{BB^{\prime}}+k\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}})}{k^{2}}\Big)\,.\end{aligned}

(G.25)

Here we recognize a similar structure as for Dirichlet, but now the flat-space momentum is replaced by the fake null momenta
-k_{AA^{\prime}}+k\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}
and
k_{BB^{\prime}}+k\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}
.

To summarize, for Dirichlet boundary conditions the exchange channel integral of the gauge variation takes a form akin to the gauge variation of the propagator in flat space. In the Neumann and mixed case, this is supplemented by a similar structure containing the fake null-momentum instead of the flat-space momentum. Because the fake null-momentum is an AdS object, and gauge invariance does not hold for the Neumann boundary condition, it can be said that the Dirichlet case is closest to the flat-space treatment of Yang–Mills.

The same steps can be repeated for the gauge variation
\Delta\langle\Psi\Phi\rangle
. For Dirichlet boundary conditions this yields

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}&\Delta\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=\\
&=-\frac{1}{4k}\frac{E_{\text{L}}+E_{\text{R}}+E}{EE_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}(E_{\text{R}}-k)}k_{AA}(3_{B}\bar{3}_{B^{\prime}}+4_{B}\bar{4}_{B^{\prime}})\,.\end{aligned}

(G.26)

This is reminiscent of the gauge variation of the flat-space propagator, c.f. (
5.93
). The difference between the gauge variation for Neumann and Dirichlet gives a similar expression with the flat-space momentum replaced by the fake null momentum
k_{BB^{\prime}}+k\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}
,

\displaystyle\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\Delta\Delta\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{2k^{2}E_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}k_{AA}(k_{BB^{\prime}}+k\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}})\,.

(G.27)

Appendix H
Gauge-covariant correlator

In Section
6
it was discussed that the four-point correlation functions we computed are not expected to be gauge-covariant for SDYM and for cYM with mixed/Neumann(-like) boundary conditions. Indeed, we observed a difference between the correlators in Feynman gauge and axial gauge. The reason for this is that the gauge field in the bulk is dual to a gauge field in the CFT for non-Dirichlet boundary conditions. However, one can construct a gauge-covariant object in the CFT, the magnetic field
B=*(da+g\,a\wedge a)
. The SDYM correlation functions computed in Section
6
have a gauge field on the boundary only for the negative helicity. The first term effectively multiplies the diagram by
-k_{4}
, but we already did this implicitly by choosing the Dirichlet scaling of the boundary-to-bulk propagators. The second term can be computed by having not one, but two bulk/boundary-to-boundary propagators connected to the boundary point at which the negative helicity CFT data is inserted on the boundary, see Figure
32
. cYM is less constrained; a bulk
\Phi
field, for instance, can come from a
\langle\Psi\Phi\rangle
or a
\langle\Phi\Phi\rangle
boundary-to-bulk propagator. It is therefore also possible for the three bulk
\Phi
-legs to be coupled to a gauge field on the boundary and the magnetic field must then be constructed on all legs.

Another observation from Section
6.3
is that SDYM and Chalmers-Siegel do not seem to coincide in the self-dual limit: there is one topological diagram belonging to Chalmers-Siegel that, due to the presence of the
\Phi\Phi
-propagator, survives in the limit, while it is absent in SDYM. We will show that the composite diagrams are responsible for canceling the contribution of this topological diagram and that SDYM agrees with the self-dual limit of Chalmers-Siegel. As we shall show, only the
\gamma
-dependent component of the correlators associated with the composite diagrams is relevant to this end, so we restrict ourselves to use only the Neumann-like propagators and the same result follows automatically for the Dirichlet-like propagators.

The
a^{2}
term in the magnetic field gives rise to a two-point vertex on the boundary. However, at this boundary point a polarization vector is attached, so it effectively looks like a three-point vertex. It is expressed by

\displaystyle V^{aa}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC}=2g\,\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\epsilon_{AC}\epsilon_{BC}\,,

(H.1)

where the pair of indices is contracted with
\epsilon^{\pm}_{CC}
. Since the SDYM vertex was constructed from the same
\Phi^{2}
term in the field strength, it is not surprising to see that this vertex has the same expression; the only difference is that it takes place on the boundary.

For cYM, the contribution to the correlation function of the diagram in Figure
32
reads

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{W}^{1}_{s,\text{L},aa}&=\int\Phi_{+}^{A,A^{\prime}}(k_{1},z)\Phi_{+}^{B,B^{\prime}}(k_{2},z)V{}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}{}^{EE}\,\langle\Psi_{EE}(-k,z)\Phi_{F,F^{\prime}}(k,0)\rangle_{\text{L}}\times\\
&\times V{}_{aa}{}^{F,F^{\prime}}{}_{,CC^{\prime},DD}\,\Phi_{+}^{C,C^{\prime}}(k_{3},0)\Phi_{-}^{D,D}(k_{4},0)=\\
&=-\frac{g^{2}}{16k^{2}E_{\text{L}}}\frac{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle\langle\bar{3}4\rangle}{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}}\Big(\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}|kk\bar{k}\bar{k}|\bar{3}4\rangle+e^{-2i\gamma}\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}|\bar{k}\bar{k}kk|\bar{3}4\rangle-2k^{2}\big(\langle\bar{1}\bar{3}\rangle\langle\bar{2}4\rangle+\langle\bar{2}\bar{3}\rangle\langle\bar{1}4\rangle\big)\Big)\,.\end{aligned}

(H.2)

The SDYM result is obtained by taking the limit
\gamma\rightarrow-i\infty
and multiplying the result by
-2k_{4}
.
69
69

69

The latter is necessary for passing from cYM to Chalmers–Siegel. It is equivalent to the freedom we have to choose the negative helicity field to come from a
\langle\Psi\Phi\rangle
propagator or a
\langle\Phi\Phi\rangle
one.
Another diagram, yielding correlator
\mathcal{W}^{2}_{s,\text{L},aa}
, in which the right bulk vertex is replaced by a composite vertex is shown in Figure

. Evaluating the correlator naively, we find
\mathcal{W}^{2}_{s,\text{L},aa}=-\mathcal{W}^{1}_{s,\text{L},aa}
and
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{s,\text{L},aa}
and
\mathcal{W}^{2}_{s,\text{L},aa}

f^{abc}
that is implicitly present in the vertices. We then find
\mathcal{W}^{2}_{s,\text{L},aa}=\mathcal{W}^{1}_{s,\text{L},aa}
instead, so they add up.

where again color factors have been taken into account.

(H.6)

Which diagrams survive the self-dual limit?

The above diagrams are accompanied by factors of
e^{in\gamma}
that are carried by the external lines (and sometimes internal
\langle\Phi\Phi\rangle
line), and some diagrams come with the coefficient of the Chern–Simons term, which becomes
2(b-a)=4e^{-2i\gamma}
if we set
a=1
in the self-dual limit, see Section
5.6
for more details. As an example, the non-topological diagrams considered in the main text come with three positive helicity external lines and no internal
\langle\Phi\Phi\rangle
ones, so they carry an overall factor of
e^{6i\gamma}
, which will be divided out for all diagrams.

Performing the same counting on the above diagrams, we find the diagrams that survive the self-dual limit to be the non-topological diagram considered in the main text, together with
\mathcal{W}_{s,\text{L},aa}^{1}
,
\mathcal{W}_{s,\text{L},aa}^{3}
,
\mathcal{W}_{s,\text{L},aa}^{4}
,
\mathcal{T}_{s,\text{L},aa}^{1}
,
\mathcal{W}_{s,\text{L},(aa)^{2}}^{2}

\mathcal{W}_{s,\text{L},(aa)^{2}}^{3}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\text{L},1}_{\gamma}
. Only
\mathcal{W}_{s,\text{L},aa}^{1}
appears both in YM and in SDYM,
70
70

70

Differing by a factor
-2k_{4}
as mentioned before. For the correct choice of boundary-to-bulk propagator, or choosing Chalmers–Siegel’s one, the diagrams are the same.
while all others only exist in YM. Note that the last diagram is a topological diagram, see (
6.2.3
). Extracting the factor
e^{6i\gamma}
and adding the Chern--Simons coefficient to the topological diagram, one finds
71
71

71

The factor
e^{-2i\gamma}
that multiplies each diagram justifies the fact that we chose the Neumann-like propagator; the difference between the Neumann-like and Dirichlet-like propagators vanishes in the limit.

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow-i\infty}\Big[2(b-a)\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{\text{L},1}+e^{-2i\gamma}\mathcal{W}^{3}_{s,\text{L},aa}+e^{-2i\gamma}\mathcal{W}^{4}_{s,\text{L},aa}\Big]

\displaystyle=0\,,

(H.7a)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow-i\infty}\Big[2(b-a)\mathcal{T}_{s,\text{L},aa}^{1}+e^{-2i\gamma}\mathcal{W}^{2}_{s,\text{L},(aa)^{2}}+e^{-2i\gamma}\mathcal{W}^{3}_{s,\text{L},(aa)^{2}}\Big]

\displaystyle=0\,,

(H.7b)

since
\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow-i\infty}2(b-a)=4e^{-2i\gamma}
. This is also shown in Figures
38
and
39
. We see that (
H.7a
) implies that correlators in SDYM and the self-dual limit of cYM agree, as the composite diagrams cancel the topological diagram in the self-dual limit. Meanwhile, (
H.7b
) present the cancellation of various composite diagrams among themselves in the limit. Note that away from the self-dual limit these cancellations do not occur.

To summarize, YM/Chalmers–Siegel theory contains six composite diagrams and one topological diagram that survive in the self-dual limit, while SDYM yields only one composite diagram and no topological ones. In the self-dual limit three composite diagrams in Chalmers–Siegel cancel against each other, while two other composite diagrams cancel with the topological contribution. The end result is that both Chalmers–Siegel and SDYM end up with no topological diagrams and one composite diagram in the self-dual limit, on which they agree. Together with the agreement between the non-topological diagrams of Chalmers–Siegel and SDYM in the self-dual limit from the main text, this proves that the correlators of the self-dual limit Chalmers–Siegel coincide with SDYM.

Appendix I
Hamiltonian analysis, Complete gauge

It may be instructive to perform the canonical analysis of cYM and SDYM theories. For one reason, one can reduce the theories down to the physical variables by imposing a complete gauge. We begin with the Chalmers–Siegel action

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\int\Psi^{AA}(\nabla_{AB^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{A,}{}^{B^{\prime}}\,+\Phi_{A,B^{\prime}}\Phi{}_{A,}{}^{B}\,-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\Psi_{AA})\,.

(I.1)

We plug in the
3+1
decomposition
\Phi^{A,A^{\prime}}=\phi^{AA^{\prime}}+\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}\rho
and
\partial_{AA^{\prime}}=-\partial_{z}\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}+\eth_{AA^{\prime}}
;
\eth_{AB}=\eth_{BA}
is the partial derivative with respect to the spatial coordinates (we replace it by
k_{AB}
at the end omitting the Fourier
i
). The result is

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\int\Psi^{AA}\left(\dot{\phi}_{AA}+\eth_{AB}\phi{}_{A}{}^{B}\,+\eth_{AA}\rho+[\phi_{AA},\rho]+\phi_{AB}\phi{}_{A}{}^{B}\,-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\Psi_{AA}\right)\,.

(I.2)

Since the action is already written in the Hamiltonian-friendly form with
\Psi^{AA}
playing the role of momentum for
\phi_{AA}
,
72
72

72

Constraint analysis of an action
S=\int p\dot{q}-H(q,p)
will give it back. The primary constraints here are
\pi_{q}-p
and
\pi_{p}
, which are second class. Reducing the system onto the constraint surface replaces
\pi_{q}
with
p
and eliminates
\pi_{p}
.
one can cut the long story short and perform the “constraint analysis” for
\rho
. The primary constraint is
\tau^{1}=\pi_{\rho}
and the secondary is

\displaystyle\tau^{2}=\eth_{AA}\Psi^{AA}+[\phi_{AA},\Psi^{AA}]\equiv\eth_{i}\Psi^{i}+[\phi_{i},\Psi^{i}]\equiv D_{i}\Psi^{i}\,,

(I.3)

which are first class. This is as expected since
\delta\rho=-\dot{\xi}
,
\delta\phi_{AA}=\eth_{AA}\xi
at the linearized level. We can gauge-fix the theory by choosing two conditions that form together with
\tau^{1,2}
second class constraints. It is convenient to choose
\tau^{3}=\rho
and
\tau^{4}=\eth_{AA}\phi^{AA}
.

At the free level, we have
\eth_{AA}\phi^{AA}=0
and
\eth_{AA}\Psi^{AA}=0
, i.e.
k_{i}\phi^{i}=k_{i}\Psi^{i}=0
are two transverse vectors (as it would happen in the Coulomb gauge
\eth_{i}A^{i}=0
). The two-dimensional space orthogonal to
k^{i}
is spanned by
k^{A}k^{A}
and
{{\bar{k}}}^{A}{{\bar{k}}}^{A}
. In the flat space (without boundary) the equation of motion
p_{AB^{\prime}}\phi{}_{A}{}^{B^{\prime}}\,=0
,
p^{2}=0
would select
\phi_{AA}\sim{{\bar{k}}}_{A}{{\bar{k}}}_{A}
and two solutions correspond to positive/negative energy waves
\exp\pm i{{\bar{k}}}_{A}x^{AA^{\prime}}{{\bar{k}}}_{A^{\prime}}
. In
\text{AdS}_{4}
, i.e. in the half-flat space, we still have two solution
\phi_{AA}\sim{{\bar{k}}}_{A}{{\bar{k}}}_{A}e^{-kz}
,
\phi_{AA}\sim k_{A}k_{A}e^{+kz}
, but the latter is not regular in the deep interior.

Eliminating
\rho
is harmless, it is a Lagrange multiplier for Gauss’ law
\tau^{2}
. We can impose
\partial_{i}\phi^{i}=0
as a gauge condition. Now, the physical variables are the two transverse vectors, we denote
\varphi_{AA}
and
\pi_{AA}
, that correspond to the transverse components of
\phi_{AA}
and
\Psi_{AA}
. There is a small complication with
\Psi_{AA}
since one needs to eliminate its longitudinal component from
\tau^{2}=0
. We split

\displaystyle\Psi^{i}

\displaystyle=\pi^{i}+{\eth^{i}}\sigma

or

\displaystyle\Psi^{AA}=\pi^{AA}+{k^{AA}}\sigma\,,

(I.4)

to find

\displaystyle D_{i}{\eth^{i}}\sigma+[\varphi_{i},\pi^{i}]

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\sigma=-\left(D_{i}{\eth^{i}}\right)^{-1}[\varphi_{k},\pi^{k}]\,.

(I.5)

The final form of the gauge-fixed Hamiltonian action is

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\int\pi^{AA}(\dot{\varphi}_{AA}+\eth_{AB}\varphi{}_{A}{}^{B}\,+\varphi_{AB}\varphi{}_{A}{}^{B}\,-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\pi_{AA})+S^{\prime}\,,

(I.6)

where the additional (instantaneous) interactions
S^{\prime}
are

\displaystyle S^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\int\frac{\epsilon}{2}\sigma{\eth^{2}}\sigma-\sigma\eth_{AA}(\phi{}^{A}{}_{C}\,\phi^{AC})\,.

(I.7)

One can massage these expressions slightly by introducing the nonabelian magnetic field
B_{AA}=\eth_{AB}\varphi{}_{A}{}^{B}\,+\varphi_{AB}\varphi{}_{A}{}^{B}\,
and noticing that
\eth^{AA}(\eth_{AB}\varphi{}_{A}{}^{B}\,)\equiv 0
:

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\int\pi^{AA}(\dot{\varphi}_{AA}+B_{AA}-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}\pi_{AA})+S^{\prime}\,,

\displaystyle S^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\int\frac{\epsilon}{2}\sigma{\eth^{2}}\sigma-\sigma\eth_{AA}B^{AA}\,.

(I.8)

Note that
\eth_{AA}B^{AA}\neq 0
beyond the free level. Instead, the Bianchi identities imply
D_{AA}B^{AA}\equiv 0
. Now, let us set
\epsilon=0
, i.e. SDYM. In the approximation sufficient to compute the quartic amplitude we find
\sigma=-({\eth^{2}})^{-1}[\varphi_{i},\pi^{i}]
and

\displaystyle S^{\prime}

\displaystyle\approx\mathrm{Tr}\int\frac{\epsilon}{2}[\phi_{i},\pi^{i}]\frac{1}{D^{2}}[\phi_{k},\pi^{k}]+[\phi_{k},\pi^{k}]\frac{\eth^{AA}}{\eth^{2}}(\phi_{AB}\phi{}_{A}{}^{B}\,)+...\,.

(I.9)

Only the last term is relevant for SDYM. These terms contain the usual
J_{0}\tfrac{1}{\Delta}J_{0}
-type interactions. In fact, the complete physical gauge is effectively identical to the Coulomb gauge, i.e. only the physical polarizations propagate, while the
J_{0}
-part of the current (or
\partial_{i}J^{i}
) is accounted for by an instantaneous interaction. Terms of this type are well-known in Yang–Mills theory.

Propagators.

Let us work out the bulk-to-bulk propagators in the complete gauge for the free theory (i.e. the instantaneous interaction will be dropped since there is no
J_{0}
). The inhomogeneous part of the propagator for the Yang--Mills theory is
73
73

73

As different from the axial gauge, this expression is already regular in the bulk (there are no
|z-z^{\prime}|
-terms).

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2k}e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA,BB}\,,

(I.10)

from which the inhomogeneous part of SDYM’s propagator follows

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}(\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA,BB}\operatorname{sign}(z-z^{\prime})-\Pi^{\text{o}}_{AA,BB})\,.

(I.11)

The Yang–Mills propagator with mixed boundary conditions reads

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2k}e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA,BB}+G^{\text{hom},\gamma}_{AA,BB}\,,

where the homogeneous term is the same in all gauges. There are no pure gauge terms of
\exp[-k(z+z^{\prime})]
-type allowed here. The latter leads to the already discussed contribution to SDYM (to be precise to the
\Psi-\Phi
propagator in the Chalmers–Siegel formulation):

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle^{\text{inh}}-e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}e^{-2i\gamma}\frac{{{\bar{k}}}_{A}{{\bar{k}}}_{A}k_{B}k_{B}}{4k^{2}}\,.

(I.12)

The equation of motion for the
\Phi
-leg is satisfied only in the self-dual limit
\gamma\rightarrow-i\infty
. This just shows that there exist homogeneous terms that are valid for the Yang–Mills theory but are forbidden for SDYM, thereby also indicating a lesser freedom to impose boundary conditions in SDYM as compared to the Yang–Mills theory. The final result is that the SDYM propagator is just the inhomogeneous part that is borrowed from flat space!

The boundary-to-bulk propagator for
\gamma\neq 0
is identical to that in the axial gauge (
5.62
)

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,0)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2k}\Big[\Pi^{\gamma}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}-\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}\Big]e^{-kz}\,.

(I.13)

For SDYM we find

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,0)\Phi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle&=-\frac{k_{A}k_{A}\bar{k}_{B}\bar{k}_{B}}{4k^{2}}e^{-kz^{\prime}}\,,&\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB}(k,0)\rangle&=+\frac{\bar{k}_{A}\bar{k}_{A}k_{B}k_{B}}{4k^{2}}e^{-kz}\,.\end{aligned}

On contracting the external legs with the physical polarizations we find

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \epsilon^{-}_{AA}(-k)\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,0)\Phi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle&=-\frac{\bar{k}_{B}\bar{k}_{B}}{2k}e^{-kz^{\prime}}\,,\\
\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB}(k,0)\rangle\epsilon^{+}_{BB}(k)&=+\frac{\bar{k}_{A}\bar{k}_{A}}{2k}e^{-kz}\,.\end{aligned}

(I.14)

Amplitude.

Lastly, let us compute the
4
-point amplitude in SDYM. There are two contributions: the exchange diagram(s) and a contact term. For the
s
-channel exchange the main observation is that the bulk-to-bulk propagator multiplied by the exponents of the boundary-to-bulk propagators integrates to

\displaystyle\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\langle\Psi_{AA}(z,-k)\Phi_{BB}(z^{\prime},k)\rangle e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2E}\left(\frac{1}{E_{\text{L}}}(\Pi^{\text{e}}-\Pi^{\text{o}})-\frac{1}{E_{\text{R}}}(\Pi^{\text{e}}+\Pi^{\text{o}})\right)_{AA,BB}\,.

The quartic vertex can be massaged into
74
74

74

Once the double commutator is expanded, one sees the same color structures as for
s
-channel,
1234
,
1243
,
2134
,
2143
, see Appendix
E
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\,\pi^{BB}\left[\phi_{BB},\frac{k^{AA}}{k^{2}}(\phi_{AC}\phi{}_{A}{}^{C}\,)\right]\,.

(I.15)

Note that in the flat space our
\Phi-\Phi
propagator corresponds to

\displaystyle G_{00}

\displaystyle=G_{0i}=0\,,

\displaystyle G_{ij}=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\left(\delta_{ij}-\frac{k_{i}k_{j}}{k^{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA,BB}\,.

(I.16)

The SDYM propagator is then
75
75

75

One needs to use the vectorial form of
\Pi^{\text{e}}
since
p
is not on-shell,
p^{AA^{\prime}}=k^{AA^{\prime}}+\omega\epsilon^{AA^{\prime}}
.

\displaystyle\langle\Psi^{AA}\Phi^{BB}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{p^{2}}p{}^{A}{}_{A^{\prime}}\,\Pi_{\text{e}}^{AA^{\prime},BB}=\frac{k}{p^{2}}(k^{AB}\epsilon^{AB}+\omega\Pi_{\text{e}}^{AA,BB})\,.

(I.17)

In the flat space, one can easily see that all gauges give the same results. For the quartic amplitude it is not important where interactions are coming from and one can always think of the usual current interaction
J^{\mu}A_{\mu}
. In the Feynman gauge the amplitude is just
J_{\mu}J^{\mu}/p^{2}
. One should remember about the conservation:
p_{0}J^{0}+p_{i}J^{i}=0
and we use
J^{i}=J^{i}_{\perp}+p^{i}J_{\parallel}
. The amplitude is

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{2}}J_{\mu}J^{\mu}=\frac{1}{p^{2}}(J_{\perp}\cdot J_{\perp})+\frac{1}{k^{2}}(J_{0})^{2}\,.

(I.18)

In the Coulomb gauge the last piece is the instantaneous interaction. In the complete physical gauge
(J_{0})^{2}/k^{2}
is directly present in the action. In the temporal gauge the
J_{0}
-contribution is obtained with the help of
J_{\parallel}
via a different
G_{ij}
.

Flat space.

To illustrate explicitly how the complete gauge produces the same flat space amplitude as the other gauges, we first observe that the
\langle\Psi\Phi\rangle
propagator (
I.17
) is transverse on both legs. Hence, this is just the
\langle\pi\phi\rangle
propagator and next we write it as

\displaystyle\langle\pi_{AA}(-p)\phi_{BB^{\prime}}(p)\rangle=\frac{k}{p^{2}}\Big(\epsilon_{AB}p_{AB^{\prime}}+\frac{k_{AA}}{2k^{2}}\big(\omega p_{BB^{\prime}}+p^{2}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}\big)\Big)\,,

(I.19)

where
p^{2}=k^{2}-\omega^{2}
is used. Since the three-point vertex is the same as in SDYM in axial gauge, i.e.
V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC}=2g\,\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\epsilon_{AC}\epsilon_{BC}
, the first term in the propagator provides the amplitude (
F.36
) when plugged into the
s
-channel diagram, while the second term vanishes due to the Ward identity, as in axial gauge. The novelty of the complete gauge is the third term and it is expected to cancel the contribution from the quartic interaction. We denote the last term in (
I.19
) by

\displaystyle\Delta\langle\pi_{AA}(-p)\phi_{BB^{\prime}}(p)\rangle=\frac{k_{AA}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{2k^{2}}\,.

(I.20)

In the exchange channel it is attached to two vertices, which gives

\displaystyle V{}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}{}^{EE}\,\Delta\langle\pi_{EE}(-p)\phi_{FF^{\prime}}(p)\rangle V{}^{FF^{\prime}}{}_{,CC^{\prime},DD}\,=\frac{2}{k^{2}}k_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\epsilon_{CD}\epsilon_{DC^{\prime}}\,.

(I.21)

Meanwhile, the quartic term in the action can be written as

\displaystyle S_{4}=\frac{1}{k^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}\int k^{AA}(\phi_{AB}\phi{}_{A}{}^{B}\,)[\phi_{CC},\pi^{CC}]

(I.22)

and the
s
-channel contribution of the four-point vertex reads

\displaystyle V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime},DD}=-\frac{2}{k^{2}}k_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\epsilon_{CD}\epsilon_{DC^{\prime}}\,.

(I.23)

It follows that the instantaneous interaction is canceled by the correction of the exchange diagram.

AdS – Dirichlet.

In AdS, the correlator is only expected to be gauge-invariant for Dirichlet boundary conditions, so we consider this first before jumping to SDYM. Since our aim is to compute the correlator in complete gauge for SDYM, we will only consider non-topological diagrams. In particular, we only evaluate the left diagram in Figure
11
, i.e. the diagram that has an analogue in SDYM. The reason is that the other diagram in the same figure contains a
\langle\Psi\Psi\rangle
propagator, which is gauge-invariant, and the contact diagram contains no bulk-to-bulk propagator.

In AdS the
\langle\pi\phi\rangle
propagator reads

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\pi_{AA}(-k,z)\phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}&=\frac{1}{2}\Pi^{\text{e}}_{AA,BB^{\prime}}\Big(\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}-e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\Big)+\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\Pi^{\text{o}}_{AA,BB^{\prime}}\Big(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}-e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\Big)\,.\end{aligned}

(I.24)

The difference between the
\langle\pi\phi\rangle
propagator and the
\langle\Psi\Phi\rangle
propagator in Feynman gauge
76
76

76

Strictly speaking, both propagators have different dimensions, since
\phi
has no
z
-component. However, by not automatically symmetrizing indices on
\phi
we implicitly add a
z
-component to
\phi
that is zero.
will be denoted by
\Delta\langle\pi_{AA}(-k,z)\phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle\equiv\langle\pi_{AA}(-k,z)\phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle-\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle
and reads

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta\langle\pi_{AA}(-k,z)\phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle&=\frac{k_{AA}}{4k^{2}}\Big(k_{BB^{\prime}}\big(\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}-e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\big)+\\
&-k\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}\big(e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}-e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\big)\Big)\,,\end{aligned}

(I.25)

which is just the gauge variation between the Feynman gauge and axial gauge propagator (
G.8
) without the terms linear in
z
and
z^{\prime}
. These linear terms were responsible for cancelling a delta function that arise when trying to write the gauge variation as
\nabla\xi
. Thus, due to the absence of these linear terms in the case at hand, it is not possible to write the gauge variation of the propagator as
\nabla\xi
, but instead we find

\displaystyle\Delta\langle\pi_{AA}(-k,z)\phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle=\nabla^{k,z^{\prime}}_{BB^{\prime}}\xi_{AA}+\frac{k_{AA}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{2k^{2}}\delta(z-z^{\prime})\,,

(I.26)

i.e. we need to deal with the delta function by manually cancelling it. Here, the gauge parameter reads

\displaystyle\xi_{AA}=\frac{k_{AA}}{4k^{2}}\Big(\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}-e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\Big)\,,

(I.27)

which again resembles the gauge parameter between Feynman and axial gauge, (
G.12
). In the exchange diagram, integration by parts evaluates the gauge parameter on the boundary
z^{\prime}=0
, where it vanishes. Still, the delta function remains in the gauge variation of the propagator (
I.26
). Evaluating the bulk integrals, its connection to flat space gauge variation (
I.20
) becomes clear:

\displaystyle\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\frac{k_{AA}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{2k^{2}}\delta(z-z^{\prime})e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=\frac{k_{AA}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{2k^{2}E}=\frac{1}{E}\Delta\langle\pi_{AA}(-p)\phi_{BB^{\prime}}(p)\rangle\,.

(I.28)

After contracting the vertices, one obtains

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}V{}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}{}^{EE}\,&\Delta\langle\pi_{EE}(-k,z)\phi_{FF^{\prime}}(k,z)\rangle V{}^{FF^{\prime}}{}_{,CC^{\prime},DD}\,e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}\Big|_{\text{on-shell}}=\\
&=\frac{2}{k^{2}E}k_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\epsilon_{CD}\epsilon_{DC^{\prime}}\,.\end{aligned}

(I.29)

Meanwhile, the vertex for the instantaneous interaction in AdS and flat space are the same, and the bulk integrals yields

\displaystyle\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}V_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime},CC^{\prime},DD}e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=-\frac{2}{k^{2}E}k_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\epsilon_{CD}\epsilon_{DC^{\prime}}

(I.30)

and cancels the gauge variation of the exchange diagram. This implies that the linear terms in the propagator in axial gauge take the role of the instantaneous interaction in complete gauge at the level of diagrams. With this we have established the agreement of the four-point correlator between Feynman gauge, axial gauge and complete gauge for Dirichlet boundary conditions in AdS.

AdS – SDYM.

It is now easy to evaluate the four-point correlator for SDYM in AdS. The gauge variation

\displaystyle\Delta\langle\pi_{AA}(-k,z)\phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{SDYM}}\equiv\langle\pi_{AA}(-k,z)\phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{SDYM}}-\langle\Psi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{SDYM}}

(I.31)

reads
77
77

77

Here we took the Neumann-like propagator. The Dirichlet-like one again leads to gauge independence.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta\langle\pi_{AA}(-k,z)\phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{SDYM}}&=\nabla_{BB^{\prime}}^{k,z^{\prime}}\xi_{AA}+\frac{k_{AA}\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}}{2k^{2}}\delta(z-z^{\prime})\,,\\
\xi_{AA}&=\frac{k_{AA}}{4k^{2}}\Big(\text{sign}(z-z^{\prime})e^{-k|z-z^{\prime}|}+e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\Big)\end{aligned}

(I.32)

and the gauge parameter becomes
\xi_{AA}(z^{\prime}=0)=\frac{k_{AA}}{2k^{2}}e^{-kz}
on the boundary. Like before, the delta function cancels with the instantaneous interaction. Similarly to show how the gauge variation between Feynman gauge and axial gauge in SDYM was computed, c.f. (
6.47
), we use integration by parts to obtain the gauge variation of the correlator,

\displaystyle\Delta\mathcal{W}_{s}^{\text{SDYM}}=-\frac{g^{2}}{4E_{\text{L}}}\frac{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle^{2}\langle\bar{3}4\rangle^{2}}{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\frac{k_{1}-k_{2}}{k^{2}}\,,

(I.33)

which matches the gauge variation between Feynman gauge and axial gauge (
6.48
). We conclude that the four-point SDYM correlator in axial gauge and complete gauge are equal.

Appendix J
Collinear correlators

In this section, we generalize the recent observation that there are nontrivial
(+\dots+-)
amplitudes
[
19
]
to AdS correlators (there are more details than in
[
20
]
).

The three-point function is accompanied by the three-momentum conserving delta function
\delta^{4}(\sum_{i=1}^{3}i_{A}\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}-E\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}})
, which also takes into account the non-conservation in the radial direction. Since the spinors live in a two-dimensional vector space, we can choose basis vectors
r_{A}
and
3_{A}
. The former is an arbitrary reference spinor
r_{A}\neq 3_{A}
. Using this basis, we find

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sum_{i=1}^{3}i_{A}\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}-E\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}&=\frac{r_{A}}{\langle r3\rangle}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\langle i3\rangle\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}+E3_{A^{\prime}}\big)+\frac{3_{A}}{\langle r3\rangle}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\langle ri\rangle\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}-Er_{A^{\prime}}\big)\,,\\
\sum_{i=1}^{3}\langle i3\rangle\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}+E3_{A^{\prime}}&=\bar{1}_{A^{\prime}}\big(\langle 13\rangle-E\frac{\langle\bar{2}3\rangle}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle}\big)+\bar{2}_{A^{\prime}}\big(\langle 23\rangle-E\frac{\langle 3\bar{2}\rangle}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle}\big)\,,\end{aligned}

(J.1)

where in the last line we chose the basis elements
\bar{1}_{A^{\prime}}
and
\bar{2}_{A^{\prime}}
. Using
\delta^{2}(a\lambda_{A}+b\mu_{A})=\frac{1}{|\langle\lambda\mu\rangle|}\delta(a)\delta(b)
, the delta function can be written as

\displaystyle\delta^{4}(\sum_{i=1}^{3}i_{A}\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}-E\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}})=\langle r3\rangle^{2}\frac{\text{sign}\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle}\delta\big(\langle 13\rangle-E\frac{\langle\bar{2}3\rangle}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle}\big)\delta\big(\langle 23\rangle-E\frac{\langle 3\bar{1}\rangle}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle}\big)\delta^{2}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\langle ri\rangle\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}-r_{A^{\prime}}E\big)\,.

(J.2)

Multiplying this measure with the correlator in (
6.10
) gives the AdS extension of the collinear limit of the three-point function. Note that the flat space case sits in the residue at
E=0
.

The four-point flat space amplitude contains the usual four-momentum conserving delta function
\delta^{4}(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{4}i_{A}\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}})
. Another delta function,
\delta(\langle 12\rangle\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle)=\frac{\delta(\langle 12\rangle)}{|\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle|}
arises from applying the collinear limit
p_{1}^{AA^{\prime}}p^{2}_{AA^{\prime}}=0
on the internal propagator,
\frac{1}{p_{12}^{2}+i\epsilon}=\text{PV}\frac{1}{p_{12}^{2}}-i\pi\delta(p_{12}^{2})
. In AdS, the first delta function is replaced by
\delta^{4}(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{4}i_{A}\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}-E\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}})
to account for the non-conservation in the radial direction. The second delta-function can be recovered by recognizing the leading energy pole of the AdS propagator as the flat space propagator after integration,

\displaystyle\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{E}\big(-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}}{E_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}}\big)+\dots\,,

(J.3)

where the ellipsis denotes corrections in higher orders of
E
, which we ignore for now. Note that this relation holds for all boundary conditions in any gauge. With
E_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}=-\langle 12\rangle\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle+EE_{\text{L}}
, we see that the flat space propagator is recovered, where
i\epsilon
can be reinstated.
78
78

78

This will be justified below.
In order to have a smooth limit, we conclude that away from the flat limit, we should write

\displaystyle\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\langle\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{E}\big(-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}}{E_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}-i\epsilon}\big)+\dots\,.

(J.4)

This results in the delta function

\displaystyle\delta(E_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}})=\delta(\langle 12\rangle\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle-EE_{\text{L}})=\delta(\langle 34\rangle\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle-EE_{\text{R}})=\frac{1}{|\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle|}\delta(\langle 34\rangle-E\frac{E_{\text{R}}}{\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle})

(J.5)

in the collinear limit.

Again we choose two linearly independent spinors,
4_{A}
and a reference spinor
r_{A}\not\propto 4_{A}
, to write

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=0}^{4}i_{A}\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{\langle r4\rangle}\Big(-\big(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{4}\langle 4i\rangle\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}-4_{A^{\prime}}E\big)r_{A}+\big(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{4}\langle ri\rangle\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}-r_{A^{\prime}}E\big)4_{A}\Big)\,.

(J.6)

This gives

\displaystyle\delta^{4}(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{4}i_{A}\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}})=\delta^{2}(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{4}\langle ri\rangle\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}-r_{A^{\prime}}E)\delta^{2}(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{4}\langle 4i\rangle\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}-4_{A^{\prime}}E)\langle r4\rangle^{2}\,.

(J.7)

The delta function without reference spinor is further reduced to

\displaystyle\delta^{2}(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{4}\langle 4i\rangle\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}-4_{A^{\prime}}E)=\delta^{2}(\langle 14\rangle\bar{1}_{A^{\prime}}+\langle 24\rangle\bar{2}_{A^{\prime}}+\frac{EE_{\text{R}}}{\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle}\bar{3}_{A^{\prime}}+4_{A^{\prime}}E)\,,

(J.8)

where the constraint in the delta function (
J.5
) was used. We now choose a basis spanned by
\bar{1}_{A^{\prime}}
and
\bar{2}_{A^{\prime}}
to get

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle 14\rangle\bar{1}_{A^{\prime}}+\langle 24\rangle\bar{2}_{A^{\prime}}+\frac{EE_{\text{R}}}{\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle}\bar{3}_{A^{\prime}}+4_{A^{\prime}}E&=\Big(\langle 14\rangle-\frac{E}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle}\big(E_{\text{R}}\frac{\langle\bar{2}\bar{3}\rangle}{\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle}+\langle\bar{2}4\rangle\big)\Big)\bar{1}_{A^{\prime}}+\\
&+\Big(\langle 24\rangle+\frac{E}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle}\big(E_{\text{R}}\frac{\langle\bar{1}\bar{3}\rangle}{\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle}+\langle\bar{1}4\rangle\big)\Big)\bar{2}_{A^{\prime}}\,.\end{aligned}

(J.9)

This allows one to factorize the delta function into

\displaystyle\delta^{2}(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{4}\langle 4i\rangle\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}-4_{A^{\prime}}E)=\delta\Big(\langle 14\rangle-\frac{E}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle}\big(E_{\text{R}}\frac{\langle\bar{2}\bar{3}\rangle}{\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle}+\langle\bar{2}4\rangle\big)\Big)\delta\Big(\langle 24\rangle+\frac{E}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle}\big(E_{\text{R}}\frac{\langle\bar{1}\bar{3}\rangle}{\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle}+\langle\bar{1}4\rangle\Big)\,.

(J.10)

The total measure now becomes

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\delta(E_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}})\delta^{4}(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{4}i_{A}\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}-\epsilon_{AA^{\prime}}E)=\frac{\langle r4\rangle^{2}}{|\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle||\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle|}\delta^{2}(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{4}\langle ri\rangle\bar{i}_{A^{\prime}}-r_{A^{\prime}}E)\times\\
&\times\delta\Big(\langle 14\rangle-\frac{E}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle}\big(E_{\text{R}}\frac{\langle\bar{2}\bar{3}\rangle}{\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle}+\langle\bar{2}4\rangle\big)\Big)\times\delta\Big(\langle 24\rangle+\frac{E}{\langle\bar{1}\bar{2}\rangle}\big(E_{\text{R}}\frac{\langle\bar{1}\bar{3}\rangle}{\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle}+\langle\bar{1}4\rangle\Big)\delta(\langle 34\rangle-E\frac{E_{\text{R}}}{\langle\bar{3}\bar{4}\rangle})\,,\end{aligned}

(J.11)

which recovers the flat space measure in the limit
E\rightarrow 0
.

Propagators.

In the above, we assumed, but not derived, that
i\epsilon
appears in the propagator after integration. Moreover, we did not have any means to know how and if
i\epsilon
enters the energy correction terms of this integral. Here, we attempt to resolve both issues and derive some useful properties of propagators in the process. We start by relating the homogeneous terms in the propagator to the inhomogeneous ones, after which we solidify the statement that
i\epsilon
appears in the inhomogeneous term the way we expected. Using the derived relation between the homogeneous and inhomogeneous terms, we argue that
i\epsilon
enters the homogeneous terms in a similar way.

In Section
5.2.1
, it was discussed that the homogeneous terms in the Feynman gauge propagator for Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions can be obtained from the inhomogeneous solution by applying the method of images. It turned out that there are two operations one can apply to the inhomogeneous term of a propagator,
G_{\text{inh}}^{\mu\nu}(-k,z;k,z^{\prime})
: reflecting the radial coordinate
z^{\prime}
along the boundary, i.e.
z^{\prime}\rightarrow-z^{\prime}
, and reflecting the spacetime index
\nu
along the vector
n_{\nu}
. Let us denote the operators that implement these transformations by
R(z^{\prime})
and
R_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}-2\frac{n_{\mu}n_{\nu}}{n^{2}}
, respectively. Indeed, if
G_{\text{inh}}^{\mu\nu}(-k,z;k,z^{\prime})
solves the equation of motion for some differential operator
D_{\mu\nu}^{-k,z}
that acts on the
z
coordinate and contains three-momentum
-k^{AA^{\prime}}
,

\displaystyle D_{\mu\nu}^{-k,z}G_{\text{inh}}^{\nu\rho}(-k,z;k,z^{\prime})=\delta_{\mu}^{\rho}\delta(z-z^{\prime})\,,

(J.12)

then
G_{\text{hom},1}^{\mu\nu}(-k,z;k,z^{\prime})\equiv R(z^{\prime})G_{\text{inh}}^{\mu\nu}(-k,z;k,z^{\prime})=G_{\text{inh}}^{\mu\nu}(-k,z;k,-z^{\prime})
is a homogeneous solution,

\displaystyle D^{-k,z}_{\mu\nu}G_{\text{hom},1}^{\nu\rho}(-k,z;k,z^{\prime})=0\,.

(J.13)

Also, if
D^{-k,z}_{\mu\nu}R{}^{\nu}{}_{\rho}\,=R{}^{\nu}{}_{\rho}\,D^{-k,z}_{\mu\nu}
, then
G_{\text{hom},2}^{\mu\nu}\equiv R{}^{\nu}{}_{\rho}\,G_{\text{hom},1}^{\mu\rho}
is a homogeneous solution.
We note that this is true for Feynman gauge, because
D^{-k,z}_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}\Box
. In axial gauge, the kinetic operator is
n
-transverse (in both indices),
n^{\nu}D^{-k,z}_{\mu\nu}=0
, and so
R_{\mu\nu}
can be used to derive a new homogeneous solution. Since
G^{\mu\nu}_{\text{hom},1}
itself is also
n
-transverse in axial gauge,
G^{\mu\nu}_{\text{hom},1}=G^{\mu\nu}_{\text{hom},2}
and reflection of the index does not yield a new solution.

Next, in any gauge the propagator for mixed boundary conditions contains
G_{\text{hom},\gamma}^{\mu\nu}
, which consists of the
\Pi_{\text{e}}^{\mu\nu}
and
\Pi_{\text{o}}^{\mu\nu}
. They can be used to construct
G_{\text{hom},3}^{\mu\nu}\equiv\Pi_{\text{o}}{}^{\nu}{}_{\rho}\,G_{\text{hom},1}^{\mu\rho}
and
G_{\text{hom},4}^{\mu\nu}\equiv\Pi_{\text{e}}{}^{\nu}{}_{\rho}\,G_{\text{hom},1}^{\mu\rho}
. If
D^{-k,z}_{\mu\nu}\Pi_{e}^{\nu\rho}=\Pi_{e}^{\nu\rho}D^{-k,z}_{\mu\nu}
, they are homogeneous solutions too. Again, in Feynman gauge this holds because
D_{\mu\nu}^{-k,z}=\eta_{\mu\nu}\Box
. In axial gauge,
D^{-k,z}_{AA,BB}=(\partial_{z}^{2}-k^{2})\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{AB}-\frac{1}{2}k_{AA}k_{BB}
and both
\Pi_{\text{e}}^{\mu\nu}
and
\Pi_{\text{o}}^{\mu\nu}
are
k
-transverse in both indices. Hence,
G_{\text{hom},3}^{\mu\nu}
and
G_{\text{hom},4}^{\mu\nu}
are also homogeneous solutions. Note that in axial gauge
G_{\text{hom},1}^{\mu\nu}\sim\Pi^{\mu\nu}_{\text{e}}
, so
G_{\text{hom},1}^{\mu\nu}=G_{\text{hom},3}^{\mu\nu}
. This can be generalized to any gauge, as the differential operator before gauge fixing reads
D^{-k,z}_{AA^{\prime},BB^{\prime}}=(\partial_{z}^{2}-k^{2})\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{-k,z}_{AA^{\prime}}\nabla^{-k,z}_{BB^{\prime}}
, where
\nabla^{-k,z}_{BB^{\prime}}=-(k_{BB^{\prime}}+\epsilon_{BB^{\prime}}\partial_{z})
. The fact that the operators
\Pi_{\text{e/o}}^{\mu\nu}
are
k
- and
\epsilon
-transverse in both indices allows one to treat
G_{\text{hom},3}^{\mu\nu}
and
G_{\text{hom},4}^{\mu\nu}
as homogeneous solutions in any gauge.

To summarize, acting on an inhomogeneous solution of Yang–Mills with the operator
R(z^{\prime})
, potentially followed by
\Pi_{\text{e/o}}^{\mu\nu}
, gives a homogeneous solution in any gauge, albeit not always a new solution. It is only the operator
R_{\mu\nu}
that does not always yield a solution, so this needs to be checked case-by-case. The homogeneous terms of the descendant propagators, e.g. the
\langle\Psi\Phi\rangle
propagator, can be obtained in the usual way by acting on
\langle\Phi\Phi\rangle
with derivatives. It can also be shown that the same projectors map the inhomogeneous solutions of the Chalmers–Siegel theory to homogeneous ones.

At this stage, one would like to use the argument that applying the above-mentioned projectors, or derivatives to pass to Chalmer-Siegel, does not affect the
i\epsilon
in (
J.4
). However, there are two issues: i) the projector
R(z^{\prime})
might not commute with the integrals and ii) the integral of the inhomogeneous term already produces higher energy corrections, for which it is still unknown where the
i\epsilon
appears. To resolve both problems, we consider the
\langle\Phi\Phi\rangle
propagator suggested in
[
96
]
, which explicitly contains
i\epsilon
. It is the propagator in axial gauge for Dirichlet boundary conditions and it reads

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{D}}^{\text{Axial}}=-\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}dp\frac{\sin(pz)\sin(pz^{\prime})}{k^{2}+p^{2}-i\epsilon}\mathcal{T}_{AA,BB}\,,

(J.14)

with
\mathcal{T}_{AA,BB}=\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}-\frac{k_{AA}k_{BB}}{2p^{2}}
. The integrand has poles at
p=\pm i\sqrt{k^{2}-i\epsilon}
. It is then straightforward to find the inhomogeneous term to be

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{AA}(-k,z)\Phi_{BB}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{inh}}^{\text{Axial}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}dp\frac{e^{ip(z-z^{\prime})}+e^{-ip(z-z^{\prime})}}{k^{2}+p^{2}-i\epsilon}\mathcal{T}_{AA,BB}\,.

(J.15)

From here one obtains the homogeneous terms for mixed boundary conditions by applying the operators discussed above. We also have an ansatz for the propagator containing
i\epsilon
in Feynman gauge:

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{inh}}^{\text{F}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}dp\frac{e^{ip(z-z^{\prime})}+e^{-ip(z-z^{\prime})}}{k^{2}+p^{2}-i\epsilon}\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\,.

(J.16)

Again, the procedure discussed above yields the homogeneous terms one may add.

These integral expressions give the propagators from the main text after evaluating the integrals and setting
\epsilon=0
. Now we keep
\epsilon\ll 1
finite to see how this changes the propagators. It shifts the pole to
p\approx\pm i(k-i\epsilon)
, where
\epsilon
was rescaled. Now, the propagator schematically looks like
79
79

79

More precisely,
\epsilon
also appears in
f
and
g
, but only in such a way that is not relevant for the collinear limit.

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle=f(k;z,z^{\prime})e^{-(k-i\epsilon)|z-z^{\prime}|}+g(k;z,z^{\prime})e^{-(k-i\epsilon)(z+z^{\prime})}+\text{linear terms}

(J.17)

and we observe that the projectors do not affect the
i\epsilon
. The bulk integral becomes

\displaystyle\int e^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})z}\langle\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(-k,z)\Phi_{B,B^{\prime}}(k,z^{\prime})\rangle e^{-(k_{3}+k_{4})z^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{E_{\text{L}}E_{\text{R}}-i\epsilon}\Big(-\frac{\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}}{E}+\dots\Big)\,.

(J.18)

This confirms the assumption from (
J.4
) that
i\epsilon
appears in the denominator of the term containing the energy pole after integration, and it extends this to the higher order terms in
E
. Moreover, derivatives that might hit the propagator in a diagram do not affect the
i\epsilon
either. Thus, the measure (
J.11
) can be used for the entire correlator, not just the energy pole.
